# Standard logic language coverage

The current package uses classical inference rules plus the map's rules for objections, rebuttals and failure to establish a premise. The displayed assessments are Warranted and Unwarranted, relative to the argument as presented. Internal unresolved states do not change the underlying classical truth tables. Separate modal, higher-order, relevant and paraconsistent packages are future work.

## September 2026 audit

### October 27: "the student" is the one "a student" speaks of; "a" inside a kind; names in capitals colored (r27.145)

The user chose to read "the student" as the one "a student" speaks of, over keeping it as one fixed thing: "B".

* "If a student cheats, the student will be caught" now says what "every student that cheats will be caught" says, as "that student" and "he" already did.
* As it does for those, the check asks whether "a student" after "if" means any student, students in general, or one particular student. Read as "every", a step with Bob follows by universal modus ponens.
* A conclusion's "the student" is the one a premise's "a student" speaks of: "Every student that cheats will be caught" and "A student cheats" give "The student will be caught" by universal modus ponens.
* That reading does not apply where two premises could be the one ("A dog barked", "A dog slept"), nor to another noun.
* The Extended Mind step of r27.119 changes with this:
  * "If an AI system is reliably and routinely consulted …, then the AI system functions as a cognitive extension" had been read about one fixed AI system, and the step had been modus ponens.
  * Now the box asks what "an AI system" after "if" means. Answered "any", it reads "Every AI system that is reliably and routinely consulted …", and the step follows by universal modus ponens.
* Inside a kind, "a" and "an" are dropped the way a verb phrase drops them. "Every tool that is used by a carpenter is sharp" and "The saw is a tool that is used by a carpenter" had named the kind differently by one "a", so universal modus ponens did not take the step; now it does.
* Five of the 4,542 sentences in the corpus read differently: four kinds with "a" inside ("a mental state of a subject"), and the AI-system conditional.
* Colors:
  * A name in capitals ("NASA", "an AI system") is now found among the box's words; it had gone uncolored.
  * "A student cheats", with "the student" in the conclusion, is colored as the thing that goes on, with "cheats" meeting the kind.
* The note for a bound "the AI system" keeps its capitals: "every AI system that is consulted".

Tests:
* rules-r27-ordinary-test.js has 82 checks; the four new ones that test the change fail on r27.144.
* colors-r27-inference-test.js has 134 checks; the two new ones fail on r27.144.
* extended-mind-r27-test.js: the r27.119 step asks its question, and once answered it is universal modus ponens, with its colors. The "any" reading now reads "every AI system that is consulted".
* anaphora-r27-test.js: "the student" is the one "a student" speaks of; "the teacher" is not.
* Take-one-out: every edit is caught.
* Not run here: the student corpus, the fallacy corpus, the soundness sweep and the highlighter audit.
* All 91 suites pass, and so does the public build's smoke test.

### October 27: "Switch on", from the step's tag and from the list of steps (r27.144)

The user, of a "Switch on" button in the check's hint, after two mockups (in a window from the tag, or in the list of steps): "both".

* A step that a rule this map has switched off would certify now has a way to switch that rule on. The check already named the switch: "With conversion switched on in the Logic panel (Replacement rules), the premises would give …".

* The button works for contraries and contradictories, for metaphysical necessity, and for any axiom of its system.

* Where the map is read-only there is no button, and the tag opens the list as before.

### October 27: a switched-off rule stays off, even inside "every"; syllogism names, a switch inside Familiar steps (r27.143)

The user, of the loophole: "sure, close the loophole". Of syllogism names: "If the rule has an ordinary name, keep it; if it doesn't, default to the syllogism name; toggle syllogism names ON for those that want to replace the ordinary with syllogism names, default OFF"; and of where the switch goes: "Inside 'Familiar steps'".

* A rule switched off no longer comes back through a rule used inside "every" or "some". With conversion off, "No dogs are cats", so "No cats are dogs", had still been certified as "transposition under ∀". It is not certified now, and the check names the switch. A rule under a quantifier still takes its own steps, as in "Every raven is black and old", so "every raven is black".
* "Syllogism names" is a switch at the bottom of the Familiar steps list, set apart by a line and off by default. With it on, Barbara and Celarent are named "categorical syllogism (Barbara)" and "(Celarent)" in place of "universal syllogism". Darii and Ferio are named "(Darii)" and "(Ferio)" in place of "existential syllogism".
* The other moods already had only their syllogism names. "Poe loves every raven", so "Poe loves every crow", keeps "universal syllogism", and universal modus ponens keeps its name too.
* The switch is saved with the map. It is not counted among the group's rules.

### October 26: "every raven is black and old" is "every raven is black and every raven is old"; technical steps off by default (r27.142)

The user, of the rules textbooks have and arguments rarely use: "Switch those to on-by-default that you think reflect ordinary reasoning. Tell me why for both those you turned on and those you turned off."

* Quantifier distribution over a kind, new; distribution had covered only "everything" and "something":
  * "Every raven is black and old" is "every raven is black and every raven is old".
  * "Some raven is black or white" is "some raven is black or some raven is white".
  * "No raven is black or white" is "no raven is black and no raven is white"; "nothing is black or white" works the same way.
* One way only:
  * "Some ravens are black and old" gives "some ravens are black and some ravens are old".
  * "Every raven is black or every raven is white" gives "every raven is black or white".
* Not certified: splitting "every raven is black or white", joining two "some"s, or splitting "no raven is black and old".
* A new group, Technical steps, off by default, holds six rules that had been on in Familiar steps, Order and grouping, and Quantifier equivalences: absorption, predicate congruence, Boolean absorption, the ⊥ laws, the rules of passage, and the vacuous quantifier anywhere. A step that needs one of them is "not recognized", and the check names the switch.
* Kept on, because ordinary reasoning uses them: resolution, change of bound variable, and quantifier distribution ("something is black or white", so "something is black or something is white").
* A map that used one of the six shows those steps as not recognized until the switch is turned on.

Tests:
* rules-r27-ordinary-test.js has 65 checks; the five new ones fail on r27.141.
* logic-r27-basis-test.js counts fifteen groups, ten lists of switches and twenty-two familiar rules. It checks that absorption, Boolean absorption, the ⊥ laws, predicate congruence and the vacuous ∃ are off by default, and that each still works when switched on.
* logic-r27-english-test.js, logic-r27-fol-test.js, logic-r27-stress-test.js and logical-theorem-r27-test.js switch the technical steps on where they test them. The proof map in logical-theorem uses absorption for its middle step, so that map switches absorption on.

### October 26: dropping a "that" clause -- "Bob is glad that Mary left", so "Bob is glad"; "shorter" has two senses; colors for a subject said once and for dropped words (r27.141)

The user, of "Bob is glad that Mary left, so Bob is glad": "Shouldn't it certify? The 'that' part of the sentence just tells us what it is that is making Bob glad." And of "The rope is shorter than the cable" giving "The cable is taller than the rope": "Perhaps not, as it should give 'The cable is longer than the rope'."

* Dropping a "that" clause, a rule of word meaning in the modifiers group: "Bob is glad that Mary left" gives "Bob is glad", in any tense ("was glad"), and after "might be". The same holds for pleased, surprised, upset, grateful, relieved, worried, ashamed, angry and the like.
* The rule leaves out "happy", "content", "proud", "confident", "sorry", "afraid", "aware", "conscious", "satisfied", "anxious" and "positive", since each can say more on its own. The check says what: "On its own, ‘proud’ can mean “vain”, which ‘proud that …’ need not." "Bob is sorry that you feel that way" is no apology.
* Not under "not": Bob may not be glad that Mary left and still be glad.
* "Shorter", "lighter" and "thinner" each have two senses: less tall or less long, less heavy or less dark, less thick or less fat. A premise does not say which, so they give no converse. The check says so and shows how to say the sense meant: "The rope is shorter in length than the cable" gives "The cable is longer than the rope".
* A converse to these words still follows, because the premise's word gives the sense: "Bob is taller than Al", so "Al is shorter than Bob".
* New converse pairs:
  * "shorter in height than" and "taller than"; "shorter in length than" and "longer than"
  * "lighter in weight than" and "heavier than"; "lighter in color than" and "darker than"
  * "darker than" and "lighter than"; "fatter than" and "thinner than"
* "Less tall than" is the converse of "taller than", for any word: "less big" and "bigger", "less heavy" and "heavier", "less good" and "better".
* The colors for "Bob, who is rich, can pay", with "Bob can pay": "Bob" now goes on with "can pay". Since r27.139 the box reads "Bob is rich, and Bob can pay", and "Bob" had been dimmed along with "who is rich".
* A step that drops words lights the words kept and dims the words dropped. In "Bob ran quickly", "Bob ran" is lit and "quickly" dimmed; r27.137 had dimmed "ran quickly" whole. In "Bob is glad that Mary left", "that Mary left" is dimmed.

### October 26: existential introduction from an object of a kind -- "Bob loves a raven", so "Bob loves something" (r27.140)

* "Bob loves a raven" gives "Bob loves something" (a raven is something) and "Something is a raven"; "Bob hit some poodle", "Bob hit something". "Bob loves Mary", so "Bob loves something", was there; an object of a kind was not.
* Not "Bob loves someone": a raven need not be a person. Not "every raven", of which there may be none; not another kind; not "almost hit"; not after a verb like "fears", whose object may be no real raven. "Does not love a raven" and "taller than a poodle" still ask which they mean, as before.

### October 26: a clause or a phrase set off by commas -- "Bob, who is rich, can pay" (r27.139)

The user's rule: commas settle grouping. A "who" or "which" clause, or a phrase naming what the thing is, set off by commas after a name or "the ...", says something of it, a claim of its own. These had been read as one claim, with the note "a comma inside the subject sets off a phrase".

* "Bob, who is rich, can pay" reads "Bob is rich, and Bob can pay"; "The dog, which barked, left" reads "The dog barked, and the dog left"; "Mark Twain, who wrote Huckleberry Finn, was born in Missouri" gives "Mark Twain wrote Huckleberry Finn".
* "Bob, a doctor, can pay" reads "Bob is a doctor, and Bob can pay"; "Paris, the capital of France, is big", "Physicalism, the view that everything is physical, is popular" the same way.
* Not "Bob, who I like, can pay", whose clause has a subject of its own; not a list ("Bob, the dog and the cat left"); not "Bob, however, can pay"; not inside an "if". A verb must follow the second comma, one the reader knows as a verb.
* The syntax tree shows the box as read: "[the match] was scheduled for noon, and the match is canceled" for "The match, which was scheduled for noon, is canceled".

Tests. syntax-r27-test.js (111: three new; the bracket of "The match, which was scheduled for noon, is canceled" is now the two claims). anaphora-r27-test.js: three checks had kept "Bob, the teacher,", "the dog, a terrier," and "Poe, which is a raven," read as one claim, guarding that the commas between adjectives (r27.87) and a "which" for what a clause says did not reach them; each is now two claims, and the "which" is still no clause's. Take-one-out: the one edit is caught. Of the 4,542 sentences from the suites, six read differently, each a clause or phrase set off by commas, now two claims. Not run here: the student corpus, the fallacy corpus, the soundness sweep and the highlighter audit. All 91 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 26: overlapping rules made separate (r27.138)

The user, of rules that take each other's steps when one is switched off: "How about both? Those that should be separate rules with separate steps are made so, and those that necessarily overlap are put under one switch." These are the first kind: a rule no longer takes a step that a narrower rule takes.

* The constructive dilemma, not where both consequents are one claim: that is proof by cases. Switched off, proof by cases had handed its steps to the dilemma.
* Substitution of equivalents, only inside a larger claim: with "A if and only if B", "A" itself or "not A" is biconditional elimination's, which had handed them over when switched off.
* Double-negation replacement, not of a whole claim: "it is not the case that it is not the case that Bob left", so "Bob left", is double-negation elimination's.
* Contraposition, not of a conversion: "No dogs are cats", so "No cats are dogs", is conversion's, which had passed to contraposition when switched off.
* With these switched off, the check now names the switch (r27.135). Each rule still takes its own steps.
* Not yet: those that overlap of necessity -- identity transitivity, which is substitution into "b = c"; the syllogisms named both by figure ("universal syllogism") and by mood ("Barbara"); a rule under a quantifier that takes a step a rule switched off would ("transposition under ∀" for conversion). Their switches, and an "Advanced" group, are a change to the Logic panel, put to the user first.

Tests. rules-r27-ordinary-test.js (46: four new, three failing on r27.137). Take-one-out: each of the 4 edits is caught. No reading changes. Not run here: the student corpus, the fallacy corpus, the soundness sweep and the highlighter audit. All 91 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 26: dropping a modifier (r27.137)

The user, of "Bob ran quickly to the store, so Bob ran to the store": "Where would this be? In the semantics option?" A group of its own in the Logic panel, "Word meaning: modifiers", after relations, on by default.

* A word of manner, time or degree left out: "Bob ran quickly" gives "Bob ran"; "Bob carefully opened the door", "Bob opened the door"; "Bob ran quickly to the store", "Bob ran to the store"; "Bob left yesterday", "Bob left"; "Bob is very tall", "Bob is tall". One or more at once ("Bob ran very quickly", "Bob ran").
* Not a word that says it may not be so: "almost", "nearly", "allegedly", "apparently", "supposedly", "possibly", "probably", "hardly", "rarely", "never" and the like. "Bob almost won" does not give "Bob won".
* Not under a denial: "Bob did not run quickly" does not give "Bob did not run".
* Not after a verb whose manner word completes it: "Bob behaved badly" does not give "Bob behaved", which says he behaved well; likewise "treat", "fare", "do", "go", "act", "word", "phrase".
* Not a noun in -ly ("Bob visited family"), nor a phrase ("Bob ran to the store" does not give "Bob ran": "to the store" may be what "ran" needs, as "on" is in "relies on"). A non-subsective adjective was already asked: "a former president need not be a president".

Tests. rules-r27-ordinary-test.js (42: four new). logic-r27-deductive-test.js: dropping a modifier among the optional rules. logic-r27-basis-test.js: fourteen groups. Take-one-out: each of the 4 edits is caught. No reading changes. Not run here: the student corpus, the fallacy corpus, the soundness sweep and the highlighter audit. All 91 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 26: relations -- converses, symmetric, transitive, asymmetric (r27.136)

The user agreed to "a 'Word meaning: relations' group, built like the opposites one". It is a group of its own in the Logic panel, after "knows that", on by default, its steps named by their relation.

* Converses, 23 pairs: "Bob is taller than Al" gives "Al is shorter than Bob", and back; denied too ("Bob is not taller than Al", "Al is not shorter than Bob"). Older and younger, bigger or larger and smaller, heavier and lighter, faster and slower, richer and poorer, stronger and weaker, higher and lower, earlier and later, greater and less, better and worse, wider and narrower, thicker and thinner, deeper and shallower, louder and quieter, longer and shorter; "above" and "below", "before" and "after", "north of" and "south of", "east of" and "west of", "parent of" and "child of", "ancestor of" and "descendant of". "More famous than" and "less famous than", for any adjective. As with contraries, a word of two senses is read in the sense the other word gives it: "shorter" than the taller, or than the longer.
* Symmetric: "married to", "next to", "near", "beside", "adjacent to", "similar to", "equal to", "different from", "a sibling of", "a cousin of", "a spouse of", "a neighbor of"; the verbs "met" and "married". "Bob is married to Al" gives "Al is married to Bob".
* Transitive: every comparative ("taller than", "smarter than", "more famous than"), "above", "before", "north of", "an ancestor of", "part of", "equal to". "Bob is taller than Al" and "Al is taller than Cy" give "Bob is taller than Cy".
* Asymmetric: every comparative and every order, "parent of" and "child of". "Bob is taller than Al" gives "Al is not taller than Bob".
* Not "taller" both ways; not "similar to" or "parent of" passed along; not of "a man", which is no one thing; not "loves", which the list does not have; not "other than".

Tests. rules-r27-ordinary-test.js (38: eight new, for every pair both ways and denied, each kind, what does not follow, the panel and the switch). logic-r27-deductive-test.js: relations among the optional rules. logic-r27-basis-test.js: thirteen groups. Take-one-out: each of the 5 edits is caught. No reading changes. Not run here: the student corpus, the fallacy corpus, the soundness sweep and the highlighter audit. All 91 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 26: the universal syllogism after a verb; "there are dogs"; every invalid syllogism named; a rule switched off says so (r27.135)

* The universal syllogism after a verb: "Poe loves every raven" and "Every crow is a raven" give "Poe loves every crow"; "Poe loves no raven" (or "does not love any raven") gives "Poe loves no crow". The existential one ("hit some poodle", so "hit some dog") was there; this is its twin, narrowing the object where that one widens it. Not after a verb like "fears", whose object may be no real raven, nor to a wider kind, nor from "every" to "no".
* "There are dogs" says what "some dogs exist" says: with "All dogs are mammals", it gives "Some dogs are mammals". Without it, that step is still the existential fallacy.
* Every categorical syllogism that is not valid is named by the first rule of the syllogism it breaks, in the tag and in the reason: the middle term not said of all of its kind in either premise (undistributed middle), a term said of all of its kind in the conclusion and not in its premise (illicit major, illicit minor), two denials (exclusive premises), an affirmative conclusion from a negative premise, a negative conclusion from affirmative premises, "some" from two "all"s (the existential fallacy). Only undistributed middle, the existential fallacy and the converse of "all" had been named; the rest were "? not recognized". "Some ravens are not black", so "Some black things are not ravens", is illicit conversion, as for "all".
* A rule a map has switched off: the check says which switch would give the step (the user: "for all inferences that are not validated by the base logic, ... the option to turn on the optional rule that would validate the inference"). "With modus tollens switched on in the Logic panel (Familiar steps), the premises would give “It does not rain”." Not for the catch-alls or the extra rule instances, which give whatever truth tables do, nor for order and grouping, which has its own reason ("the two differ only in the order or grouping of their parts"). Contraries, modal steps and "knows that" had said so already; "knows that" now says it as every rule does. A button that switches the rule on is a visual change, so it waits for a mockup.

Tests. rules-r27-ordinary-test.js (30: ten new, eight failing on r27.134; the expository syllogism switched off now names its switch, where it had said "takes two steps"). Take-one-out: 5 of the 6 edits are caught; the sixth removes a message the new hint leaves unreachable. No reading changes. Not run here: the student corpus, the fallacy corpus, the soundness sweep and the highlighter audit. All 91 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 26: rules of ordinary reasoning -- factive verbs, the expository syllogism, "either way", existential introduction in either order; "glad that" read as a claim after it (r27.134)

The user asked that the app have "all the basic logical rules that we use in ordinary reasoning", and agreed to add the missing ones: "if expository syllogism is a known rule, we can add it to optional rules"; of factives, "Maybe the T axiom could be on by default"; of "either way", "we can add it as an optional rule".

* Factive verbs, a group of its own in the Logic panel, "Word meaning: “knows that”", on by default. "Bob knows that Mary left" gives "Mary left", whatever is known: "all men are mortal", "if it rains, the game is off", a denial, a compound. Likewise "realizes", "recognizes", "learned", "remembers", "understands", "noticed", "sees", "discovered", "regrets", "forgot", "proved", "showed", "established", "is aware that", "is glad that", "is sorry that", and "it is known that". Not "believes", "thinks", "says", "hopes", "is sure that": what is believed can be false. Not a denial: "Bob does not know that Mary left" gives nothing. Not a "that" inside the subject: "The man who knows that Mary left is sad". A step of two had said the checker had no rule for "know"; it now names the box to add. Switched off, the check says what would give the step.
* A soundness fix. "Bob is glad that Mary left" had been read as "Bob is glad, and Mary left Bob", a relative clause after "glad", so it gave "Mary left Bob" by conjunction elimination. "Glad", "sorry", "aware", "proud", "surprised" and the like now take a claim after "that", as "sure" and "certain" did. "A dog barked, and Bob is glad that dog left" is read as "... Bob knows that dog left" is: it had been one unread claim. "Bob is glad that Mary left" no longer gives "Bob is glad", which came only from the misreading; "Bob is sure that Mary left" never gave "Bob is sure".
* The expository syllogism, a familiar step beside universal modus tollens: "Bob is a student" and "Bob passed" give "Some student passed" in one step, where it had taken conjunction introduction and then existential introduction. Either order; denied ("Some student did not pass"); with "someone" ("Bob is a person", "Bob loves Mary", so "Someone loves Mary").
* "Either way", a familiar step beside the destructive dilemma: "If it rains, then the game is off" and "If it does not rain, then the game is off" give "The game is off". With "It rains or it does not rain" said too, proof by cases gave it already.
* Existential introduction in either order: "Socrates is wise and Socrates is a man" and "Poe is a black raven" give "Some man is wise" and "Some raven is black". English cannot say "a raven black", and conjunction does not care about order.

Tests. rules-r27-ordinary-test.js (new, 20 checks; 10 fail on r27.133, the others guard what must not follow). logic-r27-deductive-test.js: the two new familiar steps and factive verbs among the optional rules. logic-r27-basis-test.js: twelve groups; Familiar steps twenty-four rules. hints-r27-test.js: "Poe is a raven" and "Poe is black" give "Some raven is black" in one step now, so the two-step hint is checked with "Something is a black raven"; "we know that" and "have shown" give what is known or shown, and a step of two names the box to add (where it had said the checker had no rule). Take-one-out: each of the 15 edits is caught. Of the 4,542 sentences from the suites, none reads differently. Not run here: the student corpus, the fallacy corpus, the soundness sweep and the highlighter audit. All 91 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 26: the highlighter -- a kind in its parts; "only"; words two clauses share; a kind going on denied (r27.133)

* "Whoever can vote is a citizen; Bob is a person; Bob can vote ∴ Bob is a citizen" had dimmed "Whoever can vote", "is a person" and "can vote", as if the step used none of them: the kind was matched whole, and no premise says "a person who can vote". A kind with parts that meets nothing whole is now taken in its parts: "whoever" meets "is a person" and "can vote" meets "can vote", each in its own color. Likewise "anyone who can vote", and "every raven that sings" beside "Poe is a raven" and "Poe sings".
* "Poe is a raven that sings", beside "Every raven that sings is black", had been dimmed. It now meets the kind whole.
* "Only citizens can vote", "None but citizens can vote" and "If something is a raven, then it is black" had gone uncolored in certified steps: their words are not in the order of their reading ("all who can vote are citizens"). Each part is now found by its own words: "can vote" meets, "citizens" goes on.
* Words two clauses share. "Bob and Mary left" is read as "Bob left" and "Mary left". The first had been found across all four words, so "Mary left" went unfound: with "∴ Mary left" the whole box was dimmed, and with "∴ Bob left" all of it went on. Each part is now found for itself, past the words another part has: for "Mary left", "Mary left" goes on and "Bob" is dimmed. A word two parts share takes the color of the part that goes on, else of the part that meets. Words between them that belong to neither are left plain: the "and", the "not both" of "Bob and Al did not both leave". Likewise "Bob sang and danced", "Bob is tall, rich and happy", "Bob, Al and Cy left", "Either Bob or Al is guilty" and "Neither Bob nor Al left".
* Universal modus tollens: "Every raven is black; Poe is not black ∴ Poe is not a raven" had left "Every raven" plain, though the conclusion denies it, while modus tollens underlines its "if" part. "Every raven" now goes on denied, with the red dashes, as "every raven" already did in "Poe loves every raven; Poe does not love Fido ∴ Fido is not a raven". So does "can vote" in "Only citizens can vote; Bob is not a citizen ∴ Bob cannot vote".
* Not changed: "No ravens are white; Poe is a raven ∴ Poe is not white" still underlines "are white" as going on denied, as agreed in r27.75. That is put to the user again, since the conclusion agrees with what the premise says of ravens.

### October 26: the weather's "it" after "if", "unless", "when"; "it is Tuesday"; one sheep may be "it" (r27.132)

The user's rule (r27.125): "it is cold" after a thing is asked, the weather among its readings, written with the "it" clause first.

* "The game is cancelled unless it is sunny" had offered "the game is sunny" and "the fact that the game is cancelled is sunny", neither the one meant. The weather's reading had been written only after "and", or for "if A, then it is cold". Now its clause goes first after "if", "unless", "when" and the like: "Unless it is sunny, the game is cancelled", "If it is cold, the game is cancelled", "When it is sunny, the game is on".
* Read back, "Unless it is sunny, the game is cancelled" had read "it" as the game: the check that keeps a weather's "it" had its own, shorter list of the weather's words, without "sunny". The two lists are one now.
* A day of the week is the time's: "The street is wet and it is Tuesday" asks whether "it" is the street or the time.
* "The sheep is hungry and it is cold" had asked nothing: "the sheep" had been many only, so "it" could be nothing in the box but the weather. "The sheep is", one sheep, may now be "it", and so it is asked, as with "the dog". That changed an old check, "The sheep is hungry and it is noisy" read as the sheep: "noisy" is a word of the surroundings, and the sentence now asks. The check of a pronoun's number now uses "white", and a new one says the "noisy" sentence asks.

### October 26: "both" after a subject, "not both", and "not only ... but also" (r27.131)

The user: "parse language into its syntactical components correctly".

* "Bob and Mary both left" had kept "both" in what was said ("both left"), so "Bob left" did not follow. "Both" or "each" after the subject now says it of each, as "both Bob and Mary" does; so too "Bob and Mary have both left".
* "Bob and Al did not both leave" had been read as neither leaving, with a verb "bothed leave". "Not" before "both" now denies that both did, so with "Bob left" it gives "Al did not leave" (conjunctive syllogism). "Bob did not leave or Al did not leave" gives it by De Morgan's laws, and it gives neither denial alone. "Bob and Al both did not leave", "not" after "both", denies it of each.
* "Bob is not only rich but also happy" had asked whether Bob is "not both only rich and happy"; "Bob is not only rich, but he is also happy" had been read as a denial. "Not only ... but" now says both: "Bob is rich", "Bob is happy". "Not only is Bob rich, but ..." was read so already; "Bob is not only rich", with no "but", is as before.
* Found beside it: "Bob, Al and Cy each left" had been made "both Bob, Al and Cy left", and read as Bob's "al". A list of three or more takes no "both".

### October 26: "they" of one person, bound as "he" is; "that student" for a condition's "a student" (r27.130)

The user writes "they" of one person, and the students do. Of the fixes proposed: "I agree with you on everything."

* "If anyone cheats, they will be caught" had been "✗ no referent", and "If someone cheats, they will be caught" was read as one claim. With "he", both had been read as "everyone who cheats will be caught". Now "they" is bound as "he" is, said in the singular: "they lose" is "loses", "they are punished" is "is punished", "they will be caught" as it is.
* A denied consequent is read as "no one who ...": "If someone cheats, they do not win" says what "no one who cheats wins" says. It had been read as one claim, with "he" too, since "everyone who cheats does not win" asks which "not" is meant.
* "If a student cheats, that student will be caught" -- "that student", "this student" -- is the student of the condition, as "he" would be (every student that cheats will be caught). It had been read as one claim.
* Not "the student": "If an AI system is consulted, then the AI system is reliable" keeps its words, the same in each box, for the modus ponens r27.119 gave it. Binding it so had broken that step of the Extended Mind map, which its suite caught.

### October 26: the opposites list made sound -- a word of two senses, verbs of change, an outcome of nothing, a pair of persons only (r27.129)

The user asked for "a complete dictionary of English contraries and contradictories". Of the fixes proposed: "I agree with you on everything."

* Before growing the list, it had to stop certifying steps that do not follow. Each of these had been certified:
  - "Bob is dangerous", so "Bob is not safe" (a dangerous man in his bunker is safe);
  - "The stock rose today", so "The stock did not fall today" (it can do both before lunch);
  - "Prices increased this year", so "Prices did not decrease this year";
  - "Bob won", so "Bob did not lose" (two games);
  - "The rock is not guilty", so "The rock is innocent".
* Now:
  - "dangerous" and "safe" are no pair: "safe" may only say that a thing is not in danger.
  - "Rise" and "fall", "increase" and "decrease" are gone: one thing does both in a day.
  - An outcome is opposed only with what it is of: "Mary lost the race" gives "Mary did not win the race"; "Bob won" gives nothing.
  - "Guilty" and "innocent" deny each other of anything ("the man was guilty", so "not innocent"). But from "not guilty" to "innocent" takes a premise that it is a person: "The man was not guilty" and "The man is a person" give "The man was innocent". Without that premise, the hint says so: "‘guilty’ and ‘innocent’ are contradictories only of a person: “Socrates was not guilty” gives “Socrates was innocent” once it is shown that ‘Socrates is a person’." Where the step needs no such premise, a premise that gives it is not needed, as for any step.
* 75 pairs, 8 of them contradictories (78 before).

Tests. opposites-r27-test.js (29: a word of two senses, verbs of change, an outcome, a sort; the count and the "each one's denial gives the other" check, now with the sort premise where a pair has one). ### October 26: a clause after "after", "before", "as" belongs to the verb, not the subject (r27.128)

The user: "parse language into its syntactical components correctly". Of the fixes proposed: "I agree with you on everything."

* "Mary left after Bob arrived" had been read "[Mary left after Bob] arrived". A participle's phrase after its noun ("swans seen so far") ran on past "after", so a thing called "Mary left after Bob" arrived. With "Everything that arrived was greeted", it was certified to give "Mary left after Bob was greeted", which does not follow: Bob arrives at one, Mary leaves at two, Bob is greeted at three. Likewise "Mary acted as Bob would act", so "Something would act".
* Now the words settle it where they can, past "after", "before", "since", "until", "till", "as" and "like":
  - A form that is only a participle says which thing is meant ("the man seen after the party | left early", "the book written before the war | sold well").
  - A name says no such thing ("Mary | left after Bob arrived").
  - Nor does a verb that takes no object ("the dog | died before the vet arrived").
  - A verb that must have an object, with none after it, is not the verb there ("the letters sent after the election | arrived late").
  - A word that opens the box alone, and that no list knows as a noun, is a name by its place.
* Where both groupings are English -- "The dog left after Bob arrived", "Every dog left after Bob arrived" -- it is asked where a step turns on it, as a generic is: '"left after Bob" may say which dog is meant, or what the dog did'. Its readings are "the dog that was left after Bob arrived" and "the dog left, after Bob arrived". Until it is answered, the box is read as its first reading says, word for word. So a step follows only where it follows on both ("if the dog left after Bob arrived, then Al cried").

Tests. syntax-r27-test.js (105: a clause after "after", "before", "as"). Take-one-out: each of the 8 edits is caught. Of 4,542 sentences gathered from the suites, the 14 this is about read differently, and the rest as before. Not run here: the student corpus, the fallacy corpus, the soundness sweep and the highlighter audit (dev-tools/ is not in this checkout). All 90 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 24: a clause by the syntax tree, where the word lists do not know its verb (r27.127)

"Bob likes the cat and the dog and the dog likes Bob" was read as one claim. I had said "the dog likes" was taken for a noun; the user: "shouldn't the part of speech of each of these phrases be evaluated by the internal syntax tree? There's no way 'the dog likes' should be taken as a noun if the internal syntax tree works." It was not the tree's reading -- it reads "likes" there as the verb -- but the "and" splitter's.

* Asked whether a clause begins at "the dog", the tree had said it could not tell: "the dog and the dog likes" is no subject and its verb. The splitter then guessed, split after "the cat", found "the dog and the dog likes Bob" no clause, and glued the whole back into one. A verb in -s, like "is" (r27.125), takes no subject joined by "and": no clause begins there, and the sentence is "Bob likes the cat and the dog" and "the dog likes Bob".
* With verbs no word list knows -- "Bob feeds the cat and the dog and the dog adores Bob" -- the tree could only call "feeds" and "adores" words that may be verbs, and the splitter fell back on an older test, which found no clause in "Bob feeds the cat and the dog". The tree now takes a clause by its whole shape: a subject, a word that can be its verb, and an object that ends the clause. "The dog adores Bob" is a clause whatever the lists know; "the dog adores" alone may still be one noun phrase.
* Where the tree cannot tell a singular from a plural ("the dog adore Bob" passes, "the dog" being unmarked), "Bob likes Rex and the cat and the dog adore Bob" is asked, as a sentence with two groupings is.

Tests. syntax-r27-test.js (98: verbs in -s and verbs no list knows). Take-one-out: each edit is caught. The corpora read as before; none of the 118 fallacies is certified; the soundness sweep finds no false validity. All 90 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 24: a comma before "and" divides clauses (r27.126)

The user: "Independent clauses in the same sentence should be divided by commas. And we can use 'both... and' and 'either... or' for disambiguation, no?" Of "Bob met Al and Cy, and Al met Dee": "I read: 'Bob met Al and Cy. Al met Dee.'"

* So it is now read. It had been "Bob met Al" and "Cy, and Al met Dee", "Cy, and Al" taken for one subject -- so "Al met Dee" did not follow from it. Two that a comma before "and" sets apart are no one subject; a list of three ("Al, Cy, and Dee") keeps its comma.
* With no comma -- "Bob met Al and Cy and Al met Dee" -- the "and" before "Cy" may join two clauses ("Cy and Al met Dee") or two names (whom Bob met), and the checker asks: "Bob met Al, and Cy and Al met Dee" or "Bob met Al and Cy, and Al met Dee", each reading with the comma that says which. Only where both are English: not "Mary left and Bob and Ann cried" (before "Bob", nothing for it to join), nor "Bob likes Al and Cy and Al likes Dee" ("Cy and Al likes" is no English).
* "Both ... and" and "either ... or" already group what they hold, and readings use them where a comma cannot say it ("both A and B, or C"). Here the comma says it; "both" would also have said "each" of a subject ("both Cy and Al met Dee"), which is asked on its own.

Tests. syntax-r27-test.js (95: a comma before "and"). Take-one-out: each edit is caught. The corpora read as before; none of the 118 fallacies is certified; the soundness sweep finds no false validity. All 90 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 24: "it is cold" after a thing asked, the weather among its readings; how a step's boxes were read, on a step that follows too; joined words before "is" (r27.125)

Of two ways to read "The street is wet and it is cold" -- "it" as the weather by default, with a note, or a question -- the user first chose the default. Built, it read "If Poe is a stove, then it is hot" as the weather too, which an old test of the user's rule (competing readings block a certification) caught; and a question could after all be answered in the box's own words. Shown both, the user chose the question.

* "It is cold", "it is hot", "it is late", "it is dark" after a thing now ask whether "it" is that thing or the weather (or the time): "“it” may be “the street” or the weather". The weather's reading puts the "it" clause first -- "It is cold and the street is wet", "It is hot if Poe is a stove", "If it is cold and the street is wet, then Bob stays in" -- where nothing before "it" is a thing, and so it reads as the weather, in the box's own words. It had offered the street, or "the fact that the street is wet is cold", which no one means; that reading is gone where the weather's is offered. Where the clause cannot be moved in front ("Bob thinks that the street is wet and it is cold"), it asks as before. "It is spicy" is no weather. Read so, a conjunction's order changes: a step that needs it in the old order takes commutation too. And "In the old house, it is cold" no longer says that "it" has nothing to refer to.
* A step that follows now says, in its tag's tip and in the list of steps, how its boxes were read where their words left it open: "his" as "Otto", words cut short ("Otto's notebook can't" as "Otto's notebook can not automatically influence reasoning"), a pronoun bound by "every" or "someone", "each other" -- so that a wrong guess shows on a step that follows, too. Boxes read the same way are named together, the reading once. What the reader does not take apart ("while" read as one claim) is not said there: the step follows without it. It had been said only where a step did not follow.
* Found beside it: "Bob met Al and Cy, and Al is tall" had been read as "Bob met Al" and "Cy, and Al is tall", the second unread -- "Cy and Al" taken for the subject of "is" -- so "Al is tall" did not follow from it. Words joined by "and" take "are", not "is": the clause begins at "Al", and Al and Cy are whom Bob met. So too before "was", "has", "does", and "Bob likes the cat and the dog, and the dog is tall". Only where "and" joins them: "Socrates is wise" and "the news is good", which end in -s, keep their "is". Where the verb does not show its number ("... and Al met Dee"), the reading is as before.

Tests. anaphora-r27-test.js (107: the weather among the readings, the notes on a step that follows); syntax-r27-test.js (91: words joined by "and" before "is"). Take-one-out: each edit is caught. The corpora read as before; none of the 118 fallacies is certified; the soundness sweep finds no false validity. All 90 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 24: "everything that is not black is not a raven", and the rules after a verb model-checked (r27.124)

The user: "If there's anything else in the mapper that needs to be added/deleted/edited, please do." And, of verbs like "dreams of": "I don't want to assume a specific ontology" -- a verb is read as a relation, which takes no stand on what there is.

* "Everything that is not black is not a raven" asks, rightly, whether it denies it of each one or denies that it holds of all ("all that glitters is not gold"). Its readings had taken out the wrong "not" -- the one in the kind, "that is not black" -- so that the first read "nothing that is black is not a raven", another claim, and neither could be written into the box. They are now "nothing that is not black is a raven" and "not everything that is not black is a raven"; "whatever ... not" has its "nothing that ...", and "it is not the case that whatever ...". Chosen, the first gives "All ravens are black", so the box, by contraposition.
* A reading keeps the box's capitals for its names only: "Not everything ...", not "Not Everything ...".
* The model checker that judges every certified first-order step now judges the rules r27.115-r27.120 added, the verb read as a relation: the kinds after a verb ("Poe loves some raven", "every raven is a crow", so "Poe loves some crow"; 300 steps with their look-alikes, 89 certified, none invalid), and "anything that is a part of a thing is smaller than that thing" with its case, with "a person ... that person" only once the step shows the one it names to be a person (42 certified, none invalid).

### October 23: every premise of a certified step placed (r27.123)

The user: "is the proposition highlighter working for all types of propositions? I want to make sure that both are rigorous" -- and, of the fixes proposed, "Just the highlighter."

An audit: in a certified step every premise takes part, so the painter should find how each one does. Where it found none of a premise's clauses -- in the conclusion, or in another premise -- it had painted the premise whole, as if all of it went on. Of 244 certified steps in the student corpus and the user's saved maps, 15 had such a premise, and so did 14 kinds of step in a catalog of every rule: the painter matched whole clauses, and split one into what it is about and what it says only where the step had "all" or "some".

* Such a premise is now taken in pieces, matched coarsest first: what a claim is about, and what it says -- that in its parts ("a black raven": black, raven), as the name it gives ("is Samuel Clemens"), or as a verb and its object ("loves | every raven"); an identity's two names; each claim in a compound with no words of its own ("not both black and white").
  - "Mark Twain is Samuel Clemens", "Mark Twain wrote Huckleberry Finn", so "Samuel Clemens wrote Huckleberry Finn": "Mark Twain" meets in the two premises; "Samuel Clemens" and "wrote Huckleberry Finn" go on.
  - "Poe is a black raven", so "Poe is black": "Poe" and "black" go on, "raven" dimmed; so "there is a black raven": "Poe" dimmed.
  - "Poe loves every raven", "Fido is a raven", so "Poe loves Fido": "every raven" meets "is a raven"; "Poe loves" and "Fido" go on. Back by modus tollens, "every raven" goes on denied and "does not" is red against "loves"; "loves everything", so "loves Fido": "everything" goes on as "Fido".
  - "Bob is tall", so "Bob is not short": "tall" goes on as its contrary. An objection's word against its box ("have" against "lack") is red, as before.
  - In symbols, "a = b" and "F(a)" had no words to color: "a = b", "b = c", so "a = c" -- "b" meets, "a" and "c" go on.
* Painted whole now only where the premise is another wording of the conclusion: "Poe is not not black", so "Poe is black"; quantifier negation; "∀x ∀y", so "∀y ∀x".
* Nothing that was placed is painted otherwise: of 360 steps in the corpus and the saved maps, the 17 premises that had been painted whole are painted in pieces, and no other.

Tests. colors-r27-inference-test.js (118; section 10 new): every premise of 47 certified steps placed, a rule or two each, but another wording of the conclusion; the pieces above. Take-one-out: each edit is caught. All 90 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 22, night: the kinds after a verb (r27.120)

The user, of step 4 of the Extended Mind map: "It says, 'All As are Bs', 'F plays the role of an A', so, 'F plays the role of a B', no?" And, of a rule that would read it so: "If it's strictly speaking ambiguous, then no. The ambiguity checker would need to be able to offer a suggestion."

* Existential syllogism with the kinds after the verb: "Bob hit a poodle" (or "some poodle") and "every poodle is a dog" give "Bob hit a dog", as "some F is G" and "every G is H" give "some F is H". A rule worded "a subject ... that subject" is said of the one the objects name: "... play the same causal/explanatory role as some non-occurrent beliefs within his brain" and "All non-occurrent beliefs within a subject's brain are mental states of that subject" give "... as some mental states of Otto". Not after "fears", "seeks" and their kin, a modal or "not"; not from a dog to a poodle; not with an adjective kept ("a large mouse" is no large animal); not what "is" says ("is a mental state of Otto" is a predicate, and that step is universal modus ponens, as before).
* Where an object can mean more than "some" of its kind, the step asks. A bare plural ("the same role as non-occurrent beliefs") may mean some of them, all of them, or what holds of them in general. "A" in a comparison ("taller than a poodle", "the same role as a belief") may mean some one or any one. After "not", "any" takes the place of "some". A step that follows on a reading and not as written waits, as one turning on "birds fly" does, and these questions are asked together with the generics the step turns on: step 4 as the map words it asks three -- of its premise, of the rule it uses, and of its conclusion -- and says the readings that give it. Each reading is the box's own words with "some", "all", "any" or "in general" put in, and choosing one writes it into the box. "Poe loves ravens", "Fido is a raven", so "Poe loves Fido" asks too, read as "all". A step that follows as written asks nothing: step 3, its two "mental states" alike, is as it was.
* A plural with a phrase or a clause after it is a generic, as "ravens are black" is: "Non-occurrent beliefs within a subject's brain are mental states of that subject", "Students who study pass" and "Ravens in the park are black" ask what they mean where a step turns on it. They had been read as one thing's name.
* "Beliefs within his brain" names the kind "belief within his brain", as "beliefs in his brain" does: "within", "inside", "beyond" and their kin start a phrase after a noun, and an object's "some" ends before them ("some beliefs within his brain" is "some belief within his brain").
* "Does not own any poodles" denies "owns some poodles": after "not" (or in a condition), "any" and a plural are read as "some" and it. The plural had been kept.
* Colors: the verb's words go on to the conclusion, the object meets the rule's kind, and the rule's predicate goes on as the conclusion's object.

The Extended Mind map. As the map stands, with the premise added for step 4, nothing changes. Without that premise, step 4 asks its three questions; answered ("some", "all", "some"), it follows by existential syllogism. Then step 3's rule asks for "some" too ("Anything that plays the same causal/explanatory role as some mental states of a subject ..."), and the rebuttal of step 4's first premise for "any" ("... do not play the same causal/explanatory role as any non-occurrent beliefs within Otto's brain"), each with the reading that gives it.

In the user's saved maps, no step or verdict changes.

### October 22, evening: a presentation map's "ambiguous" step (r27.119)

The user, of a presentation map: "This map also has some issues, particularly with the part labeled "ambiguous". The colors aren't matching, the disambiguation is wrong, and it doesn't certify."

* "If an AI system is reliably and routinely consulted by a student as part of the student's thinking process, then the AI system functions as a cognitive extension", "An AI system is reliably and routinely consulted ..." and "The AI system functions as a cognitive extension": modus ponens, as written. It had waited on its question about "an AI system" after "if". The "any" reading that question offered was the sentence as written: "an ai system", looked for without its capitals' marks, was not found in words that have them, so "any" was never put in -- and on that reading the step followed, so it asked. The question now says "any AI system" and "AI systems in general", with the capitals.
* "Is reliably and routinely consulted" is one part: adverbs in -ly joined before the word they go with are no part of their own. "Is reliably" had been one claim and "is routinely consulted ..." another, which split the colors. Not "is not even or odd".
* An acronym before its noun names a kind with it -- "AI system", "US senator" -- so there is no question whether "AI" describes the system itself.
* A question about "a ..." or a bare plural says its words with their capitals ("an AI system", "big Norwegian ravens"), not with the marks the reader keeps.

In the user's saved maps, the presentation map's step certifies, and the three boxes above it are established. No other step or verdict changes.

### October 22, afternoon: "a subject ... that subject" one step again; "can" red; "is" with what it says; a premise the step does not use (r27.118)

The user, of r27.117's two steps: "The idea is that you can put in any name for the subject. It's equivalent to 'For any entity, anything that is a mental state of that entity is a part of that entity's mind.' At least that's the reading I see. I don't see any other reading. And if that's what it's saying, then shouldn't it just be one step?" Of an objection: "The word "can" in the white conclusion ... should be red, no? It contradicts its parent on that predicate (can vs cannot)." And of the Extended Mind map: "The clause parsing ... isn't going quite right (e.g., "is", "are", "is itself", etc. breaking clauses) ... And none of the steps certify."

* One step. "Anything that is a mental state of a subject is a part of that subject's mind" and its case, "The records in Otto's notebook are mental states of Otto", give "The records in Otto's notebook are a part of his mind" by universal modus ponens: one general rule and one case, which gives each quantifier its thing, as "every raven that sings is black", "Fido is a raven" and "Fido sings" are one step. The two steps of "every man loves every woman" are for conditions nested one inside another, each with a case of its own; r27.117 had taken this rule for one of those.
* A noun that stands for anything at all -- "subject", "thing", "entity", "object", "individual", "item", "being" -- takes no premise that the thing is one (the user: "for any entity"); one given ("Otto is a subject") is used. A noun that limits the rule to its kind takes one in the same step -- "a person ... that person", with "Mary is a person" -- as "everyone" takes "Socrates is a person"; without it, the check says so: "“Anything that is a child of a person is loved by that person” would give “Bob is loved by Mary” once it is shown that ‘Mary is a person’." The rule said of Otto alone, given "Otto is a subject", is still a step of its own (r27.117).
* A premise the step does not use is named: "“Non-occurrent beliefs within a subject's brain are mental states of that subject” is not needed: the other premises give “The records in Otto's notebook play the same causal/explanatory role as mental states of Otto” by universal modus ponens, and a step uses every premise."
* Colors. What is said of a kind or a thing takes its "is": "is a part of that subject's mind", "are mental states of Otto", "is itself a mental state of that subject", "All ravens | are black". The copula had been dimmed between the parts, so a clause looked broken. And an objection's affirming word is red against a box that denies it however many denials both say: "can" against "cannot", which "non-occurrent" in both had left plain.

Of the 164 boxes in the user's saved maps, no verdict changes, nor does any of their 69 steps.

### October 22: "a subject ... that subject", one subject at a time (r27.117)

The user, of the step r27.115 had certified -- "Anything that is a mental state of a subject is a part of that subject's mind" and "The records in Otto's notebook are mental states of Otto", so "The records in Otto's notebook are a part of his mind": "Should it? I wasn't sure if what we did was valid, and a few steps might have been skipped. I was just using ordinary reasoning, but if we were to be logically precise, we might need to use additional steps to go from Otto to the subject."

* By the check's own standard, it should not. The step is valid, but it takes two steps and a premise the map does not have. "Every man loves every woman", "Bob is a man" and "Mary is a woman" take two steps to "Bob loves Mary", one quantifier at a time; and "Everyone loves Mary" gives "Socrates loves Mary" only once Socrates is shown to be a person. So the rule is said of Otto once Otto is shown to be a subject: with "Otto is a subject" it gives "Anything that is a mental state of Otto is a part of Otto's mind" by universal modus ponens, a step of its own, and that rule with "The records in Otto's notebook are mental states of Otto" gives "The records in Otto's notebook are a part of his mind". r27.115 had gone from the rule to the case at once, without "Otto is a subject".
* The step that goes to the case at once is no longer certified, and the check says what it takes: "Getting to “The records in Otto's notebook are a part of his mind” takes two steps, and a premise the map does not have, ‘Otto is a subject’. First, “Anything that is a mental state of a subject is a part of that subject's mind” and ‘Otto is a subject’ give ‘Anything that is a mental state of Otto is a part of Otto's mind’ by universal modus ponens. Then ... by universal modus ponens. Add ‘Anything that is a mental state of Otto is a part of Otto's mind’ as a box of its own, with ... under it." With "Otto is a subject" beside them it still takes two steps, and the check says so; words that differ in the second step ("role" and "roles") are named there.
* Not under "some", nor said of another subject than the one shown, nor of a person for a subject.

Of the 164 boxes in the user's saved maps, no verdict changes, nor does any of their 69 steps.

### October 21, night: named claims (r27.116)

The user: 'there are some nodes that involve naming a principle, like: "Ex nihilo principle: Nothing comes from nothing". I want the content of the principle to be counted, not the name of the principle. It's sort of doing double duty as a premise and a definition such that all instances of the ex nihilo principle refer to the definition. I want those to behave cleanly and as one would expect in the argument mapper.'

* A box "<name>: <claim>" says its claim, and names it. The name is a principle, a law, a thesis or one of their kin ("the problem of evil", "the law of identity"), an "-ism" ("Physicalism"), an acronym ("PSR"), or a name with its acronym ("The principle of sufficient reason (PSR)"). "Ex nihilo principle: Nothing comes from nothing" had been read as one claim, its name and all, which matched nothing. A kind of claim alone ("Definition:", "Thesis:") names nothing, and the box says its claim, as "Premise 1:" does. Not a clause before the colon ("There are two options: stay or go").
* Wherever the map says the name as a claim, it is read as the claim, as a premise link is: "the ex nihilo principle is true" (or "holds"), "... is false" (or "does not hold", "is mistaken"), "if the PSR, then ...", "physicalism implies that ...", the name alone. As a noun it stays a name: "Aquinas accepts the ex nihilo principle", "Physicalism, the view that ...", "theism and atheism".
* The colors put the claim's words where its name is: "the ex nihilo principle is true" takes the color of "Nothing comes from nothing" beside it. In the box that names it, the name is plain.
* A premise link to the box reads its claim without its name (so too a link to a box labelled "P1:"). A name inside a named claim is read too, no further than once round. A name the map gives two claims is read as neither, and the check says so: "“ex nihilo principle” names more than one claim on this map (“Nothing comes from nothing” and “Something cannot come from nothing”), so it is read as neither: give each its own name."
* Help says how to name a claim, in one sentence.

Of the 164 boxes in the user's saved maps, no verdict changes, nor does any of their 69 steps.

Tests. named-claims-r27-test.js (new, 20). Take-one-out: each edit is caught, the ones others build on together with them. The corpora read as before (583 sentences); none of the 118 fallacies is certified. All 90 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 21, evening: an Extended Mind map (r27.115)

The user, of a student's map on Otto's notebook: "This file has what I think is valid reasoning throughout, but a lot of things get marked as unrecognized. Moreover, in color mode, some of the clauses aren't being colored as expected ... Make use of the internal syntax tree as well."

Of its eleven steps, two had been certified. Four more failed on misreadings, now fixed; five failed on the map's own words, which the check now points to.

* A clause cut short inside a condition: "If non-occurrent beliefs can automatically influence reasoning while Otto's notebook can't, then ...". The cut-short clause's subject had run back to the "if", so no subject was found and the "can't" was left unread; it now runs back to its own clause's start ("while"), and the box is read as it is alone. After "it's not the case that", the clause before is the one inside the that-clause, where only that one's helping verb is of the kind.
* "It's not the case that P while Q" denies "P while Q" whole: the that-clause runs on to the end. It had been read as one claim, "while Q, it is not the case that P", which denied nothing, so the objection to "P while Q" now follows.
* A "while" claim keeps its words as the box has them, for the colors: "P while Q" is the claim "while Q, P", whose words had been looked for in that order and not found, so a certified step's condition and premise were dimmed.
* Letters. A capital "A" inside a sentence is a letter, not the article: "belief set A changes ..." had read "A changes" as a noun phrase and "set" as its verb. By the syntax tree, a letter after a noun is its label ("belief set A", "person B"), and the subject runs on through it to its verb. Letters listed as a noun's labels, "belief sets B, C, D, and E", are one phrase after a noun of a subject or an object (not after the clause's verb: "Bob believes A, B, and C" lists sentence letters): their commas and their "and" join no claims, as a bracket's do not. The list had been read as four claims, "C", "D" and "E" sentence letters, and the condition "if belief set A changes belief sets B, C, D, and E, and if ..." as "if A changes B, then C, D, and E". The step now follows by modus ponens with its conditions together, and the list is one color. "All B are A", "Some B are A", "Bob believes A" and "Mary said that A is true" keep their "A", which had been dropped as an article ("all B are A" had been left unread).
* "Anything that is a mental state of a subject is a part of that subject's mind": "that subject" is the one "a subject" speaks of, any subject, as a second "every" says. The step is tried with the rule said of each subject the step's other claims name ("Otto"), so with "The records in Otto's notebook are mental states of Otto" it gives "The records in Otto's notebook are a part of his mind" by universal modus ponens. So too "a child of a person ... that person" (or "the person"), "every mental state of a subject", "a subject's brain", and universal modus tollens; not under "some", and not of another subject. The colors take the rule as said of Otto.
* "Is itself a mental state" says what "is a mental state" says ("is not himself today" keeps "himself"). "A child of a person" keeps its "person": the rule that makes "black things" read as "black" had made it "child of a". In "anything that plays the same role as mental states of a subject is ...", "states" after "as mental" is a plural noun, not the verb after the relative clause.
* What the check says. What a denial denies, in its own words: "influencing reasoning is a necessary function of ...", which had been shown as "influencing reasoning necessary function of ...", so the typo "fucntion" read as two differences. A near miss in words is "Worded the same way each time" ("the mind" and "minds"), not "Spelled". The premises of an objection or a rebuttal that give a claim that does not deny its box: "The premises give “The records in Otto's notebook do not have to perform all the relevant functions of non-occurrent beliefs” by modus ponens, which does not deny “The records in Otto's notebook cannot perform all the relevant functions of non-occurrent beliefs”" (r27.99 said this of supports only). "That subject" in a box with no subject before it, beside a box worded "a subject ... that subject": "“that subject” points back to nothing in this box, and each box is read alone: name the subject (“Otto”?)". A rule's case worded nearly as a premise words it is a near miss, as a claim is ("role" and "roles"); the near misses of a rule that then failed are no longer listed. A claim the check makes says its "is" ("Bob is loved by Mary"), and a copula's number is no difference in words. The verdict's reason ends with one full stop, not two.
* The map's own words, which the check now points to: the objection's "the mind extends into the world" against "Minds don't extend into the world" ("minds extend" follows); "that subject" with nothing before it in the box steps 3 and 4 share, and "role" and "roles", "mental states" and "a mental state" in step 3; step 4 takes a rule from "the same role as non-occurrent beliefs" to "the same role as mental states" (a premise "Anything that plays the same causal/explanatory role as non-occurrent beliefs within a subject's brain plays the same causal/explanatory role as mental states of that subject" gives it); "do not have to perform" does not deny "cannot perform"; "fucntion". With the words made alike, ten of the eleven steps certify; the eleventh does not follow.
* The colors follow the check's reading of each box, so that they show what the check matched. The syntax tree now decides more of that reading (a letter's label, a list of them, where a relative clause ends), and with the readings right, the colors were too.

Of the 164 boxes in the user's saved maps, no verdict changes. Of their 69 steps, two say more: an objection's premises give a claim that does not deny its box.

Tests. extended-mind-r27-test.js (new, 34); hints-r27-test.js (an objection's premises: what they give, and that it does not deny the box). Take-one-out: each of the 32 edits is caught, the one the "any subject" reading begins with together with the edits built on it. The student corpus reads as before (the same 82 of 258 steps uncertified); none of the 118 fallacies is certified; the soundness sweep (56,436 steps) finds no false validity. All 89 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 21: an objection whose premise has lost its support fails (r27.114)

The user, of an Experience Machine map with every step certified and its contention unwarranted: "So, all the parts check now, but it says it's unwarranted. The initial supporting premises were successfully defended at the bottom, so, what gives?"

* A step that rests on a box whose every argument has been defeated fails with it. In the map, the objection to "Someone could rationally choose not to plug into the experience machine" rested on "Real experiences are not more valuable than fake ones in the world of the experience machine", whose one support fell when its premise "We are unable to say ..." was refuted. That objection had been left pending, neither standing nor defeated, so the box it attacked was contested, the support above it could not go through, and the contention was unwarranted. Now the objection fails: the box stands, the support holds, and the contention is warranted. A box never argued for is still assumed until challenged, and a box whose argument is still open (a weak rebuttal unsettled below it, say) still leaves the step resting on it pending.
* Of the 164 boxes in the user's saved maps, three verdicts change. In the Experience Machine, the contention goes from unwarranted to warranted, and the box the objection attacked from contested to standing. In the Chinese Room, the conditional at the top goes from contested to standing, but stays unwarranted: no objection succeeds, and it has no support of its own. There the objection's premise "Computers can't be minds" had lost its one argument to the rebuttal of "Minds are only semantic".

Tests. assessment-r27-test.js (an objection whose premise has lost every argument for it is fended off, and the contention is warranted; one whose premise is still argued over stays pending). All 88 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 20, night: a toolbar above the keyboard (r27.113)

The user, of the menu that a finger's hold in a box being edited no longer opens (r27.111): "I wanted access to the right-click menu for mobile devices so they could insert links, switch the font, etc." Of three sketches -- a menu once words are selected, with a button for when none are; the button alone; a toolbar above the keyboard: "Option 3".

* On a touch screen, while a box is edited, a toolbar sits just above the keyboard with what the right-click menu has: B, I and U (bold, italic and underline, for the selected words or the word at the caret, as Ctrl+B does), "Link a premise" and "Link selection". Its buttons keep the box open and the keyboard up. It stays along the bottom of what is seen as the keyboard comes and goes, and goes when the box closes. With a mouse it never shows, and a right-click opens the menu as before.
* "Link selection" with no words selected says "Select words first".
* Linking a premise or a cross-reference on a touch screen, the hint says to tap the box, or the canvas to cancel: there is no Esc key to press there.

### October 20, late: "would" and "could"; red words by the syntax tree, and in a denied part (r27.112)

The user, of "everyone would rationally choose to plug into the experience machine" against "someone could rationally choose not to plug into the experience machine": 'So, "Everyone would" is ∀x □P vs "Someone could not", ∃x ◇~P, and ∀x □P is equivalent to ~∃x ◇~P, and ~∃x ◇~P straightforwardly contradicts ∃x ◇~P.' Asked whether the duality belongs in Standard or behind a switch: "Let's add that as a switch but leave it on by default." Of a rebuttal's colors: 'it makes "never" red instead of "no", yet "no" is the words that contradicts, not "never" ("never" is in both)'; and "We could always try using the internal syntax tree to reveal it." Then: "I think we should always make the contradictory part of a red-underlined conclusion red (as in the modus tollens picture attached)", and "So there, 'is' would be red".

* "Would" and "could", a switch in the Logic panel under Modal logic, on unless a map switches it off. Said of a choice -- "choose", "decide", "opt", "elect" -- with its "not" plain, "would" and "could" are duals: "everyone would choose to stay" denies "someone could choose not to stay", "Bob would choose to stay" denies "Bob could choose not to stay", and "everyone would decide to leave" is "no one could decide not to leave". After "then", such a "would" is what is chosen, kept in the claim, and the conditional is no what-if. So the map's objection follows by modus ponens. Switched off, "would" after "then" makes a what-if claim, as before. A "would" not said of a choice ("the ground would be wet") is a what-if either way.
* The red word of a denial that contradicts is the outermost, by the phrases of the syntax tree: in the clause's own verb or an object of it, before one in a clause inside it ("that ...", "who ...", "not to ..."); then the first. In "learning that someone never really loved you gives you no genuine reason ...", against "... gives you a genuine reason ...", "no" is red, not "never". In "the dog that never barks is not old", "is not" (it had been "never", the first).
* In a part that goes on to the conclusion denied (its red dashes), what the conclusion contradicts is red: a denial the conclusion does not say, or, where the conclusion says one the part does not, the part's affirming word. In "If pleasure is the only good, then no one could ...", with "Pleasure is not the only good" concluded by modus tollens, "is" is red; in "it does not rain", against "it rains", "does not"; in "it is not the case that both it rains and it snows", against "it does not rain or it does not snow", "rains" and "snows". An attack's parts are read, as before, against the box it attacks.

Tests. modal-r27-test.js (7: the map's objection by modus ponens; modus tollens with "could choose not to"; "everyone would decide to leave" is "no one could decide not to leave", and "Bob would choose to stay" gives "Bob could not choose not to stay"; switched off, not certified; "the ground would be wet" a what-if as before; the panel row, on by default). colors-r27-inference-test.js 109 ("no", not "never"; "is not", not "never" in a relative clause; "is" and "does not" in parts that go on denied, and four checks of such parts brought up to date). language-r27-qualifier-test.js 55 (no what-if for "would rationally choose"; the adverb check moved to "would rationally leave"). logic-r27-basis-test.js (the panel's thirteenth row). Take-one-out: each edit is caught. All 88 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test. Of the corpora's 582 sentences, none reads or is noted differently; the student corpus is as before; no fallacy is certified; the soundness sweep finds no false validity.

### October 20, night: holding a finger in a box being edited; a claim in one color; "light" a noun by its phrase (r27.111)

The user: "Android users (probably Apple users as well): when they try to highlight text in a node while in text edit mode, the context menu comes up and they can't highlight text." Of a rebuttal's inference colors: '"learning that someone never really loved you" is treated as an independent clause when it's not.' And: "We should be able to just use the built-in syntax trees to parse the parts of speech so that 'light' only give 'not heavy' when it's an adjective."

* On a touch screen, pressing and holding in a box being edited selects its words, as the browser does, and opens no menu. After half a second the hold had opened the box's menu, on iPhones and iPads too; on Android it also opened the menu of bold, italic and links, and so the browser selected nothing. A right-click with a mouse still opens that menu.
* Inference colors: a claim none of whose parts meets anything is one color. Beside a claim about all or nothing of a kind ("there is nothing bad about learning that ..."), a step the check did not recognize had colored each claim's subject apart from what it says ("learning that someone never really loved you" in one color, "gives you a genuine reason ..." in another), as if they were two claims. The claim was read as one. The check did not certify the step because it is a rebuttal, and its premises give the box it attacks, not that box's denial; as a support, it follows by modus ponens.
* A word of a pair of opposites is a noun where it heads a phrase that a determiner opens, by the phrases of the syntax tree: "is a light", "is the right", "is my light". So "The lamp is a light" does not give "The lamp is not heavy", nor "Voting is the right" give "Voting is not wrong" (it had, with "the"). "Mary is a good person" still gives "Mary is not a bad person". (r27.110 had looked only for "a" or "an" before a last word.)

Tests. editexit-r27-press-test.js 17 (6: a finger held in the box being edited opens no menu and prevents nothing; a right-click opens its menu). colors-r27-inference-test.js 107 (the claim in one color beside "there is nothing bad about ..."; an "all" whose parts meet nothing, one color). opposites-r27-test.js 24 ("the right" and "my light" nouns; "a friend of Lee" still the pair). Take-one-out: each edit is caught. All 88 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.
