### October 20, later: "tall" and "short"; "in it" when its clause puts the same thing outside; "would rationally choose" (r27.110)

The user, of pairs whose other word says which meaning ("John is tall" giving "John is not short"): "Sounds good." Of an "it" that takes its container from its own clause: "is it always the case ...? I'm having a difficult time coming up with a counterexample, so, maybe it's safe." And of "If real experiences are not more valuable than fake ones in the world of the experience machine, then everyone would rationally choose to plug into the experience machine" against "Someone could rationally choose not to plug into the experience machine": "It feels like this should work, but it doesn't."

* Contraries: "tall" and "short", "long" and "short", "heavy" and "light", "dark" and "light", "soft" and "hard", "easy" and "hard". These are words of more than one meaning, listed where the other word of the pair says which. "John is tall" gives "John is not short"; "John is not short" does not give "John is tall", as one can be neither. The list now has 78 pairs. A small risk of equivocation is taken: a map that uses "light" of weight in one box and of color in another can be certified across the two.
* A word after "a" or "an" that ends what is said is a noun, not the adjective of a pair, though the two read alike. "The lamp is a light" does not give "The lamp is not heavy", nor "Free speech is a right" give "Free speech is not wrong" (it had, by contraries "right" and "wrong"). "Mary is a good person" still gives "Mary is not a bad person".
* "In it": the container in the pronoun's own clause is taken only where something else is placed there ("real experiences outside the experience machine ... fake ones in it"). Where the clause puts the same thing outside it, the check asks: in "If the cat is in the box, then the mouse, which is outside the house, is in it too", the box or the house. It had taken the house, which the mouse would then be both in and outside of.
* What follows from a "would" keeps its adverbs before the verb: "everyone rationally chooses to plug into the experience machine" (it had said "everyone rationallies choose"); "Bob was not really happy"; "the streets quickly flood".

The map's step does not follow, and is not certified: "everyone would rationally choose to plug in" is not the denial of "someone could rationally choose not to plug in". What everyone would choose leaves open what someone could choose. "No one could rationally choose not to plug into the experience machine" is the denial, and with it the step follows by modus ponens. The map's rebuttal of "Learning that someone never really loved you gives you a genuine reason ..." had lost its "does not"; with it, it follows by modus ponens.

Tests. opposites-r27-test.js 23 (78 pairs, each both ways; 5: "tall" and "short" and the rest; not "a light", "a right", "short of money"; the rule's note). anaphora-r27-test.js 97 (18: the mouse outside the house, asked). language-r27-qualifier-test.js 54 (what would follow, with adverbs before the verb). Take-one-out: each edit is caught, except a comment. All 88 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test. Of the corpora's 582 sentences, none reads or is noted differently; the student corpus is as before; no fallacy is certified; the soundness sweep finds no false validity.

### October 20: "Bob makes cakes and sells them"; the Experience Machine again (r27.109)

The user, of "Bob makes cakes and sells them", where "them" had been offered "bob makes": "Can you fix this?" And of the reading chooser on the Experience Machine's objection, both of its rows greyed out: "I can't select an option here." Then: 'is this ambiguous? I only see "Mary loves dogs and Mary feeds dogs."'; of the map's steps, "Currently, these steps aren't verifying for me"; and 'It says "it" is ambiguous between "the world" and "the experience machine", which seems wrong.'

* A plural after its verb or a preposition, with no determiner ("makes cakes", "talks about cakes", "likes cats and dogs"), is what a "they" or "them" may be, where nothing else before it is many. "Bob makes cakes and sells them" says "sells cakes"; "Mary loves dogs and feeds them", "feeds dogs". Not where a plural opens a clause ("Ravens eat worms and they fly" is the ravens, and the worms are not asked about), nor a verb ("feeds them"), nor "lots of".
* A plural that opens a clause has its verb after it, as a plural takes one. "Bob makes cakes" had been read as opening with a plural, "bob makes"; "sells them" with "sells", taken for the subject of its own clause, so "them" was offered Bob, who sells.
* A clause with no verb of its own is no subject: in "Bob likes the cats and the dogs and feeds them", "them" is the cats or the dogs, not Bob.
* A capital that opens a clause before its noun is no name: in "Black ravens fly and they caw" and "Computer programs are formal and they lack semantics", "they" had been asked about "Black" and "Computer". "Brown left" and "Young sings" keep their names.
* "Lack" is a verb the reader knows: "computer programs lack semantics" had been read with "programs" as its verb.
* "Bob, a baker, makes cakes": no verb among adjectives parted by commas ("a baker, makes" had been one phrase).
* The chooser: "someone couldn't" is answered in the box's place, its other words kept: "If pleasure is the only good, then there is someone who could not rationally choose ..." or "... then no one could rationally choose ...", which the step follows from by modus tollens. The rows had put the "then" clause alone for the whole box, not the same claim, so neither could be picked. One person's reading is worded "there is someone who could not ...", which asks nothing more ("cannot" for "can").

* "Mary loves dogs and feeds them" is two claims, "Mary feeds dogs" one of them. After a verb in -s, a word in -s with a bare plural after it is a verb and its object, though no list knows the verb ("feeds", "feeds hungry dogs"). It had been one claim, "dogs and feeds dogs" what Mary loves. Not "loves dogs and sports cars" or "pays income and sales taxes" (a short list of plurals that go before a noun), nor "eats apples and pears raw".
* "Being genuinely loved has more value than ...": after "being" or "having", a participle, past its adverbs, is the gerund's own. "Being" had been the subject and "genuinely loved has more value ..." what was said of it, so "being genuinely loved does not have more value ..." was not its denial, and the objection that gives that denial was not certified. It is now, by modus ponens. ("Being loved" had been read rightly.)
* "Fake ones in it": an "it" that things are in is what its own clause puts things in or out of ("outside the experience machine ... fake ones in it"), not one in another clause ("in the world of the experience machine", in the "if" before it), which had made it a question. Where it is asked, "the world of the experience machine" is offered whole, not "the world". The map's support step then follows by modus tollens with "would" for "should", as the check says.

Tests. anaphora-r27-test.js (17: "them" the cakes, and "sells cakes" follows; "feeds them" two claims, "sports cars" and "pears raw" one; "Bob sells them"; the cats or the dogs, not Bob; "black ravens", "computer programs"; "lack"; "Bob, a baker,"; and as before: the ravens, Brown, Young; "they sell well" asked. 18: "fake ones in it" the experience machine, modus tollens; "the world of the experience machine" whole). language-r27-qualifier-test.js 53 (the readings' words; the chooser's rows picked and the box rewritten, modus tollens; "cannot"; "being genuinely loved", its denial, and the objection by modus ponens). Take-one-out: each edit is caught, but a comment. All 88 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test. Of the corpora's 582 sentences none reads or notes differently; the student corpus as before; no fallacy is certified; the soundness sweep finds no false validity.

### October 15, evening: the Experience Machine (r27.108)

The user, of a map on the experience machine: 'It registers "it" as being ambiguous between "that someone" (the "that" should be interpreted as a conjunction rather than a demonstrative determiner). That's just one problem. There's a bunch of other things it gets wrong. Can we address them? This map seems absolutely unambiguous and valid to me.'

* "That" before "someone", "something", "everyone" and their kin, or after a word that takes a clause ("learning that", "knowing that", "told the boy that"), opens a clause. It is no "that" pointing ("that someone"), and no relative pronoun. "Nothing bad about learning that someone never really loved you" had been read as "nothing bad about learning", which someone never really loved; "Bob is sad about learning that someone never loved him" as two claims; "every student learning that Mary left" as a learning Mary left.
* "It". The deed a gerund names after its preposition ("about learning that someone never really loved you") is a thing an "it" may be. What a relative clause says something of is not that clause's own "it" ("the bad feeling it produces"). An "it" in or out of which things are ("fake ones in it") is what the box puts things in or out of ("outside the experience machine"), and not what a clause says. "Being" before a participle opens a clause ("the pleasure being loved gives you"), so an "it" may be "the pleasure", not "the pleasure being".
* "Ones" after an adjective stands for a plural after its own adjective earlier in the box, with no determiner: "what makes real experiences ... more valuable than fake ones", "big dogs and small ones". It had stood for nothing there, though "fake ones" beside a clause opening with "real experiences" stood for experiences, so the support's two boxes did not match. Not a verb ("the pleasure being loved gives you").
* "Never", "always" and their kin inside a clause taken whole ("learning that someone never loved you", "sad that Mary never sings") get no note: that clause is not read there either.
* A box read again with a pronoun's referent asks every question it asks. A clause the referent left alone had come back from memory without its question: "someone could not ..." beside "fake ones"; "we should conclude" beside "it".
* "Someone couldn't rationally choose not to plug in" may say that one person couldn't, or that no one could. Where a step turns on it, the step asks ("? ambiguous") and says what follows: read as "no one could ...", the objection follows by modus tollens and the rebuttal by modus ponens. Answered, the box says "no one could ...". "Someone didn't" and "someone could" are not asked about.
* What the check says is English. The denial of "someone could choose" is "no one could choose" (it had been "every person not could choose"); of "someone sings", "no one sings"; of "someone doesn't sing", "everyone sings"; of "some ravens can fly", "no raven can fly". And "learning that ... does not give you a reason", where it had been "not learning that ... gives you a reason".

What the map still asks, in its own words (the check matches claims worded alike): the support's antecedent, "real experiences outside the experience machine are more valuable than fake ones in machine", against the box it supports, "Real experiences are not more valuable than fake ones in the world of the experience machine" -- and its "should be able to say", which "We are unable to say" refutes neither as a hedge nor as an ought (the check says so: with "would", modus tollens); the rebuttal's "does not give you a reason" against "gives you a genuine reason"; and the three-premise objection, which takes two steps (modus ponens twice) where a step is one rule.

Tests. anaphora-r27-test.js (16: "learning that ..." and "it" the learning; "fake ones in it" the experience machine; "sad about learning that ...", one claim; "ones" after an adjective). language-r27-qualifier-test.js 49 (10 more: "someone couldn't" asked, "didn't" and "could" not; the step's question and its "? ambiguous" tag; with "no one could", modus tollens; a box read again still asks; denials as English; "that" after "learning"; "the pleasure being"; "never" in a clause taken whole). Take-one-out: each edit is caught, but two that make the new question a scope question everywhere the "any" and "can" questions are. All 88 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test. Of the corpora's 582 sentences none reads differently or notes differently; the student corpus as before; no fallacy is certified; the soundness sweep finds no false validity.

The user, of the objection "Computers can't be minds." / "If O1, then not M1.", O1 a link to the box beside it: "In both color modes, the "not" in O2 doesn't turn red, and the conclusion doesn't validate, when I switch the antecedent from "computers can't be minds" to the link O1 (which has the same content)."

* Read with the links' words, "If (Computers can't be minds), then not (... the woman ... her ...)" offered "Computers" as what "her" may be: a capital after a bracket was taken for a name. The step waited on that question. A pronoun inside brackets now goes back to what they hold, where they hold something it can be, as a linked box's words are read as the box is alone (r27.89). And we can't even tell in the first color mode that S6 and S3 have the right [consequent]." Of four mockups: "Let's go with A, but the tints are a little difficult to distinguish between." Behind a link's short label the teal tint and colors 2's green, both dim blue-greens, had looked alike. ### October 15: a part one piece with its red words; a later condition's "if"; "really"; "the butler did it"; "fails to" (r27.106)

The user, of the green line under "not S1" broken at the space between the red "not" and the link: "Still getting this gap (see attached)." And: "add/delete/edit anything else to improve the app."

* A word that denies, in a part with a tint or a line, is a span inside the part's own, so the part is one piece, its tint and line unbroken. A space at the edge of a span had gone without the part's line in some browsers, between the red "not" and the link in "not S1". In a dimmed part a red word stays a span of its own, so that it is never dimmed. Pictures draw them as before.

Not after "is" ("is really tall" may say "is very tall"), nor after "not" ("not really" may hedge). r27.105's report had said "God really exists" was read; it was not.

### October 14, night: an attack's denial however worded; a plain claim against a denial; "S1 is false" (r27.105)

The user, of the objection "If computers can't be minds, then not S1." (S1 an if-then): "Maybe the negation should be shown in either the parent or the child conclusion that rejects it so it can be easy to see that one rejects the other. But then there's the problem that if the parent has the red negation... Maybe we just mark the predicate in the non-negated child red? E.g., in the attached image, I think "not" in "not S1" should be red. What do you suggest when we have a negated parent and a non-negated child?" Then: "We should probably give the same treatment to all the negation locutions ("it's not the case that", "is not the case", "is false", "is false that", etc.)."

* An objection's or a rebuttal's denial of its box is red where it denies the box whole, as well as clause by clause: "not" in "not S1", "it is false that" in "it is false that S1", with S1 an if-then or any box the reader takes apart. "Not S1" had stayed green.
* Each wording of a denial that the reader takes is one denial, red whole: "it's not the case that", "it isn't true that", "it is untrue that", "it is not so that"; and, after what it denies, "is false", "is untrue", "is not the case", "isn't true". "It's not the case that" had been only its "not", and "is false" no denial. "Is not false" is two.
* The reader: a premise link denied after it ("S1 is false", "S1 is not the case", "S1 is untrue", "S1 is not true"), or affirmed ("S1 is true"), is read as a letter or a quotation is, with the box's words its claim. These had gone unread.
* A claim with no denial against a box with one ("God exists" against "God does not exist"): the word stressed in saying it back is red, in the objection. That is its verb, or the first word of what it says of its subject ("exists"; "is" in "Socrates is mortal"; "can" in "computers can think"), past an adverb; or its quantifier ("some" against "No ravens are white"; "is" in "there is a God"). A word that opposes one of the box's still goes first ("have" against "lack"). Nothing is marked in a compound. The box attacked keeps its own colors: red there is its own step's.
* A pronoun: in "fool the investigator into identifying it", the investigator does the identifying, so "it" is not the investigator, and is read as "the computer" (it had been "the investigator"). "Talked the boss into hiring him": "him" is not the boss. "Investigator", "interrogator", "examiner", "experimenter", "observer", "interviewer", "researcher", "guard", "referee", "umpire" and "juror" are people.

### October 14, evening: Ctrl+K again; an objection's denial in color; links blue; rich text on (r27.104)

The user, of Shift+L: "Oh, let's revert that. It should be Ctrl + whatever is most intuitive and available." And: "(1) Color negations red in green conclusions if they reject the parent premise/contention. Not sure what to do if they're non-negated and reject the parent premise/contention, however. (2) There's a strange space in-between green conclusion words and premise links in light mode (dark mode seems fine). * Ctrl+K again, as in Word; Shift+L (r27.103) goes, and is Labels, as L is. While typing in a box, Ctrl+K links a premise at the caret; with a box selected, into it, where its caret was when typing in it was last finished (if its words are as they were; else after them), as Link a Premise on the box's menu does. The menu items and Help name Ctrl+K.
* An objection's or a rebuttal's denial of the box it attacks is red where it goes on to the conclusion: "it is not the case that" in "it is not the case that physicalism is false", "don't" in "pre-linguistic humans don't lack minds" -- as many denials as it says beyond the box's own, the outermost first. Where it denies the box with a word and no denial, that word is red: "have" against "lack", "true" against "false", "sad" against "happy". Where no word does the denying ("God exists" against "God does not exist"), nothing is. Not a weak objection's, which does not deny the box.

### October 14, later: contraries and contradictories; Shift+L; the Given button (r27.103)

The user, of a dictionary of contraries and contradictories for (weak) negations: "Sure." Also: "switch Ctrl + K to Shift + L for premise link if possible", and "the green background of the Given button on the left panel seems darker than on the canvas (the canvas is good, the button is not)."

* Contraries and contradictories, a list of 72 pairs, in the Logic panel as "Word meaning: contraries and contradictories", on unless a map switches it off. Of a contrary pair both cannot be true, but both can be false ("unhappy" and "happy": one can be neither): "Mary is unhappy" gives "Mary is not happy", and that is all. Of a contradictory pair, one or the other is true of whatever both can be said of ("lack" and "have"): each gives the other's denial, and each's denial gives the other ("Pre-linguistic humans did not have minds" gives "Pre-linguistic humans lacked minds"). Eight pairs are contradictories: "lack" and "have" or "possess", "absent" and "present", "guilty" and "innocent", "married" and "unmarried", "distinct from" and "identical to" or "with", "different from" and "the same as". The 64 contraries include "unhappy", "immoral", "unjust", "irrational" and the like (left out of the denials "im-" and "in-" make, as one can be neither), "sad" and "happy", "good" and "bad" or "evil", "hot" and "cold", "dead" and "alive", "odd" and "even", "contingent" and "necessary", "friend of" and "enemy of", and the verbs "reject" and "accept", "lose" and "win", "fail" and "pass", "forget" and "remember", "forbid" and "allow". Not listed: words of more than one meaning ("light", "hard", "short"), and pairs whose opposition is a thesis, not a meaning ("free" and "determined", "mental" and "physical"), which a map states as a premise.
* A step of one pair is its own step, its tag naming the pair as the words come: "✓ contraries ‘unhappy’ and ‘happy’", "✓ contradictories ‘have’ and ‘lack’". In any tense ("lacked", "did not have", "was dead", "will be dead", "will lack", "forbade"), of all or some of a kind ("all zombies are dead" gives "no zombie is alive"), of an identity ("Kim is the same as Lee" is the denial of "Kim is different from Lee"), and against the box an objection attacks ("Pre-linguistic humans have minds" against "Pre-linguistic humans lack minds").
* Hints. Contraries taken for contradictories: "‘happy’ and ‘unhappy’ are contraries: one can be neither, so “Mary is not happy” does not give “Mary is unhappy”." With the group switched off: "With Word meaning: contraries and contradictories switched on in the Logic panel, the premises would give “Mary is not happy” by contraries ‘unhappy’ and ‘happy’." Two rules in one step, the claim between them in the box's own words: "Getting to “Zombies are not possible” takes two steps. First, “Pre-linguistic humans have minds” gives “Pre-linguistic humans do not lack minds” by contradictories ‘have’ and ‘lack’. Then ..."
* Shift+L, with a box selected, links a premise into it, as Link a Premise on its menu does: where its caret was when typing in it was last finished, if its words are as they were; else after them. To link while typing, press Enter to finish, then Shift+L (Shift+L in a box types an L). Ctrl+K goes, and is the browser's again. Help lists Shift+L beside Shift+R, and Link a Premise on the box's menu names it.
* The Given button: the same green as a given box, but on a panel as light as the green itself, where the canvas behind a box is darker, so it read darker. In the dark theme its green is lighter, by as much as the canvas is darker than the panel; the light theme is unchanged.

Tests. opposites-r27-test.js 19 (new): every pair both ways, by name; tenses, kinds, identity, objections; what is not taken; the hints; the panel. shortcuts-r27-link-test.js: Shift+L (18 checks). typecolors-r27-menu-test.js: the Given button. logic-r27-basis-test.js: the panel's twelfth group. logic-r27-deductive-test.js: the optional rules, with the new one. Take-one-out: each edit is caught, but for a comment. The soundness sweep and the fuzzers find no step certified that does not follow; the corpora are unchanged. All 88 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 14: keys for linking, "pre-linguistic humans", colors on links, the picture as the canvas shows it (r27.102)

The user: "A few things. (1) A keyboard shortcut for premise linking would be useful. Maybe one for cross-referencing as well. (2) For some reason, "Pre-linguistic humans don't lack minds" is not considered the negation of "Pre-linguistic humans lack minds"; we need to fix that, I think. (3) In color mode, highlighting on linked premises doesn't work as well as you'd expect, especially if it's a negated conclusion (green with red dotted underline). (4) Colors from color mode aren't showing when saving as a PNG. Also, bracketed node names are showing instead of how they show on the canvas. The PNG (and other image types) should look identical to the canvas display save for the map, collapse, and cross-reference buttons (cross-references might need to have some other way of showing their content, e.g., "See NODE#")."

* Keys. Ctrl+K, while typing in a box, links a premise at the caret, as Word's Ctrl+K inserts a link: the box clicked next is linked there, as with Link a premise on the box's menu. Elsewhere it says what it is for. Shift+R, with a box selected, cross-references it: the box clicked next is the one it refers to. R alone is still Rebuttal. Help lists both, and the menu items name their keys.
* "Pre-linguistic humans lack minds" had been read with "humans" as its verb and "pre-linguistic" as its subject, so its denial was not recognized. An -s word after adjectives alone is the plural noun they go with, where a verb follows it ("conscious humans lack minds", "the conscious humans lack minds"). A hyphenated word is an adjective when its last part is one ("non-human"), or when a prefix (pre-, post-, non-, anti-, self-, ...) is on a word that ends as one ("pre-linguistic", "post-modern"); "self-esteem" is not. "Pre-linguistic humans don't lack minds" now denies it, and an objection that gives the denial is certified. The corpora read as before.
* Found beside it: "Free will exists" had been read with "will" as a helping verb ("free" its subject), so "If free will exists, then we are responsible" and "We are not responsible" gave "Free will not exists" by modus tollens, not "Free will does not exist". A helping verb is followed by a verb's plain form ("will exist"), never a finite one ("will exists", "will is"): before one, "will", "can", "may", "might" and "must" are nouns ("free will", "might makes right", "the can is empty"). The step is now certified, as is "Free will is an illusion; no illusion is real; so free will is not real". Two sentences of the student corpus read better ("Free will is necessary for moral responsibility"); no verdict changes.
* Colors. A premise link in a part has the part's color and lines (the conclusion's green and underline, a denied part's red dashes), not the link's blue and underline over them. An objection's step is colored against the denial of its box that the check matched ("it is not the case that physicalism is false"), not the first one it tried ("physicalism is true"); the premise that says it had been dimmed as unused. A part is marked as denied when it is not the conclusion's own claim. This is now decided by comparing the claims, not by counting their denials, which would have underlined "it is not the case that physicalism is false" in red.
* Pictures (PNG, JPEG, SVG, PDF). Each box's words are drawn as the canvas lays them out: each run of text where it stands, in its own color, weight, slant and opacity, with the tints behind words, underlines and lines through, links as their labels, code, and formulas, whose fonts are carried in the picture. So the inference colors, bold and italic show as on the canvas. A box's label is the small tag at its top left, not "[S1]" before its words. Where the canvas has a cross-reference's button, the picture says "See" and the labels of the boxes it points to ("See M2, S2"). Boxes have their shadows, and evaluation dots are drawn. Left out: the minimap, the collapse buttons, the cross-reference buttons, and the selection's ring. Also fixed: in the light theme the lines had the dark theme's colors (white on the light picture), and an objection's border was a darker red than the canvas's. The PDF's words, selectable as before, are measured in their own weight and slant.

Tests. shortcuts-r27-link-test.js 12 (new). picture-r27-test.js 32 (new), on a layout made up for the test: the words where they stand, their colors, tints and underlines; labels and "See"; the selection; the theme's colors; shadows; the math's fonts, from the page's own stylesheet in the public build or fetched for the source file; the PDF's words. syntax-r27-test.js: the readings (6 more). colors-r27-inference-test.js: links in parts, objections' conclusions (5 more). ### October 13, night: metaphysical necessity (r27.101)

The user: "Okay, let's try adding some modal logics." Then: one kind of necessity at a time ("we shouldn't have one broad form of necessity and possibility, as those are ambiguous"), metaphysical first, in S5; its system from the cube of K and the axioms D, T (= M), 4, B and 5 (the Stanford Encyclopedia's "Diagram of Modal Logics", which the user sent), chosen by switching the axioms on.

* The Logic panel: under "Modal logic", "Metaphysical necessity", off until a map switches it on. On, it shows the axioms D, T, 4, B and 5 and the system they make -- T and 5 to start, which is S5, with D, 4 and B implied, ticked and grayed. Every choice is one of the cube's fifteen systems (K, D, T, K4, D4, S4, K5, D5, K45, D45, KB, DB, B, KB5, S5). The choice is the map's, saved with it. "Modal (later)" leaves the base-logic menu: modal logic is a group a map switches on.
* Read: "it is metaphysically necessary that P", "it is metaphysically possible that P", "it is metaphysically impossible that P", "it is not metaphysically necessary that P", "in every metaphysically possible world, P", "there is a metaphysically possible world in which P", and □ and ◇ (◻, ◊, ⋄, ♢; LaTeX's \Box and \Diamond). A denial is the dual: "it is not metaphysically possible that P" is "it is metaphysically necessary that not P". What follows "that" runs to the end of the sentence, unless a comma before "and", "or" or "but" ends it there.
* Steps of one rule each, named by the axiom, and certified only with the group on and the axiom in the system: axiom K ("necessarily, if A, then B" and "necessarily A" give "necessarily B"; with "possibly A", "possibly B"), axiom D ("necessarily A" gives "possibly A"), axiom T ("necessarily A" gives A, and A gives "possibly A"), axiom 4 ("necessarily A" gives "necessarily necessarily A"; "possibly possibly A" gives "possibly A"), axiom B (A gives "necessarily possibly A"; "possibly necessarily A" gives A), axiom 5 ("possibly A" gives "necessarily possibly A"; "possibly necessarily A" gives "necessarily A"), and modal duality. Nothing like "A, so necessarily A", which holds of theorems only. The claims inside are matched as strictly as any; the operator's words are logic, as a quantifier's are ("in every metaphysically possible world, P" is "it is metaphysically necessary that P"). To the other rules a modal claim is one claim: modus ponens on it, commutation, De Morgan. These rules are a list of their own, not Standard's.
* Necessity without its kind -- "necessarily, P", "it is necessary that P", "it is impossible that P", "in every possible world, P", and "possibly, P" (beside "for all we know") -- is asked, with the group on, which kind is meant: "it is metaphysically necessary that P", the answer written into the box. With the group off it is read as before. "Must", "can", "could" and "might" stay as they are.
* A step the map's system does not take says what would: "With Metaphysical necessity switched on in the Logic panel, the premises would give “God exists” by axiom T." "The premises would give “...” by axiom 5, which this map's system, T, does not have: switch on 5 in the Logic panel."
* Help: "For modal logic, switch on Metaphysical necessity in the Logic panel and choose its axioms (S5 to start). Boxes can then say it is metaphysically necessary that …, it is metaphysically possible that …, or use □ and ◇."

Tests. modal-r27-test.js 37 (new), with a Kripke model checker of its own: each axiom's steps in each of the fifteen systems are certified exactly where every frame of the system, up to three worlds, has them (210 of 210); all 32 choices of axioms are named from the cube, and each implication the closure draws is true of the frames. logic-r27-basis-test.js: the panel's eleventh group, and two base logics. Take-one-out: each edit is caught. A fuzzer of modal steps judged by Kripke frames, for every choice of axioms: in three runs, of 33,600 steps in symbols and 17,006 in English, 16,786 and 8,833 were certified, none that a frame refutes, and none of each axiom's own steps was missed. The soundness sweep and the fuzzers find no step certified that does not follow, as before; the student corpora are unchanged. All 85 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 13, late: a fallacy's name is the step's tag; what someone knows or believes (r27.100)

The user, asked whether a named fallacy should change the tag itself ("✗ affirming the consequent" instead of "? not recognized"): "Yes." And: "Add/delete/edit anything that will improve the app."

* A step in the form of a common fallacy is tagged with its name, where it had been "? not recognized": "✗ affirming the consequent", "✗ denying the antecedent", "✗ converse", "✗ inverse", "✗ affirming a disjunct", "✗ undistributed middle", "✗ illicit major", "✗ illicit conversion", "✗ existential fallacy", "✗ some to all". So in the list of steps. So an objection or a rebuttal, against the denial of the box it attacks: "“All men are mortal” and “Socrates is mortal” do not give the denial of “Socrates is not a man”: that is the fallacy of undistributed middle." Not a weak objection, which would give only that a box has not been established. Help: "✗ and the name of a fallacy, such as ✗ affirming the consequent: the step has the form of a common fallacy, so its premises can be true and its conclusion false."
* The verdict takes such a step as invalid, as it takes one a counterexample shows invalid. An objection in the form of a fallacy is fended off: "no objection succeeds — the objection from ‘All men are mortal.’ and ‘Socrates is mortal.’ is invalid (undistributed middle)". A claim whose only support is a fallacy is "not established — the step from ‘All men are mortal.’ and ‘Socrates is mortal.’ is invalid (undistributed middle)", where the step "isn’t confirmed"; with an objection that stands, the claim is refuted, where it had been unresolved. "We cannot conclude that Socrates is a man from 'All men are mortal' and 'Socrates is mortal'" is warranted where the map makes that argument: it is invalid.
* Never where the step follows: a fallacy is named only of a step that can have true premises and a false conclusion, each of its claims not built of "not", "and", "or" or "if" taken as true or false. "If it rains, then both it rains and it is cold" and "It does not rain" do give "It is not the case that both it rains and it is cold", in the form of denying the antecedent though they are; r27.99 had named it.
* Letters again: "If Q, then R" and "R" to "Q" had shown "? not recognized" since r27.98 (the name had taken the place of the counterexample); it shows "✗ affirming the consequent".
* A quoted argument ("We cannot conclude that Socrates is a man from ...") is checked with the capitals the box gives it. Read as "socrates is a man", "socrates" had been a plural noun with a grammar slip, and the step waited on that.
* A premise that says what someone knows, shows or believes, where the check had said nothing: "With “All men are mortal” for “We know that all men are mortal”, the premises would give “Socrates is mortal” by universal modus ponens. But getting from the one to the other takes a rule for “know”, and the checker has none yet: such rules belong to epistemic logic." "“Mary believes that God exists” does not give “God exists”: what is believed can be false." So "everyone agrees that", "science shows that", "studies have shown that", "it is known that". Not "denies that", "nobody believes that", or "does not believe that". Nothing is certified that was not.

Tests. hints-r27-test.js 68 (16 new; the fallacy checks expect the tag). Take-one-out: each edit is caught, but one that a later edit builds on. Of the 118 student fallacies, 33 carry a fallacy's tag; none of the 258 student steps (each valid) does. In three fuzzer runs the check named 2,026 fallacies, objections among them, and the truth tables found every one of those steps invalid. The soundness sweep and the fuzzers find no step certified that does not follow, as before. All 84 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 13, night: one action for several selected boxes, and steps the check said nothing about (r27.99)

The user: "Add/delete/edit anything that will improve the app."

From the user's notes: "Stretch goal: Selecting multiple nodes and performing an action on one performs it on all of them, e.g., resizing, collapsing tree (only two I could find)."

* Collapse or expand: the button on one of several selected boxes does every selected box that has children, to the state the clicked one goes to. Its tip says so: "Collapse the children of this premise and of the other selected boxes".
* Resize: the width one selected box is dragged to, every selected box takes.
* Reset the width (a double-click on the grip, or a double tap on a touch screen): of every selected box.
* A box outside the selection, or a selection of one, acts alone, as before. Each action is one step to undo.

Of the 90 student steps the check does not certify, 21 said nothing beyond "? not recognized". Now 17 do (of the 118 student fallacies, 43 where 49 did):

* An inference word after the claim, set off by a comma, is flagged as "Therefore, Socrates is mortal" is: "In “Socrates is mortal, then.”, “then” says the box follows from something. What it follows from is the boxes below it, so the word says nothing the check can use — and in a premise it makes the claim sound established. Leave it out." So "therefore", "thus", "hence", "consequently", "accordingly", "so", "as a result", "it follows". It had been read into the predicate ("mortal then"), or as a second claim ("Socrates is mortal, consequently": "Socrates is consequently"). Not "then" of time ("He was happy then"), "I think so", "two years hence", or "If it rains, then" with its claim still to come.
* "Poe must be black" to "Poe is black": "Getting from “Poe must be black” to “Poe is black” takes a rule for “must”, and the checker has none yet: such rules belong to modal logic." It had said nothing, and "All ravens must be black" to "All ravens are black" only that the claims differ. Not the other way round: "what is, must be" takes more than a rule for "must".
* Where the check has nothing else to say, it says what the premises do give, and by what rule: "The premises give “Socrates is mortal” by universal modus ponens, but not “Socrates will die”." With one premise, the box is named: "“Poe is not not black” gives “Poe is black” by double-negation elimination, but not “Poe is not black”." Not of an objection.
* A derived claim keeps the capitals the premises give a name: "Phosphorus is identical to Hesperus", where it had been "... to hesperus", in that message and when a conclusion is derived from a box.

Tests. multiselect-r27-test.js 13 (new); hints-r27-test.js 52 (9 new); locutions-r27-test.js 78 (3 new). Take-one-out: each of the 15 edits is caught. In three fuzzer runs the check said "the premises give" 681 times, and the truth tables found that every one of those claims follows. The soundness sweep (56,436 steps) and the fuzzers find no step certified that does not follow, as before. All 84 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 13, evening: a fallacy is named (r27.98)

The user: "Add/delete/edit anything that will improve the app."

A step that is not certified had often been left without a word: of the 208 such steps in the student and fallacy corpora, 94 said nothing beyond "? not recognized", so a fallacy and a valid step the checker cannot read looked alike. Now a step in the form of a common fallacy says which, in the boxes' own words. The tag stays "? not recognized"; nothing is certified, or refused, that was not.

* "“If it rains, then the ground is wet” and “The ground is wet” do not give “It rains”: that is the fallacy of affirming the consequent." So denying the antecedent; a conditional's converse ("a conditional does not give its converse") and its inverse; affirming a disjunct ("“or” allows both"); undistributed middle ("All men are mortal" and "Socrates is mortal", to "Socrates is a man"; "All men are mortal" and "All dogs are mortal", to "All men are dogs"); illicit major ("All men are mortal" and "Fido is not a man", to "Fido is not mortal"); illicit conversion ("All men are mortal" to "All mortals are men"); the existential fallacy ("All unicorns are white" to "Some unicorns are white": "“all” does not say that there are any", or "“no” ..."); and "some" to "all" ("what holds of some need not hold of all").
* An "all" worded as a conditional is named as a conditional is: "If someone is a man, then he is mortal" and "Socrates is mortal", to "Socrates is a man", is affirming the consequent; "Being a man is sufficient for being mortal" and "Socrates is not a man", to "Socrates is not mortal", denying the antecedent.
* Never where a premise alone gives the conclusion: "Fido is wise if something is wise" and "Fido is wise" do give "Something is wise". A fuzzer found this one; it had been named affirming the consequent.
* On the map the tag's tip gives the fallacy alone, without "That is the limit of the check, not a verdict"; so does the list of steps.
* Help: "Hover over the tag (on a touch screen, press and hold it) to see what it found, such as a missing premise or a fallacy."
* Two notes name the box where they had said "this": "so the box says what “everyone who is a man is mortal” says".

Tests. hints-r27-test.js 43 (15 new). Of the 118 student fallacies, 29 are now named; none of the 258 student steps (each valid) is. Of the 1,617 steps the check named as fallacies in two fuzzer runs, the truth tables and models found every one invalid. Take-one-out: each of the 11 pieces, taken out, fails a test. All 83 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 13, later: what the check says of a step that takes two rules (r27.97)

The user: "Add/delete/edit anything that will improve the app." Then, of the first wording of these hints: "I'm sorry. I don't understand this"; "What is 'this' in 'this would follow'?"

One rule per step stays (the user's rule, r27.84): the steps below are still not certified. What changes is what the check says of them, so that a student can see how to make each one a step of one rule. Every hint now names the box it is about where it had said "this", says "give" for what follows, and takes one step at a time.

* **Two rules in one step.** "Getting to “The game is off” takes two steps. First, “If it rains, then the ground is wet” and “It rains” give “The ground is wet” by modus ponens. Then “The ground is wet” and “If the ground is wet, then the game is off” give “The game is off” by modus ponens. Add “The ground is wet” as a box of its own, with “If it rains, then the ground is wet” and “It rains” under it." The claim between is put in the map's own words and read back, and both steps are checked as written; the second may use a premise again ("Poe is black" and "Poe is a raven" give "Poe is a black raven"). Up to three premises; a few dozen claims are tried.
* **A box that holds two claims.** "“Socrates is a man, and all men are mortal” holds two claims. As boxes side by side, “Socrates is a man” and “All men are mortal” would give “Socrates is mortal” by universal modus ponens."
* **"Someone" with no person shown.** "“Socrates is a person and Socrates is wise” would give “Someone is wise” by existential introduction, but the premises do not show that ‘Socrates is a person’." So for "some raven": "Poe is a raven". Only "every" and "all" had had a hint like this.
* **"Must", read as written** (r27.96): "The premises give “Socrates is mortal” by universal modus ponens, but not “Socrates must be mortal”: the checker has no rules for “must” yet, as they belong to modal logic." With "must" in a premise: "With “If Socrates is a man, then he is mortal” for “If Socrates is a man, then he must be mortal”, the premises would give “Socrates is mortal” by modus ponens. But the checker has no rules for “must” yet ..." Nothing is said where taking "must" out would only repeat the premise ("Socrates is mortal" to "Socrates must be mortal").
* **The older hints** name the box too: "Worded the same way each time, the premises would give “The ground is wet” by modus ponens, but ...", "Spelled the same way each time, ...", "With the parts of the claims in the same order and grouping, ...", "With “would” for “should”, ...", the "-able" hint, and "“Everyone loves Mary” would give “Socrates loves Mary” once it is shown that ‘Socrates is a person’." For an objection: "the denial of “...”"; for a weak objection: "that “...” has not been established".
* **A name in -s is one** in what the check says: ‘Socrates is a person’, where it had said ‘Socrates are people’.
* **"One" for a noun.** "All men are mortal, and Socrates is one" is read with "one" as "a man"; "Fido is a dog, and Rex is one too": "a dog"; "Poe is a black raven, and Edgar is a white one": "a white raven" -- a predicate's noun had not counted. Two nouns joined ("a dog and a cat") are asked about; the clause's own subject is not taken ("the answer is one" is a number).

Tests. hints-r27-test.js 28, new, in run-all-tests; anaphora-r27-test.js 74 (5 new); the suites that quote a hint, in its new words. Of the 258 student steps, 90 are still not certified, 8 of them now with one of these hints; of the 118 student fallacies, none is certified and none gets a two-step or two-claim hint; the hints some get say what the step lacks (‘The cat is a person’, ‘Tabby is a person’). Take-one-out: each of 10 pieces, taken out, fails a test; 3 more are needed by later ones; three that changed nothing were taken out. Fuzzers: English 11,210 reads, none misread, 4,505 steps, none unsound; names and identities 3,000 steps, none unsound; crashes 4,000, none. Time: 40 failing steps checked in 227 ms (166 before); the hardest about 10 ms more a step. All 83 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 12–13: what students write (r27.96)

The user: "My students seem to be able to come up with all kinds of locutions that don't produce validities when they intuitively should. Add/delete/edit anything that will improve the app." And: "Also, I want to be sure not to accommodate things that seem valid when they actually aren't." Then, of a first version, the user's answers (October 13): no lone suppositions ("we require conditionals to be made of them typically"); "must" waits for the modal extensions ("Those kinds of inferences need to be built from an extension of the chosen logic"); "clearly" flagged; the people shortcut kept, with a note; "biconditional to conditional" a familiar step; a hint for "it rains" beside "it is raining".

Each change was tried on 258 steps students write and 118 fallacies they commit.

* **Words misread.** "We have free will" had been cut short at "will", as "so is Fido" is. "Socrates loves wisdom", "Descartes doubts everything": a name in -s that opens a box had been taken for a plural. "Every student read the book": after "every student", "read" is the past. "Every integer is even or odd" said nothing of seven: "even" was taken for the adverb. "Assuming Socrates is a man, he is mortal": "Assuming Socrates" had been one name, so "he" was "Assuming Socrates" (so "supposing", "provided", "granted", "considering").
* **Variables.** "For any x, if x is a man, then x is mortal" says what "everything that is a man is mortal" says; "for some x, x is wise" and "there is an x such that x is wise", that something is. "If x is a man, then x is mortal", with no "for any x", is asked: x may stand for anything, or name one thing.
* **People.** "Anyone who is a man", "whoever is a man" and "everyone who is a man" are every man, and the note says why: ""anyone who is a man" is read as every man: a man is a person". Only nouns said of people alone count (man, woman, philosopher, student, ...), not those that may name an animal or a thing ("a mother", "a friend", "a patient", "a king" in chess): "anyone" keeps its "person" there, and "anyone who is wise" is still a person who is wise, so "the cat is wise" gives nothing. "If someone is a man, then he is mortal" ("he", "she", "they", "that person") says what "everyone who is a man is mortal" says, and says so; it had been read as one claim.
* **Words around a claim.** "It is a fact that P", "the fact is that P" and "the truth is that P" say P; the first had been read as something said of "it". A box's label goes: "P1:", "Premise 2:", "C:", "Conclusion:", "Lemma:", "1.", "1)".
* **No lone supposition.** "Suppose that God exists" on its own is no claim: "✗ “suppose that” in a box" says what to write instead, "If God exists, then …"; so "assume that", "imagine that", "let us suppose, for the sake of argument, that". With what it leads to, a supposition makes a conditional, as "suppose that P, then Q" did: now "assume that P, then Q" and "let us suppose that P; then Q" too. A report of supposing is a claim ("we suppose that P", "Mary assumes that P"), not flagged and not P.
* **"Clearly", "obviously", "of course"** and "it is clear that" are flagged as "therefore" is: they say how sure the writer is, which the check cannot use, and in a premise they make the claim sound established. Not "clearly" of manner ("speaks clearly", "is clearly marked"), nor inside what someone thinks, nor "a matter of course". "We know that P" is a knowledge claim: read as one claim, as before, until an epistemic extension.
* **Necessary and sufficient conditions, said of nothing in particular.** "Being a man is sufficient for being mortal" (or "a sufficient condition for", "enough for"): everything that is a man is mortal. "Having free will is necessary for being responsible" (or "a necessary condition for", "required for", "needed for"): everything responsible has free will. "Being responsible requires having free will" (or "implies", "entails", "guarantees", "ensures"): the same. "Necessary and sufficient": both. Nouns that name no claim ("oxygen is necessary for fire") are still not read so.
* **Identity.** "The same as", "one and the same as", "identical with", "the same planet as" (and other kinds of thing) say an identity; "the same color as", a likeness, does not. "Hesperus is one and the same as Phosphorus" had been read as two claims joined by its "and". A pronoun is no name beside a name of two words either: "He is the same person as Samuel Clemens" is asked.
* **"Not not".** "Poe is not not black" is two denials, and double-negation elimination gives "Poe is black"; "not black" had been one word.
* **"Which means Socrates is mortal"** and "this shows Socrates is mortal": inference words without their "that", flagged as "this means that" is. Not "this means war".
* **Biconditional to conditional**, a familiar step, on, which the Logic panel can switch off: "A if and only if B" gives "if A, then B", and "if B, then A", in one step, where it had taken material equivalence and then conjunction elimination.
* **"It rains" and "it is raining"** are told apart where a step would follow but for them: "Worded the same way each time, this would follow by modus ponens, but ‘it is raining’ and ‘it rains’ differ: “is raining” in one where the other has “rains”." So for a denial, in modus tollens and disjunctive syllogism. Neither gives the other.
* **Held back for the modal extensions: "must".** Reading "Socrates must be mortal" as what follows is axiom T for a modality, a modal step: built and asked about in a first version, it was taken out before release. "Must" is read as written, as before, as are "necessarily" and "it is necessary that". The modal parts already in Standard stay until the modal extensions replace them: ab esse ad posse (in "Word meaning: “can” and “-able”"), the "can" question, and "should" read as "presumably".

Left as before: tense ("Socrates was a man"), word meanings ("dry" for "not wet", "unmarried" for "not married"), "always" and "never", "did it" with nothing named (flagged: the box names no deed), generics.

Tests. locutions-r27-test.js 75, in run-all-tests: each reading, with the fallacies it must not let through. Of the 258 student steps, 90 are not certified (113 on r27.95): most by design (inference words, tense, word meanings, lone suppositions, "clearly") or asked ("x"). Of the 118 student fallacies, none is certified. A sweep of 56,436 steps built from the new wordings and plain ones, each of the 2,880 certified checked against every model of up to three things: none certified falsely. Take-one-out: each of 29 pieces, taken out, fails a test; 6 more are needed by later ones. Fuzzers: English 11,241 reads, none misread, 4,516 steps, none unsound; names and identities 3,000 steps, none unsound; brackets 2,000, formulas 28,645 reads and 11,603 steps, five deep 15,581 and 6,031, nesting 1,500: none misread or unsound; crashes 4,000, none. All 82 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 8: Save As on a phone or tablet asks the format first (r27.95)

The user: "Also, on mobile, when choosing 'Save As', is it possible to allow selecting the type of file? It seems to default to JSON, and I'm not sure how to allow mobile users to change that."

* **Why.** Since Chrome 132, Android's Chrome has the browser save screen that Save As uses on a computer, so Save As went straight to it. On a computer that screen lists the file types; Android's names a file and a folder only, so the first type, the map's own (JSON), was all it could save. On an iPhone or iPad there is no such screen, and Save As already showed its menu of formats.
* **The fix.** On a phone or tablet (where the main pointer is a finger), Save As shows the menu of formats first: the map, PNG, JPEG, SVG, PDF or a text outline. Then the save screen opens for that one, named with its ending, and the file is written in the format chosen, whatever name the screen hands back. The map saved so stays linked to its file, as on a computer. Where the save screen is refused, the format chosen is downloaded. Save to File's first save still offers the map alone, with no menu; computers are as before.

Tests. save-r27-formats-test.js 31 (6 new, on a phone or tablet: the menu first; SVG alone, written though the screen drops the ending; the map linked; Save to File unchanged; a refused screen downloads the format chosen), 4 of them failing on r27.94. Take-one-out: each of the four pieces, taken out, fails a test. All 81 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 7, night: "implies" for a conditional in a condition; "→" twice is asked (r27.94)

The user, of r27.93: "the first formulation [W -> (X -> Y) -> Z] is ambiguous between what you gave and (W -> (X -> Y)) -> Z ... I think I had meant my corrected formulation rather than my non-well-formed formula"; and "I'm just barely able to read the 'If, if's. Maybe we should say, 'If X implies Y, then Z' rather than 'If, if', as almost no one talks like that ... 1. If W, then if X implies Y, then Z (or W implies that if X implies Y, then Z) 2. If W implies that X implies Y, then Z. I'm not sure if nesting multiples ifs in a row is grammatical. It might be. If it is, keep it, but definitely don't default to suggesting it as a disambiguation."

* **"→" twice with no brackets is asked.** "W → (X → Y) → Z" had been read as W → ((X → Y) → Z) without a word. Now each grouping is offered with its brackets, in the box's own arrows, the first "→" inside first: "(W → (X → Y)) → Z", "W → ((X → Y) → Z)". Four parts, five groupings; inside brackets and under a quantifier too. With brackets, nothing is asked.
* **"implies" twice, likewise.** "X implies Y implies Z" is asked ("if X implies Y, then Z" or "X implies that Y implies Z"); "X implies that Y implies Z" says which.
* **What the checker offers says "implies", not "if" twice.** The user's sentence, with a "then" too many: "If W implies that X implies Y, then Z" (the user's reading, first), "If W, then if X implies Y, then Z", "If W, then if X, then first Y and then Z". "If A, if B, then C": "if B implies that A, then C". Two conditions after a claim: "if it rains implies that Poe flies, then Mary sings". Derive Parent: "If it rains implies that Poe flies, then Bob dances". Two claims joined before "implies" take "both" or "either". Each wording is checked to read back as the reading it offers; where none does (a denial, a that-clause, "unless", a list of three before "implies"), it is "if it is the case that if ...", as for an "if" whose own condition is an "if". "If, if ..." and "if it is the case that if ..." are still read as before.
* **"and" and "or" before "implies"**, as before a condition's "if": "both A and B implies that C" is "if both A and B, then C" (so "either ... or", "neither ... nor"); "A, and B implies that C" keeps "implies" with B, as the user's comma does; "A and B implies that C" is asked. It had been read as "A, and B implies that C", unasked.
* **A denied conditional** is said "it is not the case that if A, then B": Derive Parent's modus tollens had found no wording for "If it rains implies that Poe flies, then Mary sings" with "Mary does not sing".
* **No condition after a claim holds a "then" with no "if" of its own**: "If it rains, then Poe flies, if Ann sings, then Bob dances" had read "Ann sings, then Bob dances" as one condition; that part is now left unread.
* **Help**: "So can both … and, either … or, implies (If A implies B, then C) and brackets."

Take-one-out: each of the 25 pieces, taken out, fails a test. ### October 7, evening: each "if" with its own "then" (r27.93)

The user: "The logic checker seems to have trouble with nested if-thens, especially weird ones in natural language, e.g. 'If W, then if X, then Y, then Z', which to me reads as W -> (X -> Y) -> Z. Can we make sure that the logic checker is able to handle complex expressions, both formal and in natural language, that involve a lot of nesting?"

* **Formulas were already right.** "→" groups to the right ("W → (X → Y) → Z" is W → ((X → Y) → Z)), brackets nest to any depth, and modus ponens and modus tollens apply at each level. A fuzzer of formulas five deep found nothing misread.
* **Each "if" with its own "then".** In English the reader took the first "then" after an "if" as its own, so a condition that is itself an "if" ("If, if A, then B, then C"; "If it is the case that if A, then B, then C"; "If if A, then B, then C") was refused ("use brackets or quotes") or misread. Now the "then" that closes an "if" is the first after which each "if" inside its condition has had its own. Such conditions read at any depth, and beside denials ("if it is not the case that if ..."), "either ... or if ...", that-clauses ("If Mary believes that if ..., then ..., then ..."), "provided that", "only if" and "unless". A "then" that says when ("and then", "by then", "since then") closes none. "If if ..." with a "then" for each is no typing slip.
* **The user's sentence has a "then" with no "if" of its own** (two "if"s, three "then"s), so it is asked about, each reading written out: "If W, then if it is the case that if X, then Y, then Z" (W → ((X → Y) → Z), the user's reading, first: the second "if" meant twice), "If it is the case that if W, then if X, then Y, then Z" ((W → (X → Y)) → Z: the first "if" meant twice), and "If W, then if X, then first Y and then Z" (the last "then" as "and then"). Two "then"s too many: the "if"s meant three times. In the second of two conditionals ("If A, then B, and if C, then D, then E"), it is asked about that one, its readings placed in the whole sentence.
* **"If A, if B, then C" is asked**, as is "If A, if B, C": the second "if" may add a condition ("if A, then if B, then C") or put one on the first ("if A if B, then C"). They had been read, one each way, unasked. "If A, and if B, C" still reads as two conditions, unasked.
* **Two conditions after a claim.** A comma before the last sets it over all before it, as the user's commas do with two clauses: "Mary sings if Poe flies, if it rains" is "if it rains, then Mary sings if Poe flies" (it had been read the other way). A comma before the first only: the first takes in the second ("Mary sings, if Poe flies if it rains"). With none, asked. "Poe flies if, if it rains, then Mary sings" and "... only if, if ..." take a condition that is an "if"; "Poe flies if it rains, and if Mary sings, then Bob dances" is two conditionals.
* **Derive Parent** says a condition that is an "if" as "it is the case that if ...": "If it is the case that if it rains, then Poe flies, then Bob dances".
* **Met on the way.** "(it is not the case that A) and B" had been read as one denial running past its bracket. "; and", "; but", a full stop before "And", and "; or" had left an "and" in the claim ("Mary sings and"). In the inference colors, a formula's stretch that goes on takes in a bracket that pairs with one inside it ("W → ((X → Y) → Z)" goes on as "(X → Y) → Z"; its "(" had been left out).
* **Help**: "So can both … and, either … or, a then for each if (If, if A, then B, then C) and brackets."

Tests. nesting-r27-test.js (new, 62 checks: the user's example; each "if" with its own "then"; steps and Derive Parent; "If A, if B"; conditions after a claim; slips, semicolons, brackets; formulas and Help; the colors), 47 of them failing on r27.92 (the other 15 guard what already worked). Changed: language-r27-audit-test.js (an ungrouped nested conditional now reads as grouped; a "then" with no "if" of its own is still asked about, and certifies nothing), logic-r27-basis-test.js (the bare challenge's offer needs no brackets), logic-r27-english-test.js ("If it rains, if it is cold, it snows" out of the two-conditions group). Take-one-out: each piece of the change, taken out, fails a test; nine pieces that failed none were dropped. Fuzzers: a new one for nesting (fuzz-nesting.js, two seeds): 3,000 random nested sentences read exactly (45% had been refused), 1,000 with an "if" left out all asked with what was meant among the readings, 3,000 steps with none unsound; English 33,587 reads, none misread, 16,520 steps, none unsound; brackets 1,500, none misread (3 before: the bracketed denial); formulas 21,474 reads and 6,234 five deep, none misread or unsound; crashes, 4,000 cases, none. All 81 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 7, later: the denial that does the contradicting (r27.92)

The user: "What about for quantifiers, e.g., modus tollens for 'If ravens are black, then some feather is black' and 'No feather is black'? Perhaps we need to highlight the negation or negative word that's doing the contradicting in such cases rather than just every instance of 'not'." (And, of an objection's "Poe is not a raven": its conclusion says the premise it attacks is false, so the "not" goes on to it and contradicts no premise -- not red, as r27.91 had it.)

* **Counted, not sought by kind.** A part contradicts another premise's part when it denies it and says more denials than it does; a word or phrase that denies counts one ("not", "didn't", "no", "never", "nothing", "it is not the case that"), and so does each denial symbol, a formula's too. r27.91 had looked for a denial made with "not", so "No feather is black" beside "some feather is black" -- the user's modus tollens -- showed no red, nor "Some ravens are not black" beside "All ravens are black".
* **Only the denial doing the contradicting.** As many of a part's denials are red as it says beyond what it denies, the outermost first: "It is not the case that Poe does not fly" beside "Poe does not fly" reddens "It is not the case that", not the "does not" both premises say. "No" in "No feather is black"; "are not" in "Some ravens are not black".

Tests. colors-r27-inference-test.js 83 (3 new: the user's modus tollens, its "No" red; a denial inside a denial, only the outer one red; "Some ravens are not black" beside "All ravens are black"), all three failing on r27.91. With the limit to the outermost taken out, or a formula's denials left uncounted, at least one fails. All 80 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 7: a denial is red where it contradicts a premise (r27.91)

The user: "Also, I think I only want the negations to be turned to red when they're contradicting a premise that lacks the negation."

* **Red only where it contradicts.** The words that deny ("not", "didn't", "no", "it is not the case that", "~", "¬") are red and bold in the inference colors only inside a part that denies what another premise of the step says without them: "The dog didn't bark" beside "If the alarm went off, then the dog barked"; "It is not raining" beside "Either it is raining or it is snowing"; "Poe is not black" beside "All ravens are black". Elsewhere they take their part's color: a denial two premises share ("Syntax by itself is not sufficient for semantics", given and in the "if"), one that goes on to the conclusion, one that denies nothing another premise says ("No ravens are white" beside "Poe is a raven"), an objection's "Poe is not a raven".
* **Help** says so: "words that deny what another premise says, red".

Tests. colors-r27-inference-test.js 80 (2 new: the user's step, its "is not" not red; "Poe is not black" beside "All ravens are black", red; six checks that had a denial red that contradicts no premise now expect it plain, and a denial inside a green part is now one that contradicts a premise). All 80 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 6, late night: a bracket keeps its part's color (r27.90)

The user, with S4 now "Computer programs are only formal (syntactic).": "It seems to be working, but if I put something in parentheses at the end, it cuts off the right parenthesis (dims it) for some reason."

* **Why.** A part is colored from its first word to its last, so the "(" between two of its words was colored and the ")" after its last word was not.
* **The fix.** A part's stretch now takes in a bracket or quotation mark that pairs with one inside it, and a pair that holds all of it: "only formal (syntactic)" keeps its ")", "(Poe is black)" both brackets, "Poe said "Fido barks" (loudly)" its quotes and brackets. The full stop after it is the sentence's, and stays as it was. Likewise for the stretch that goes on to the conclusion, underlined in colors 2.
