### October 6, night: a linked box is read as it is alone (r27.89)

The user, of a map with S4 "Computer programs are only formal", S5 "Syntax by itself is not sufficient for semantics" and S6 "If [S4], and if [S5], then computers lack semantics" under S1 "Computers lack semantics": "For whatever reason, this is coming out as not recognized. I have two ifs that are satisfied, the then part matches the conclusion, but the inference doesn't go through. Do I really have to combine the two ifs with conjunction introduction first? I also tried 'If S1 and S2, then...', and that didn't work either."

* **No: one step does it.** "Modus ponens, conditions together" (under Familiar steps, on by default) takes "if A, and if B, then C", or "if A and B, then C", with A and B given separately. The step failed for another reason: the check reads a link as its box's words in brackets, and the capital those words begin with was read as a name's, since it no longer began the sentence. "If (Computer programs are only formal), ..." was about something named Computer, not about computer programs, so it did not match S4.
* **The fix.** A capital just inside a bracket is the sentence's capital, as at the start of a box: a bracket opens a clause. A linked box is read in brackets as it is read alone, and so is a clause typed in brackets ("If (Computers think), then ..."). The words shown keep their capitals. The user's step now gives "✓ modus ponens, conditions together" -- with "and if", with "and", and with the "then" part a link to the conclusion -- and the colors match each condition to its box.

Tests. premise-links-r27-test.js 44 (2 new: the user's step in its three wordings; a clause typed in brackets), both failing on r27.88. The English fuzzers: 13,520 and 3,000 steps, none unsound; the crash fuzzer: 4,000 cases, no errors. All 80 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 6, evening: the other anaphora (r27.88)

The user, after r27.87: "If there are any other anaphors that we haven't taken care of, we should probably tackle those, too." A survey of the reader found two misreads, and a list of words that point back that it did not read.

* **Two misreads.** "Bob and Mary love each other" was read as "both Bob and Mary love other" (the "each" of "each other" taken for "Bob and Mary each ..."), which certified "Bob loves other". Between two, it is now read as each to the other: "Bob loves Mary, and Mary loves Bob". Among three or more, which pairs it says is not settled, and it is not read, as before. "Poe is black, which surprises Mary" was read as "Poe surprises Mary" -- an adjective was taken to head the relative clause -- and "Poe is black, and it surprises Mary" was read with "it" as Poe, while its note said "it" was what "Poe is black" says. A pronoun the older reader resolved by the sentence's shape is now kept only where the pronoun reader agrees.
* **A clause cut short** is read with the clause before it in its box: "Bob left, and Mary did too" as "Mary left"; "so does Mary", "neither did Mary", "Mary can too", "Fido is not", "Fido isn't", "but evil does", "Mary did so" likewise. It takes the clause before's own words where the tense fits ("Mary left", "Mary has a car"), and the helping verb otherwise ("Mary does love Ann", "Mary did not leave"). In one box, "If Mary knows all the physical facts, then she knows what red looks like, and she does not" reads "she does not know what red looks like". With nothing before it in its box, after a helping verb of another kind ("Bob can swim, and Mary does too"), or in a comparison ("taller than Mary is"), it stays cut short, and says so.
* **"If so", "if not", "thinks so".** "Poe is a raven, and if so, Poe is black" reads "if Poe is a raven"; "if not", "if it is not the case that Poe is a raven". After "Poe may be a raven", "if so" is "if Poe is a raven". "Mary thinks so" is "Mary thinks that" the clause before, or what the clause before says someone thinks ("Bob thinks that Poe is black, and Mary thinks so too").
* **What a clause says.** ", which" after a whole clause, where the word before it names no thing ("is broken", "broke"), is what the clause says: "The vase is broken, which is sad" reads "the fact that the vase is broken is sad". After a noun it may be the thing too, and is asked about, as an "it" there is ("Bob bought a car, which surprised Mary": the car, or that Bob bought it). "This" or "that" opening a clause, with a verb after it, is read the same way. With nothing before it, "This shows that God exists" stands as written.
* **"The former", "the latter"**: the first or the second of the pair the box names last ("Bob and Mary walked to the castle, and the former cried": Bob, not the castle). **"Said road", "the aforementioned road"**, "the above road": as "that road" (r27.87); "said" as a verb is untouched. **"One"**: "Bob bought a red car, and Mary bought a blue one" reads "a blue car"; "white ones" after "black ravens" are "white ravens"; where it may be either of two nouns, it is asked about.
* **Reflexives.** "Bob praises himself" and "Bob praises Bob" are one claim, and "praises himself" is one predicate in a quantifier's terms too: "If Bob praises himself, then Bob is vain" and "Bob praises Bob" give "Bob is vain" by modus ponens. Not of many ("ravens love themselves" is not "ravens love ravens"), not in a possessive ("Bob's dog"), not in a clause after "that".
* **A full stop ends a clause** for the words that point back: "Poe is black. That surprises Mary."; "Poe is a raven. If so, Poe is black."
* **The colors** find a clause cut short in the box's words: "Mary did too", read as "Mary left", goes on to the conclusion.
* **Left as they were.** A pronoun or a clause cut short whose referent is in another box: each box stands alone (the user: "The nodes are supposed to be standalone, intelligible sentences whose pronouns only refer to referent within the node"). Also "there", "then", "such", and a plain "the road" (r27.87).

Tests. anaphora-r27-test.js 69 (38 new: "each other" between two, its possessive, among three, a floating "each"; "which", "it" and "this" for what a clause says, "which" after a car, set off inside a subject or inside an "if", a "that" opening a second sentence, "this" alone; nine ways of cutting a clause short, with the box's note, in the shape of modus tollens, one after another, "lies"; what stays cut short; "if so", "if not", after "may be", after a full stop, "thinks so", "thinks so too"; "the former", "the latter", "the former president"; "said road", "the aforementioned road", "said" as a verb; "one", "ones", "one" asked, "no one"; reflexives, their limits; the colors). 29 fail on r27.87. With any one of 26 parts of the change taken out, at least one fails. logic-r27-stress-test.js: "Poe is black, and so is Fido" now gives "Fido is black" (it had been left unread), and "If Poe is black, so is Fido" with "Poe is black" gives it by modus ponens. logic-r27-basis-test.js: "if Poe is black, so is Fido" and "The vase is broken, which is sad" as above. The English fuzzers: 13,520 and 3,000 steps, none unsound; the crash fuzzer: 4,000 cases, no errors. All 80 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 6, later: "that road" points back; adjectives before one noun (r27.87)

The user: "I also need to be able to handle that-clauses that are anaphors, e.g., 'the long, dark, windy X...., and that (very) X...'. I'm not sure we have that functionality." It was not there: "that road" was a name of its own, and "the long, dark, windy road" was not read at all -- its commas made it "a phrase set off" in the subject, so the sentence was read as one claim.

* **A phrase that points back.** "That road", "that very road", "this road", "those ravens", "that same road" and "the same road" are read as the phrase they point back to in the box, by its noun -- as a pronoun is read as its referent. "The long, dark, windy road leads to the castle, and that road is dangerous" gives "the long, dark, windy road is dangerous" by conjunction elimination, and "If the long, dark, windy road leads to the castle, then that road is dangerous" gives it by modus ponens. The box says how it was read ("read with 'that road' as 'the long, dark, windy road', the one it can be in this box"). A word before the noun must be one the earlier phrase has: "that dark road" points back to it, "that short road" does not. It may be in a second sentence of the box ("The long road is long. That road is dangerous."), and where it begins one, the phrase written in has its capital.
* **The same rules as a pronoun.** Where it may be either of two ("the long road and the short road meet, and that road is dangerous"), it is asked about, where it matters. After "a road" it is one road, joined as "a teacher left and she cried" is: "A road leads to the castle and is dangerous". It may be what an "it" in the box is, too.
* **As written.** With nothing to point back to in an earlier clause of the box, "that road" stands as written: it may point at a road the box does not name. So does "that" after a word that takes a clause, where a verb follows ("we know that road is dangerous" says what is known; write "that that road" for the other), "the same road as Mary's", a phrase a relative clause says which, and "those ravens" after "ravens", which is said of a kind. A plain "the road" after "the long road" is its own phrase, as before: a box's "the road" may stand alone.
* **Adjectives before one noun.** Commas between adjectives set nothing off: "the long, dark, windy road" is the subject, where the sentence had been read as one claim ("Bob, the teacher," and "the dog, a terrier," still are phrases set off). "The long and winding road" and "the long, dark and windy road" are one road, where "the long and winding" had been asked about as two subjects, said of each or together. An "it" after "the long, dark, windy road" is offered the whole phrase (it had been "the long"), and a plain word that may be a verb after adjectives ("the windy road") is that phrase's noun.
* **The inference colors** find the part read with its referent in the words the box has: "that road is dangerous", and "she cried" read as "the teacher cried", go on to the conclusion (they had been left uncolored).

Tests. anaphora-r27-test.js 31 (new): the user's example, with "that road" and "that very road", in a conjunction, a conditional and an object, and in a second sentence; "this", "that same", "the same", a plural, someone, a modifier the phrase has, a phrase named twice; two it may be, asked; nothing before it, its own clause, after "know", "the same ... as", a kind, a modifier it lacks, a plain "the road"; after "a road", joined; adjectives parted by commas or joined by "and", a phrase set off still set off, an "it" offered the whole phrase; with an "it"; the colors. 18 fail on r27.86. With any one of 22 parts of the change taken out, at least one fails. The English fuzzers: 13,520 and 3,000 steps, none unsound; the crash fuzzer: 4,000 cases, no errors. All 80 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 6: weak objections and rebuttals lettered WO and WR (r27.86)

The user: "Using 'minus' for weak rebuttals and objections is getting a little confusing when you link either such premise." Asked for the letters: "WO and WR unless you can think of another unambiguous 1-letter pair." No unambiguous one-letter pair was found.

* **The letters.** A weak objection is WO and a weak rebuttal WR: W for weak (the Weak Objection key) before the O or R it weakens. So M1S1-O1-R1 is now M1S1WO1WR1, and a separate weak rebuttal is WR1 ("WR1 @ +400,-200" under Positions). The map's labels (Simple and Complex), String Mode, the text file and premise links all use them.
* **Old names still read.** Text written with -O and -R (r27.71 to r27.85), or with Q and P (before that), still imports, and is written back with WO and WR. A premise link written as [-O1] says WO1 on the map, as any link says its box's name now, and is brought up to date when its box is opened for editing. A link to a box that is gone keeps the name it was written with; "-O1" there is still one name, not "not O1" (r27.72).

Tests. weak-r27-attack-test.js 115 (the labels as WO and WR; 1 new: a string written with WO and WR, a separate WR1 placed by name); stringmode-r26-label-test.js 33 (old -O and -R text written back with WO and WR; 1 new: WO and WR text back byte for byte); stringmode-r27-positions-test.js 44 (a separate weak tree as WR1: its name, Positions entry, cross-reference and premise link); premise-links-r27-test.js 42 (2 new: a link written as -O1 to a weak objection still there says WO1, M1S1WO1 with complex labels, and is brought up to date; the check reads it as the box's words). With any one of the four changes taken out (the letters, the Positions line, the label splitter, the letter-to-kind table), at least one of them fails. All 79 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 5, late night: affirmations beside their denials; which "can"; ab esse ad posse; "any" anywhere (r27.85)

The user, on the same argument: "'We can't conclude that others think' was supposed to be a kind of alethic impossibility (or something like that), which is falsified by the existence of any possible world in which we do conclude that other think (in fact, it's this world)." And: "I'd like the 'We can know that X' extension that you proposed. Maybe there's a general rule that we can use instead of making sure we take care of every instance." And, of the "any" left unasked: "Build it."

* **An affirmation beside its own denial is its denial's denial: one rule for every phrasing.** "We know that P" denies "we don't know that P", "it has been shown that P" denies "it has not been shown that P", "we can conclude that P" denies "we can't conclude that P", and so on through every "not established" phrasing the reader knows: an affirmation is the sentence whose English denial ("not" after its helping verb, or "do not" before its verb) is one of them. As in r27.84, only where the denial is there too: in the step, or (new) in a step it shares a box with, so that a box reads one way in each. The skeptic's "If I know that I have hands, then I know that I am not a brain in a vat; I don't know that I am not a brain in a vat; so I don't know that I have hands" was not recognized; it is modus tollens. "We are not entitled to conclude that P" and "we are not in a position to know that P" say what "we can't conclude/know that P" says in this sense.
* **"Can't" may mean "not established" or "impossible for us".** On the second, what is actual refutes it. Which is meant is asked where it matters: where a step follows on "impossible for us" and not on "not established" (a weak objection's "we can't conclude that P" follows as before, and asks nothing). The answer rewrites the box: "we are not entitled to conclude that P", or "it is impossible for us to conclude that P" ("it is possible for us to conclude that P" for an affirmation), which the reader now reads.
* **Ab esse ad posse**, a new rule under Word meaning ("can" and "-able"), on by default: "We do conclude that P" gives "it is possible for us to conclude that P". "We do" is not itself the denial of "impossible for us": they are contraries, not contradictories (what we could do but don't makes both false), so "we can" comes from "we do" by this step of its own. The user: "'We can' is supported by 'We do'; a 1-premise step."
* **Plain "can": possible, able or allowed.** The user, of "Poe flies" and "Poe can fly": "Maybe it should. When 'can' is interpreted in an alethic manner, it follows that Poe can fly." The "can" of "possible for X" is dynamic modality: what the circumstances allow, or (ability) what X can do by choice; ab esse ad posse holds of the first, but one lucky success shows no ability (Kenny's darts player, 1976), and what is so need not be allowed. So "Poe can fly" is asked about where a step turns on it -- "Poe is able to fly", "it is possible for Poe to fly", "Poe is allowed to fly" (the user: "Possible, able, allowed") -- and ab esse ad posse applies on "possible" only. Read as ability otherwise, as before; "Poe is able to fly" says what "Poe can fly" says so read, and "it is possible for Poe to fly" is another claim.
* **"Any" anywhere in such a that-clause** is asked about where it matters, every one or at least one: as an object ("we can't conclude that Mary loves any raven"), as "anyone", "anything", "anybody", and in a belief ("if Mary believes that Poe loves any raven"), where it had been dropped. After "there is" ("there is any reason") only "at least one" makes sense, and nothing is asked.

The argument now runs: modus ponens to the middle conditional; under "We can conclude that others think", "We do conclude that others think", by ab esse ad posse once "can" there is answered "possible for us"; then modus tollens, once the middle box and C are answered the same way.

Tests. logic-r27-english-test.js 130 (18 new: the skeptic's modus tollens, "it has been shown", "we know" in a disjunctive syllogism; a marker beside a different denial still flagged; the argument as written, answered, and worded "possible for us"; "we do" not itself a denial; alone, "we can conclude that" still flagged; a weak objection asking nothing; "any" as an object, "anyone", "there is any reason", a belief's object; "Poe flies", so "Poe can fly", asked, and its three rewrites; "possible for" following; "can" in both boxes asking nothing; "possible" and "able" two claims; "is able to" one with "can"; a plural's "are"; 14 fail on r27.84). logic-r27-deductive-test.js and logic-r27-basis-test.js: ab esse ad posse among the optional rules, and Word meaning a group of two in the Logic panel (eight rule lists). The English fuzzers: 16,520 steps, none unsound; the crash fuzzer: 4,000 cases, no errors. All 79 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 5, night: "we can conclude that" beside its denial; "any" in a that-clause; "not both" (r27.84)

The user, on an argument about the Turing test -- P1: the evidence we have that the machine thinks is the same kind we have about the people we meet; P2: "If we can't conclude that any machine that passes the Turing test can think, and if [P1], then we can't conclude that others think"; P3: "We do conclude what others think"; C: "We can conclude that any machine that passes the Turing test can think" -- asked: "The structure is a kind of P, (P & Q) -> R, ~R, therefore ~Q. Do we have to conclude ~(P & Q) first, and then get ~Q later?" Two steps, yes: one rule per step stays (the user: "Two steps, as now"). With P2's evidence condition first, modus ponens on P1 gives "If we can't conclude that any machine that passes the Turing test can think, then we can't conclude that others think", and modus tollens on that and P3 gives C. But the step had failed before any rule was tried:

* **"We can conclude that X" beside "we can't conclude that ..." is a claim.** At the head of a box it is an inference word, flagged and left out of the reading, so C had been read as "any machine that passes the Turing test can think", which nothing in P2 denies. In a step that also says "we can't conclude that ..." (or "can't infer", "are unable to conclude", "it can't be concluded that"), it is now the claim it is there: that X is established, the denial of "we can't conclude that X". The user: "we require at least 2 conclusion locutions (one's affirmation and one's denial) unless we flag it as an inference word." Alone, it is flagged as before.
* **"Any" in a that-clause under a denial or a condition is asked about where it matters.** "We can't conclude that any machine ... can think" may mean every machine or at least one. It had been read as every one on its own and as some one inside P2's if-clause, so P2 and C did not match even when worded alike. The user: "Ask when it matters." It is read as "every" until a step turns on it; then the step asks, and the box can be rewritten with "every" or "some". So too "If Mary believes that any raven is black, ...", whose "any" had been dropped ("believes that raven is black").
* **"It is not the case both that A and that B"** is "not both A and B"; it had been read as about "it". And **"both" holds its own "and"**: "Both we can't conclude that P and Q" is two claims; the that-clause had taken in "and Q".

P3 as written says something else: "conclude what others think" is about what they think, not that they think, and "do" is not "can" (we may conclude what we are not entitled to). As "We can conclude that others think", the second step asks about "any" and follows by modus tollens on "every".

Tests. logic-r27-english-test.js 112 (11 new: alone, "we can conclude that" still flagged; beside its denial, a claim, and the one-step argument not recognized; its modus ponens step; its modus tollens step asking about "any", certified with "every", and with both boxes worded alike nothing asked; the chooser rewriting "any" as "every" or "some"; "it is not the case both that"; "both" holding its "and"; a belief's "any" in a condition, asked, and said plainly, modus ponens; 7 fail on r27.80, the reader unchanged since). All 79 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 5, small hours: formatting changes no reading; formulas colored as their English (r27.83)

The user: "When a literal is underlined by the user, it behaves differently. When it's bold or in italics, it's not affected. How do we stop underlining from affecting the literal? Also, I'm noticing when a negation is in front of an atomic (in its formal form) used in something like modus tollens, it doesn't become red like the negations in English do; can we make them turn red as well?" And: "the formal forms of the conditional don't change colors when they're used in the conclusion (unlike how they do in English, e.g., with a proof of a biconditional). Can we make sure their behavior is consistent with their English forms?"

* **An underlined letter is the letter.** A formula is read after its formatting is taken off, and that took off the marks of bold and italic (`*`) but not underline's `__`: "__P__ → Q" was no formula at all, so "P" and it gave no modus ponens. Underline's `__` and italic's `_` around words now go too; an `_` inside a word stays.
* **Formatting around "it" changes nothing.** "If **it rains**, then the ground is wet" and "If *it* rains, ..." asked what "it" refers to, where "If it rains, ..." does not: the word after "it" was read with its marks still on ("rains**"), so the weather's "it" went unseen. It is now read without them. Bold, italic and underline on a letter of a formula, on "it", on a name: every reading is as without them.
* **A denial in symbols is red**, in every spelling the reader takes: "¬", "~" and "∼" before a letter or a bracket, spaced or not; "!", "−" and "-" right before one. Not after a letter or a digit ("well-known", "x-1"), and not a dash with a space after it. A denial in symbols before a letter is part of the claim it denies, as "didn't" is part of "the dog didn't bark": "~Q" takes Q's color and, on light boxes, its tint behind the red "~" (the user: "Make sure that when it's in light mode that the highlight extends to the negations"). One before a bracket ("~(P & Q)") denies more than the letter after it, and stays as it was; so do the words "it is not the case that".
* **A formula in `$…$` is colored too** (the user: "Is it possible to color those as well?"). The colors had left math alone. They now read it as it is drawn: each letter and symbol is a span of its own there, so each takes its color inside it, as the same formula typed out would ("¬Q" one claim, its "¬" red); the copy of the math kept for screen readers is left alone.
* **No gaps in colored math** (asked by the user). The math spaces its symbols with margins, around an arrow and after a slanted letter, and neither a tint nor an underline covers a margin. Its letters and symbols also come in fonts of different heights. Between two symbols of one color, each margin now becomes padding of the same width after the first, so nothing moves. Each colored symbol's tint is as tall as the box's words', so a stretch of math has one even band, as typed symbols do. Colors 2's line under math is a strip of its own where the underline would be, reaching half a pixel into the next symbol's: separate underlines left a faint seam where two met. Sub- and superscripts keep their spacing. With colors off, the math is as KaTeX drew it.
* **A formula's connectives go as their English ones do.** Once a step is valid, the symbols between two parts that go on to the conclusion are part of the stretch: "P → Q" and "Q → P" for "P ↔ Q" are each one stretch, as their English is; "(P & R) → (Q ∨ S)" gives "Q ∨ S". In a chain of conditionals the arrow goes with the part that carries on: "P →" in the first, "→ R" in the second, as "If it rains" and "then the game is cancelled" do.

Tests. logic-r27-english-test.js 101 (1 new: bold, italic -- with "*" or "_" -- and underline on a letter of a formula in either premise, in modus tollens, on a weather "it" and on a name, each reading as without them; it fails on r27.82). colors-r27-inference-test.js 77 (20 new: a formula in `$…$` colored as the math draws it, the copy for screen readers not (both fail on r27.82); red denials in five spellings and none in "well-known"; "~Q" and "¬ Q" one claim, the tint behind the "~" on light boxes, not before a bracket; the arrows of a chain of conditionals; two conditionals one stretch each; "Q ∨ S"; an underlined letter; a bold "it rains"; math without gaps: the spacing between two symbols of one color as padding as wide, and only there, its margins kept and put back with colors off, painted once, each colored symbol's tint padded to the words' height, a line reaching back into typed words, sub- and superscripts left as they are, the line's strip (5 fail without the fix)). In Chrome: modus tollens with "~", a chain, a biconditional and an underlined letter, in both themes; colored math beside the same formula typed, before and after, at 100%, 204% and 300%, light and dark: one even band, no gaps, the line's joins read pixel by pixel. All 79 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 5, later past midnight: what goes on to the conclusion, one stretch (r27.82)

The user, on a fourth sketch (a color per claim): "I think the parts that go into the conclusion (including the connectives, like 'the roads freeze or the game is off') should be a single color (the same in colors 2 and colors 3, perhaps the green used in colors 2) in all modes once there's a validity. We want to make it super obvious (but not obnoxiously so) to the user where the conclusion is coming from. Until the validity has been established, they can keep separate colors." Built so, with a colors 3 that also gave every other claim its own color; on a valid step that differed from colors 2 only in a part the step does not use. Then: "Let's just take the underlined portion of the conclusion from Colors 3 and put it into Colors 2. Then remove Colors 3."

* **Once a step is valid, what goes on to the conclusion is one stretch.** The words joining its parts go with them: "the roads freeze or the game is off", "If it rains, then the ground is wet" when both of its parts go on (as in a biconditional built from two ifs), "The sky is blue and grass is green" when both sides go on, and the spaces between pieces of it. In colors 1 it is plain, its joining words no longer dimmed. In colors 2 it is green and underlined, the line 1px, 3px below the words, in the same green under a red word too. Words that deny stay red inside it, and a part the conclusion denies keeps its red dashes. A step not yet valid keeps a color for each part, and nothing is underlined.
* **No colors 3:** the swatch and Shift+C go off, colors 1, colors 2, off, as in r27.81.
* **A claim read as two, whose pieces have no words of their own, is one part.** "Poe is a raven that lives in a city" is read as "Poe is a raven and Poe lives in a city", but only the whole is written; its pieces could not be found, and the box went uncolored. A premise is now taken apart only where every piece has words of its own.
* Help says how the two go.

Tests. colors-r27-inference-test.js 57 (11 new: one stretch for commutation, two ifs that both go on, "the roads freeze or the game is off" and a denied stretch, none for a step not valid; the "raven that lives in a city" box colored; colors 2's green line on dark and light boxes, the red dashes kept, no colors 3 left; colors 1's stretch undimmed; and the cycle back to off after colors 2). In Chrome: both versions on four steps, in both themes. All 79 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test. The reader is unchanged.

### October 5, past midnight: a box's menu in order, no ellipses; colors 1 and 2, Shift+C (r27.81)

The user: "If 'Open Reference Arguments' is the same thing as clicking the badge, we should probably put it below 'Add Cross-Reference'. We can also get rid of all the ellipses on the context menu, as those don't seem to serve any purpose. If none of the left panel ellipses are abbreviating anything, we can remove those, too. And maybe move the B, I, and U right below the Status group of the context menu. Beyond these, organize the context menu in whatever way you think makes the most sense." And: "if the grouped premises are defeated where they're imported from, do the grouped premises import that defeat? ... I lean toward 'should'." And: "let's not have color mode turn on deductive. And I'd like the color mode to be assigned to a key if there's one that's intuitive. ... I'd like to use that other one, and pressing color cycles through them (off, on v1, on v2). Maybe the colored bars inside could switch to indicate the version when it's activated."

* **A box's menu, in order:** Edit Text; Add Premise; Change Type; Status, now with Evaluation Notes; Text: B, I and U right below Status, then Left, Center, Right (the "Align Text" group it replaces), then Link a Premise and Link to Web Address; Add Cross-Reference, then Reference Arguments below it (what the box's ↗ badge opens, as its tip now says); the deductive check (Check This Step or Derive Parent, Deductive Check: All Steps); Select More (touch screens), Take Out of Branch, Cut, Copy, Delete, Paste as Child, Paste as Co-Premise; ↑ Forward and ↓ Backward.

A panel now lets the click that opened it pass; a later click elsewhere closes it as before. Help says what it is for.
* **A grouped premise brings its defeat with it -- as it already did.** It is judged where it lives: refuted there, the argument it is grouped into is defeated and cannot establish its box; contested there, the argument waits. On a map: the main box argued for only by such an argument is Warranted; with an undefeated objection to one of its premises where that premise lives, it is not established.

Colors 2 is the second mockup's: what goes on to the conclusion green (#6fbf8b; on light boxes #17803a on a pale green tint), and nothing dimmed -- not the words around the parts, not a part the step does not use. Meeting colors, red denials and the dashed underline are the same in both, and so are the parts: only the look changes. On, the swatch's third bar says which, white for 1 and green for 2, and its tip says what each is.
* **The colors no longer need Deductive.** The swatch no longer turns it on, and turning Deductive off no longer turns them off: with it off they read every step and tag none.

### October 5, third follow-up: display names that Object.prototype also has (r27.80)

The user: "the collaboration code keeps display-name leases in plain objects keyed by the name a user types, so names that are Object.prototype property names misbehave".

* **What went wrong.** Signing in as "__proto__" set the lease table's prototype instead of storing a lease, so none was kept (JSON dropped it) and anyone could take the name. A name the table inherits -- "constructor", "toString", "valueOf" -- read back as Object's own function where no one held it. A merge in which one side held such a name kept the function, which JSON drops: the lease left the shared map, the two windows wrote the map back and forth (four writes in two idle rounds, over Firebase too), and each could take the other's name.
* **Now** a lease is read only as the name's own entry, and written as one (defineProperty never calls the __proto__ setter), whether it is taken, renewed or released. The tables the merge and a window's first pull build have no prototype, nor has the copy the wire serializer makes for the server, which dropped "__proto__" as well. As with the reader's word tables in r27.76, "constructor" is a name like any other.
* **The JSON is unchanged.** A map without such a name is saved and shared byte for byte as before; "__proto__" is kept as an ordinary key, and Firebase and saved files read it back.
* **Checked, not changed:** _presence, _commentDeletions, _orphanComments, the version and deletion registries, and a room's invites and members are keyed by ids the app makes (client, comment and node ids, random tokens, Firebase uids), never by anything typed.

Tests. New suite collab-r27-name-keys-test.js (68 checks: signing in as "constructor", "__proto__", "toString", "hasOwnProperty" and "valueOf"; the lease through the browser's copy, a saved file and an open elsewhere, which then refuses the name; merges with such a name on one side, the other and both; the shared JSON byte for byte; two windows over the sync engine; a window's first pull). collab-r26-firebase-test.js 126 (7 new: a room owned by "__proto__" with an editor named "constructor": both leases reach its document, the clients settle, and a second "__proto__" account is refused the name). Without this change, 33 of the new checks fail, and 5 of the Firebase ones; collab-r27-name-lease-test.js passes as it did. Every suite passes but qualified-undercut-r27-test.js, which reads zombie-verdict-diagnostic.txt, a file not in the repository; the public build's smoke test passes. The reader is unchanged.

### October 5, second follow-up: a refused name switch keeps your name (r27.79)

Found while checking r27.78's release. The user: "Do this task here: 'Keep your name when a name switch is refused'".

* **What happened.** Signing in under a new name released the old one first, and only then asked whether the new one could be taken. Refused -- the name in use, or "used here before, from another browser" declined -- you stayed signed in under the old name with its lease released, so anyone could sign in under it, and edit or delete its notes. A typed name stayed free until you signed in again; a Google one until your next edit (r27.78).
* **The fix.** The old name is released only once the switch is certain, after both refusals. A switch that goes through, and signing out, release at once as before; a Google lease's release keeps its uid.

Tests. collab-r27-name-lease-test.js 42 (14 new, for a typed name and a Google sign-in alike: a switch to a name in use refused, the old lease untouched, and no one else able to take the name; a switch to a name used before declined, the lease untouched; a switch that goes through releasing at once). Six fail on r27.78. Released between the two refusals, the declined pair fails; not released at all, the switches and sign-outs that must still release fail. Every suite passes but qualified-undercut-r27-test.js, which reads zombie-verdict-diagnostic.txt, a file not in the repository.

### October 5 follow-up: the name stays held while either device uses it (r27.78)

Found after r27.77: with one Google account signed in on two devices, the lease went with whichever device signed in last. The user: "Do this task here: 'Renew name lease from either device of one account'".

* **What happened.** A lease names one holder, and only the holder's edits renewed it. Once the phone had signed in, the laptop's edits no longer did: ten minutes after the phone's last use the name was free, with Vera still at work on the laptop, and anyone could sign in under it -- and, since a note's rights go by its author's name, edit or delete her notes. Signing out on the phone freed the name the same way, the laptop still signed in.
* **The fix.** A lease taken through Google is the account's. Each device remembers which account vouched for its name, and one signed in with that account renews the lease as it edits, whichever of the account's devices took it last; it takes the lease back after another of them signed out, since a release now keeps the account's uid. Signing out still frees the name at once; until the other device's next edit it is free, as no device can know which of the others are still signed in.
* **Left alone.** A lease someone else took -- another account, or a typed name, once the account's devices had been quiet past ten minutes -- stays theirs. A typed name's lease is refreshed and released in its old shapes, with no uid.

Tests. collab-r27-name-lease-test.js 28 (13 new: the laptop renewing the lease the phone took, on the device and in the room's copy; eleven minutes on, a different account and the name typed both still turned away; the phone renewing it in turn; a sign-out on the phone, its release naming the account, and the laptop taking the lease back, so the name is not free; a lease taken fairly left alone; a typed name's release). Every device reads one clock, moved forward to age the leases. Seven fail on r27.77. With any one of the three changes taken out (the account remembered at sign-in, the renewal by the account, the uid on a release), at least two fail; a renewal that takes any lease fails the fair one. Every suite passes but qualified-undercut-r27-test.js, which reads zombie-verdict-diagnostic.txt, a file not in the repository.

### October 5, last: one Google account on two devices (r27.77)

The user: "the display-name lease refresh in `diffAndStamp` drops the verified Google uid from the signed-in user's claim, which defeats the 'same verified person on two devices' exemption in `applySignIn`."

* **What happened.** A name is leased while it is in use: signing in takes the lease, each real edit renews it, and for ten minutes anyone else signing in under that name is turned away. A Google sign-in writes the account's uid on the lease, so the same account on another device is let in (r26). The renewal wrote the lease again without the uid, so once the first device had made one edit, the second was told the name "is in use by someone else right now", for up to ten minutes.
* **The fix.** The renewal keeps the uid. A lease taken by typing a name has none and is renewed as before, with no uid key, so its saved form is unchanged. The sync fingerprint reads only a lease's time and holder, so it is unchanged either way.
* **Still turned away** while the lease runs: a different Google account under the same name, and the name typed with no account. A Google account cannot take a typed name's lease either.

Tests. collab-r27-name-lease-test.js 15, a new suite: two devices and the sync engine, the lease reaching the second device as the shared map does. Four fail on r27.76: the renewed lease's uid, on the device and in the room's copy; the second device's sign-in; and the lease it then holds. Every suite passes but qualified-undercut-r27-test.js, which reads zombie-verdict-diagnostic.txt, a file not in the repository.

### October 5, very late: "constructor" is a word like any other (r27.76)

The user: "Because these objects inherit from Object.prototype, an all-lowercase word that names an inherited property -- 'constructor' -- returns a function."

* **What went wrong.** The reader looks words up in tables -- spellings, plurals, pronouns, denials, contractions -- and a table made the usual way also answers to the names every object has. Only one of them is a word in small letters, "constructor", and each such table answered it with Object's constructor function. "The constructor is broken" was read as "the function Object() { [native code] } is broken", and steps with it went unrecognized ("If the constructor is broken, then we stop" and "The constructor is broken" gave no modus ponens); the map asked whether "constructor" was plural ("make the verb agree"); in "The constructor is broken, and it is old", "it" had nothing to refer to; and a box beginning "Constructor" ("Constructor theory is true") stopped the check: with Deductive on, no step on the map was tagged.
* **No prototype.** Every table the reader looks words up in is now made without one, as eleven already were (the irregular pasts and plurals among them), so "constructor" finds nothing there, like any word not listed: the generated word-form tables (build-morphology-data.js writes them so, and its check holds the app to it), and eighteen in the app -- contractions, pronouns, denials, verb number, quantifier words, LaTeX symbol names and the rest. Two are lists inside a function; one, the syntax tree's "both", "either" and "neither", would have read "Poe saw the bridge and constructor kits" as two claims once the rest was fixed. A name with capitals ("toString", "valueOf") never reached a table: the reader looks words up in small letters.
* **What changes.** Only a sentence with "constructor" in it, in any form ("Constructor", "constructors", "constructored"). Read every way the checker reads a sentence, in 140 sentences -- the word as a noun, a plural and a verb, first, before a noun, joined to another, with a pronoun for it -- and in 34 arguments, it now reads as "conductor" does; before, 344 of these readings differed.

Tests. logic-r27-english-test.js 100 (5 new, in a section of their own: "The constructor is broken" read as itself; its modus ponens on the map, nothing asked; a box beginning "Constructor" beside another tree, every step tagged; ten sentences read as with "conductor"; no word table with a prototype, 34 tables found by name in the app; and in the stress corpus three sets of sentences that say one thing, six arguments and two notes). Eight fail on r27.75. With any one table's change taken out, at least one fails, but for one: the "anyone" words of a denial, where "constructor" happened to come out as itself. All 76 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test.

### October 5, night: inference colors; B, I and U on whole boxes (r27.75)

The user: "I was wondering if we could come up with a option to turn on a coloring scheme that changed the color of the text inside the nodes to reflect which parts of an inference they correspond to ... the idea is to help users tell which parts of a node are combining with other parts (especially when they contain the same phrase) to derive the conclusion above." Settled over three mockups: colored words; normal text for what goes on, the rest dimmed; a red dashed underline, thinner than the mockup's, where the conclusion denies a part ("the "NOT" makes it look like its written as a part of the text when it's not"); in the light theme a tint behind, with denying words keeping it; the first "if" and the last "then" of a chain normal; a step not recognized, "each part its own color to make it explicit that nothing's connecting"; the button a swatch beside Deductive. Then: "I also need to add the B, U, and I for Bold, Italic, and Underline to the right-click / long-press context menu", and "B, I, U should affect all of the text in a node when the context menu is opened for the entire node. Also, we should probably add those to formatting in the left panel."

* **The colors button**, a swatch of three bars after Deductive. On, it turns Deductive on; Deductive off turns it off, and every box is as it was. In the side panel Evaluations, Deductive and the swatch are one row without their key letters (still in their tips), so the panel keeps its height (the View group 255 px before and after, in Chrome); in the top toolbar it sits after Deductive.
* **Each premise's words, by their part in its step.** Where premises meet -- a part in two premises that does not reach the conclusion -- a color, one for each part that meets, one claim one color (six colors, each theme its own; in the light theme darker, with a pale tint of the color behind). What goes on to the conclusion, normal text; a part the conclusion denies, underlined in red dashes, 1 px (modus tollens' "the alarm went off", "white" in "No ravens are white", "it snows" in "It is not the case that both it rains and it snows"). The rest -- "if", "then", "are", commas, a part the step does not use -- dimmed. Words that deny ("not", "didn't", "No", "It is not the case that", ¬), red and bold, never dimmed, keeping the color and tint of the part they are in. "All ravens" whole, quantifier and all; in a chain of ifs the first "if" and the last "then" normal. A step the check does not recognize: each part its own color, one part said twice one color, nothing normal.
* **How.** The parts come from the check's own reading of each premise: its clauses (if-parts, then-part, the sides of an "and" or an "or"), and where "all", "some" or "no" is in the step, the kinds, the things and what is said of them. Two parts meet when the check reads them as one claim or as each other's denial. The words are found in the box as the check reads them -- contractions, regional spellings ("cancelled"), a pronoun for its name ("he is black"), "does sing" for "sings", formatting, a premise link as its box's words -- by matching words in order, most of them needed. A formula is left as it is. Reference arguments and steps with premises missing are not colored.
* **Painted once.** The tags are redrawn after every layout; a box keeps a note of what it was painted with and is painted again only when its words or their parts change, and then the boxes are measured once more (bold words are wider). A box open for editing is left as it was under the editor until it closes.
* **B, I and U on whole boxes.** In a box's menu (right-click, or press and hold) they format all of the box -- all of each selected box, every box of a group selected whole: on unless every word has it already, then off; pressed when every word has it; one undo step; links and formulas kept. A box kept as typed (written with Rich text off) is formatted as it shows, its marks written so they still show. Put on, the mark is the outermost one: *If **Poe** sings*, not _If_ ***_Poe_*** ... In an open box its own menu formats the words selected, pressed when they have it. Formatting (under Advanced) starts with B, I and U, a row of their own in the side panel: the words selected in an open box (pressing them takes no focus from it), else the selected boxes. Ctrl+B, Ctrl+I and Ctrl+U with boxes selected and none open do the same; with none selected the keys are left to the browser. With Rich text off, a note says formatting is not kept.
* Help says what the colors mean, and that B, I and U format whole boxes.

Tests. New suite colors-r27-inference-test.js (38 checks: the button, its row and its tie to Deductive, the boxes exactly as they were after; modus ponens and tollens, disjunctive syllogism, universal modus ponens with "All" and "No", a chain of ifs, a part not used, proof by cases' two colors, a syllogism, an objection, a denied part affirmed, steps not recognized; words that deny, in English and in symbols; a pronoun, formatting, "does sing" and a premise link found; both themes' colors, tints, the underline and the dimming; painting once, again after a render and an edit, and not while a box is open; Help). editor-r27-wysiwyg-test.js 49 (13 new: an open box's menu; a box's menu on all of a box, on and off, pressed; links and formulas kept; one undo step each; several boxes and a whole group; a box kept as typed; Rich text off; Formatting's B, I, U with and without a box open; Ctrl+I). premise-links-r27-test.js: an open box's menu lists B, I, U first. In Chrome: the colors in both themes; the side panel's height, the top toolbar's row; the swatch, B, I and U as tall as the buttons beside them. All 76 suites pass, and the public build's smoke test. The reader is unchanged.

### October 5, evening: a box is edited as it shows -- Word's way (r27.74)

The user: "I'd like a way to have rich text and premise links to convert from their syntax once completed stay converted when editing, but once you start to delete them, you merely start deleting elements of the syntax. That is, I'd like their behavior to be more like it is in Word." Chosen with the user: a link or a formula is selected by the first Backspace and deleted by the second; a formula stays rendered and opens on a double-click; Ctrl+B, Ctrl+I and Ctrl+U format; a prototype first. After trying the prototype: "Everything looks good. Build it into the app."

* **A box is edited as it shows.** Its text field is gone: a box opens with its formatting applied, each premise link and each formula one piece, at the height it had. Its words are still kept as text with marks (node.texts); the renderer decides how any text looks, and with its new `annotate` option says what each piece was written as, so the editor can write it back. Without the option its HTML is as before.
* **What is typed converts once it is finished**: when the next character is typed, a line is started, or the box is closed -- so ** is not read as * on the way, nor \alph before its a. Ctrl+Z right after puts the marks back as typed and keeps them so (a ! before them, as the renderer has long allowed).
* **Deleting what is seen.** Backspace takes the letters of formatted words one at a time, the rest keeping its formatting. Next to a link or a formula, the first press selects it and the second deletes it; Delete the same forward; typing over a selected one replaces it.
* **A formula** opens on a double-click (or Enter while it is selected) and shows as a formula again once the cursor leaves it.
* **Ctrl+B, Ctrl+I, Ctrl+U** on the selection, or on the word the cursor is in. Not every mix of bold and italic inside a word can be written in the marks (the renderer reads them with patterns: *b****la* is not read as written), so the editor writes each italic with * or _, or nests it outside bold, until the text reads back as shown -- and a mix no text can show comes out at once as the nearest that can: the editor never shows what the box will not.
* **Paste and copy.** Pasted marks convert. Copied words go to other programs as plain words ("If S2 ..."), and to another box with their marks.
* **Closing** keeps text the renderer shows exactly as the editor did (checked on every close). A box opened and closed unchanged keeps its text exactly and adds no undo step; an edit is one undo step, as before. A click, double-click or double-tap opens a box with the cursor where it landed; Ctrl+click follows a link.
* **Rich text off:** only premise links convert, as only they show in such a box.
* The rest of the app treats the editor as it did the text field: picking a premise to link (offsets into the text), Save As, collaboration (an open box stays open through a collaborator's change, its cursor kept), keyboard shortcuts (none act while a box is open), present mode, a press elsewhere closing it, dragging (a press inside an open box selects words) and the touch double-tap.
* Help's formatting line says how marks convert and how links and formulas are deleted and edited.

Tests. New suite editor-r27-wysiwyg-test.js (36 checks: the renderer with and without `annotate`; opening and closing, the cursor where a click landed; converting, waiting and Ctrl+Z; selecting and deleting a link; Backspace in bold words; Ctrl+B, Ctrl+I, Ctrl+U; new lines; a formula; paste and copy; closing exactly as shown, over 120 boxes given random formatting; Esc; the selection as offsets; a link from the menu at the cursor; Rich text off; shortcuts while a box is open). The 13 suites that drove a box's textarea drive its editor (the edit-mode suite now checks that no height is written at all). In Chrome, with a real keyboard: typing converts, Ctrl+Z keeps the marks, Backspace selects then deletes a link, Enter saves one undo step, a box keeps its height when it opens, and a double-click puts the cursor in the word clicked. All 75 suites pass, and the public build’s smoke test. The reader is unchanged.

### October 5, later: a link read as its label is one name (r27.72)

The user, on r27.71's "-O1" read as "not O1": "We need to fix that. Any suggestions on how? For what it's worth, if it's a linked premise like -O1 and someone wants to negate it, the negation would be outside of the syntax for the linked -O1."

* **One name.** The deductive check reads a premise link as its box's words, and a link to a box that is gone, or back round a loop, as the box's label. That label is now one name however it is spelled: a link to weak objection -O1 is -O1, not "not O1". A negation goes outside the link -- "It is not the case that [-O1]", or "~[-O1]" in symbols. Typed without a link, "-O1" is still "not O1".
* **How.** A WORD JOINER (U+2060), the character that says "these are one word", goes before each hyphen of the label as the check reads it. The English reader already kept "-O1" one word in a sentence; the symbolic reader read a hyphen at the start of a word as "not", and one inside a word ("M1S1-O1") as "not" between two letters. It now reads a joined name as one sentence letter, spelled as the English reader spells it, so "[-O1] -> S4" and "[-O1]" give modus ponens. The joiner is invisible where the check shows the words, and never goes into a box.
* **Derive Parent** writes such a name back as its link. From "If it rains, then [-O1]" and "It rains", the new box says [-O1] (struck through while its box is gone), where it said "-O1", which reads as "not O1": the opposite of what was derived.
* **What changes.** With a deleted weak objection's link, "If S4, then O1" and "[-O1]" were certified by modus tollens; now the step is not recognized. A complex name in a formula ("[M1S1a-O1] -> S4" with "[M1S1a-O1]") was not recognized; now it is modus ponens. Labels without a hyphen read exactly as before.

Tests. premise-links-r27-test.js 40 (10 new: the deleted box's link beside "If S4, then O1"; a negation outside the link, in words and in symbols; "-O1" typed; a simple and a complex name in a formula; which hyphens are joined; a box that is only a link to itself; the list of steps; Derive Parent's box and its step). Five of them fail on r27.71: the four behaviors that changed, and the joining itself. With any one of the six changes taken out (joining, only inside a name, the symbolic reader's three, Derive Parent), at least one fails. Every suite passes but qualified-undercut-r27-test.js, which reads zombie-verdict-diagnostic.txt, a file not in the repository.

### October 5: weak objections and rebuttals lettered -O and -R (r27.71)

The user: "I need to switch the String Mode abbreviation for weak objections and weak rebuttals from Q and P to -O and -R."

* **The letters.** *(Changed in r27.86 to WO and WR: a minus in a linked name was confusing.)* A weak objection is -O and a weak rebuttal -R: an objection and a rebuttal, weakened. The letter names the box wherever a label does -- String Mode and the text file, the map's labels (Simple and Complex), and a premise link -- so M1S1Q1P1 is now M1S1-O1-R1, and a separate weak rebuttal is -R1 ("-R1 @ +400,-200" under Positions). They were Q and P from r27.20. Neither name is shared now: Q1 and Q2 are also the Hilbert basis's quantifier axioms, and P1 is the checker's usual name for a premise ("P from P1 and P2").
* **Reading them back.** The reader takes the hyphen as part of the letter. It used to stop at a hyphen, which would have dropped any line, Positions entry, cross-reference or premise link naming a weak box; and its splitter stepped over one, which would have read M1S1-O1 as M1S1O1, an objection. Text written with Q and P still imports, and is written back with -O and -R.
* **On one line.** A label on a box no longer breaks after its hyphen.
* **Left as it was.** *(Fixed in r27.72: such a label is one name. The English reader did keep "M1S1-O1" one word, but the symbolic reader read it as "M1S1", "not" and "O1".)* The deductive check reads a premise link as its box's words, and only a link back to its own box (or round a loop), or to a box that is gone, as its label. A weak box's simple label is then read as a denial: "-O1" as "not O1", since a leading hyphen is the checker's "not" ("-P"). A complex label is one word: "M1S1-O1".

Tests. stringmode-r26-label-test.js 32 (3 new: -O beside O, byte for byte; Q and P text read and written back with -O and -R); weak-r27-attack-test.js 114 (1 new: a label on one line); stringmode-r27-positions-test.js 44 (7 new: a separate weak tree's name, Positions entry, cross-reference and premise link, written and read, byte for byte). With any one fix taken out, at least one of them fails. Every suite passes but qualified-undercut-r27-test.js, which reads zombie-verdict-diagnostic.txt, a file not in the repository; it fails without this change too.
