### September 28, later: one referent is the referent, and what a clause can be the subject of (r27.54)

* **A pronoun that can be one thing only is that thing, with no question.** The user, of "? pronoun" (r27.53): "Mustn't 'she' in the teacher sentence refer to the teacher, as that's the only available referent? Granted, someone might have meant 'she' was someone else, but they'd need to fix that themselves. The sentences are supposed to be analyzed as standalone sentences." They were right; "? pronoun" is gone. The box is read with the referent in, and a note says how ("read with “she” as “the teacher”"). Two cases need care:
  * **After "a teacher" or "someone"**, writing "the teacher" in would lose the link once the box is split: "A teacher left and the teacher cried" is two claims, and the second may be about another teacher. So the pronoun that opens the next clause is dropped instead: "A teacher left and she cried" is read as "A teacher left and cried", one claim about one teacher (whether every, some, or in general is still asked). Where the pronoun cannot be dropped ("A teacher left and Bob praised her"), the box is read as one claim.
  * **A pronoun kept for the believer's sake is not read in** ("Mary believes that she is late", Castañeda's she*), and neither is one the warrant reader already flags as naming no premises or conclusion ("its conclusion").

  "According to the teacher, she left" now says that "she" has no referent: the phrase in front says in whose view the rest holds, and names no referent (r27.50's rule for claims kept whole). An unquoted name there still counts ("In Hamlet, … she" may be Hamlet, as the user pointed out).
* **"It" is what a clause says only where a clause could be the subject.** The user: "If Poe is a raven, then it is black" should have "it" refer to Poe, "Analogously for the cat sentence. If there's a general rule for this, that would be great". There is one, and it is syntactic in the sense that matters. Whether a predicate can take a clause as its subject is a fact about the predicate:
  * "That the vase is broken surprised Mary" is English;
  * "That Poe is a raven is black" is not, nor "that the cat bit Mary bit her again".

  So an "it" before a predicate no clause can be the subject of is a thing. The same goes for an object "it" of a verb no clause can be the object of ("dropped it"). This covers colours, sizes, shapes, weights, materials and textures, bodily states, what bodies do, and what only the living do (see, meet, praise, love). A predicate not on those lists leaves the question the user chose in r27.53. A fact can surprise, show, help, give, tell, cost or be a problem, so those are left out on purpose.

  The user had suggested the first clause's shape: an adjective after "is" in the vase sentence, a noun phrase in the raven sentence. That does not settle it. "Poe is a raven, and it surprised Mary" can be about the fact. "The vase is broken and it is heavy" cannot. What decides is what is said of "it".
* **The cat sentence.** "The cat bit Mary and then it bit her again": "it" is the cat, and "her" is Mary. The user's point about "again" is right: a repetition keeps the first event's roles. The rules the user adopted give the same answer without it, though. A name is someone unless the box says otherwise, so "it" is not Mary. An object is not its own clause's subject, so "her" is not the cat. Only where the box made Mary a cat too would "again" be what decides.
* **Tests.** The basis test and the warrant test check the following:
  * a pronoun read in, including "A teacher left and cried" and the one-claim case;
  * Poe, the cat, "the book is heavy" and "dropped the vase";
  * the vase and "Poe … surprised Mary" still asked;
  * "According to the teacher", and "In Hamlet" still asked;
  * she* left alone;
  * the warrant reader's unnamed conclusion.

### September 28: nothing to ask, "it" after a clause, and typos (r27.53)

* **Where a pronoun can be nothing in its box, the step says so; it does not ask.** The user: "In 'Every teacher left and she cried', 'she' has no referent, so, there's nothing to ask rather than to inform the user of the missing referent." There are now three outcomes, from one analysis of what each open pronoun in the box may be:
  * **✗ no referent**, when there is nothing to choose. The step says why and offers nothing:
    * "every teacher" reaches no pronoun outside its own clause;
    * "The teacher is tired and he cried and she laughed": "he" and "she" are two people and the box names one, so one of them is missing a referent, as the user pointed out;
    * "The teacher is tired and the teacher praised her": "her" is not the subject of its own clause ("that would be 'herself'", in the user's words);
    * "After the storm, she left": nothing before it is someone;
    * "Mary chased it" and "The cat chased it": what comes before it is someone, or is its own clause's subject.
  * **? ambiguous**, when it may be two or more things. The note names them ("“she” may be “Mary” or “Mary’s dog”"), and the chooser offers each.
  * **? pronoun**, when it can be one thing only, but the check does not read it in as the box stands. "A teacher left and she cried" offers "A teacher left and the teacher cried"; "someone" is offered as "the person".

  An offer gives every open pronoun its referent at once. It also writes in the names the check already reads for pronouns, so each offer is the box as the check reads it, and every pronoun offer in the test corpus reads back.
* **An "it" after a whole clause is asked about** (the user's choice (a)). It may be the thing, or what the clause says: "The vase is broken and it surprised Mary" offers "… and the vase surprised Mary" and "… and the fact that the vase is broken surprised Mary". The same goes for "The book is old and it is heavy", "If the vase is broken, then it is worthless", "When the vase broke, it surprised Mary" and "The cat bit Mary and then it bit her again". Where nothing before it is a thing, the fact is the one reading ("Mary left and it surprised Bob": "? pronoun"). Not asked about:
  * an "it" that stands for nothing: weather ("it rains", "it doesn't rain", "it is cold" where nothing before it is a thing); "it is clear that …"; "it is not the case that …"; "it is not warranted to conclude that …"; "it does not follow that …";
  * an "it" a quantifier binds ("If a raven sings, then it is black");
  * "its" with a noun before it, as decided in r27.51 for the user's Mary step;
  * an object whose own clause has another subject ("The box is so heavy that Bob cannot lift it": the box).
* **Typos, fixed with a button** (the user: "if it detects double spaces or any other algorithmically detectable errors that are fixable, have it suggest the fix and allow the user to press a button to okay it"). Placed like "? grammar" (the user's choice from three mockups) and tagged **✗ typo?** (the user: "maybe keep extra spaces under an '✗ typo?' tag"). What counts as a typo:
  * two spaces or more between words, or a tab;
  * a space before a comma, semicolon, colon or stop;
  * no space after a comma or semicolon between words, or after a sentence's stop ("left.Mary");
  * a small word twice ("the the"), a comma or semicolon twice, or a stop twice.

  Quotations, `code`, formulas, link targets, an ellipsis, numbers ("1,000"), "e.g.", "had had" and symbolic boxes are left alone. The step waits until the box is fixed. The box is read as fixed meanwhile, so the chooser can say what the fixed box gives ("Poe is black. — the step follows by modus ponens") and "Fix the box" writes it in. The chooser shows the box as typed, spaces kept. The old "✗ extra space" near miss is "✗ typo?" too, and Derive Parent says "Without the typo, …". Not done: "a"/"an" (in this app "a is F" uses "a" as a name) and misspellings (no dictionary).
* **Two clauses read as things.** Both are older, and both came up in this audit:
  * "That the vase is broken surprised Mary" was split into "that the vase is broken", "that the vase is surprised" and "that the vase is Mary". A clause as the subject is now one claim, as "the fact that …" already was.
  * "The vase is broken, which is sad" gave "the vase is sad". No noun comes before "which", so it is what the clause says; the sentence is kept whole. After a noun ("Poe is a raven, which is black") it is still a relative clause.
* **The user's question: should a "that" clause that could stand alone as a sentence be read the same way everywhere?** Yes: a relative clause always has a gap, a missing subject or object that "that", "which" or "who" stands for ("the book that Mary read _"). A complement clause is whole ("clear that it's raining": "it's raining" stands alone). "Who" and "which" clauses always have the gap, so they are never complements. On the surface, though, the test cannot tell a gap from a verb used without an object: "Mary read" stands alone, yet "the book that Mary read" is a relative clause. So the check applies it through the word before "that":
  * an adjective or a participle ("clear", "known") takes only a whole clause;
  * a plain noun ("book", "raven") takes only a relative clause;
  * a content noun ("claim", "fact") takes either ("the claim that Mary made" and "the claim that Mary left"). The check keeps these whole and matches them as written.
* **Tests.** The basis test checks the three outcomes and their reasons, the offers and their read-back, (a) and what it leaves alone, the two clause readings, and the typo check with what it leaves alone. The English test asserts "✗ typo?" with its fix. A browser session opens "✗ typo?" on "Poe  is black.", and "Fix the box" gives modus ponens. Fuzzers: English 0 unsound and 0 misread (20,280 instances, 91,376 steps); three-premise 0 unsound of 3,000; crash 0 errors in 4,000; Derive Parent 0 bad of 190; the adversarial corpus passes 144 of 144; the pronoun generator 0 wrong of 261.

### September 25, late night: pronouns after any clause, the "it" of "it is clear that", and a cleanup (r27.52)

The user asked for a search for "anything else we need to add/fix/remove". What it found:

* **A "he" or "she" after any clause goes back to the one someone before it.** "The teacher is tired and she cried" read "she" as the teacher, but "The teacher left and she cried" was asked about: only a clause with "is" gave a referent. Now the subject of any earlier clause does ("the teacher", "my mother", "this student"), provided the words before the pronoun in the box give no one else. Still asked about:
  * "The teacher praised the student and she cried" (two people);
  * "Every teacher left and she cried" (a quantifier does not reach across "and");
  * "A teacher left and she cried";
  * "The storm raged and she left" (no one).

  So "The teacher left and she cried" gives "The teacher cried" by conjunction elimination, and "If the teacher left, then she cried" with "The teacher left" gives it by modus ponens. An animal counts, as it already did after "is": in "The dog barked and she ran", "she" is the dog.
* **One someone is not both a "he" and a "she".** "The teacher is tired and he cried and she laughed" had read both as the teacher. It is asked about now, as it already was with a name ("Mary left and he cried and she laughed").
* **An object pronoun is not the subject of its own clause, wherever the box names that subject.** "The teacher is tired and the teacher praised her" (and "… and she praised her") read "her" as the teacher of the first clause, and passed. English would say "herself", so it is asked about now. A pronoun subject's gender also settles the other gender: in "The teacher is tired and she told the boy that he left", "she" is the teacher, so "he" is the boy.
* **The "it" of "it is clear that" stands for what follows.** "It is clear that the cat bit Mary", "Bob left and it is obvious that Mary cried", "… it is hard to say whether …" and "… it is a fact that …" were asked about, as if the "it" were a pronoun with no referent. Worse, "clear that Mary cried" was read as a relative clause, "it is clear, and Mary cried it", which the flag hid. Now:
  * a "that" after an adjective or a participle ("clear", "known", "hard to say whether"), after a content noun ("the fact that", "the claim that"), or in "so … that" and "such … that" begins no relative clause. The words are kept whole and matched as written; "a raven that Mary loves" still is a relative clause;
  * the "it" of "it is clear / obvious / known / hard to say … that, whether, if, why …", and of "it is a fact / a pity / a mystery … that", stands for what follows. It asks for no referent, and it is no earlier thing: "The book is old and it is clear that Mary cried" says nothing of the book;
  * what follows is read as any clause is: "It is clear that she left" asks who she is, and so does "It seems that she left", which had not been checked. An "it" there that stands for nothing asks for no referent ("It seems that it rained"; "It is clear that it rained" had been asked about); "It seems that it broke" asks what broke;
  * "It is a claim that Mary made" and "It is hard to read" keep an "it" that is a thing, and ask what it is.

  "It is clear that P" is not read as P, as "It is known that P" is not: the check does not use what "clear" says.
* **A pronoun subject's predicate sees the earlier clauses**, as every other predicate has since r27.51: in "The box is heavy and it is so heavy that Bob cannot lift it", both are the box.
* **The chooser settles every pronoun at once.** "Lenore admired Poe and she praised him" offered "… Lenore praised him" and "… Poe praised him". Each still had a pronoun, so both were greyed out ("put it in your own words"). Now each offer gives every open pronoun its someone, with a "he" and a "she" as two people and an object other than its clause's subject: "… Lenore praised Poe" and "… Poe praised Lenore". Where no one in the box can be the pronoun, nothing is offered ("Poe wrote his poem and she read it": "his" is Poe, so "she" is no one). "That the cat" (from "it is clear that the cat bit Mary …") is no longer offered as a someone. Every pronoun offer in the test corpus now reads back.
* **Help** says what the pronoun rules are now: a box stands alone; a pronoun is its one name or its one someone; "? ambiguous" offers the box with each someone in place; the "it" of "it is clear that" stands for what follows.
* **Removed.** The color key's hide toggle and its CSS: the button went in r27.20, Help shows the legend always, and only a test called the toggle. Also five names nothing used: `claimInAttitude`, `targetBottomRel`, `CLAIM_PAST_OF`, `CLAIM_SAME_IN_PAST`, and a rectangle measured for the + buttons.
* **A question put to the user.** After a clause, "it" may be the fact that clause states. "The vase is broken and it surprised Mary" reads "it" as the vase, so it gives "The vase surprised Mary", though what surprised Mary is more likely that the vase is broken ("The claim is false and it shows that Mary lied" too). After a clause without "is", "it" stays as written ("The vase fell and it surprised Mary": the vase or its falling), so no step goes through it.
* **Tests.** The basis test checks the following:
  * the someone after any clause, and what stays asked;
  * "he" and "she" as two people;
  * the object pronoun and its subject named earlier;
  * the "it" of "it is clear that", what follows it, and the "it" that is a thing;
  * the "that" that begins no relative clause;
  * the offers that settle every pronoun.

  The color key test asserts that nothing hides the legend. A browser session opens the chooser on "Lenore admired Poe and she praised him", picks "… Lenore praised Poe", and the step becomes conjunction elimination. ### September 25, night: an emphatic "do", "at all", and the light theme's pill (r27.51)

* **An emphatic "do" says no more than the plain verb.** The user, of "We do conclude that she is surprised when she leaves the room": "'do' isn't fundamentally changing anything here." The reader now takes "We do conclude that P" as "we conclude that P", "Poe does sing" as "Poe sings", and "Poe did sing" as "Poe sang", in a claim kept whole too. The key had read them so already, but the words had not, so "Poe does sing" had been "spelled differently" from "Poe sings". Some uses stay as written:
  * "do" with an object, which is the verb "do" ("does the dishes", "did well", "do so");
  * a question ("Do we conclude that P?");
  * a "did" that alone says the tense, where the past is spelled as the present ("did beat") or is also a present verb ("did wind", "did bear").

  "Does quiz" is written "quizzes" (the third person now doubles a "z" after one vowel).
* **"At all" only stresses a claim.** "Mary is not at all surprised" is "Mary is not surprised"; "if she is at all surprised" is "if she is surprised" (to any degree). It stays where it says "every": "at all times", "at all costs".
* **"No longer" does not hide the one who concludes.** In "we would no longer conclude that she is surprised", the one who concludes is "we": "no", "longer", "any more", "even", "just", "ever", "again", "always" and the like now stand between a believer and the verb, as "not" and "would" do. So the "she" is read as the box's name, where it had been kept as written. What "no longer" itself says is a question put to the user (below).
* **The Unwarranted pill in the light theme.** It had kept the dark theme's pale amber, about 1.9:1 against a white box. It now takes the light theme's dark amber, #8a6100 (about 5.6:1), which its ambiguous tags use already.
* **"Would no longer" is "would not" in what a "would" conditional says would follow** (the user's choice). "No longer X" says two things: X is not so, and it was so before. Readings differ only on where "before" is: in how things actually are, or earlier in the imagined case. The check uses only the first part, which every reading shares; the note says so and commits to neither. The user asked whether this forces one disambiguation over another. It does not, since everything drawn from it holds on every reading:
  * modus ponens gives "we would not conclude X", which either reading entails;
  * modus tollens goes from "We do conclude X", which contradicts the "not X" that either reading asserts.

  The one assumption is the one the check makes for every "would" conditional since r27.46: the then-part speaks of the same time as the premise it meets. A change still to come ("we would eventually no longer conclude X", "would soon no longer ...") is not read as "not", so modus tollens is not recognized there. Elsewhere "no longer" stays as written: "We no longer conclude X" is not "We do not conclude X", since it also says that once we did (and from "we do not conclude X" nothing gives "we no longer conclude X"). The user's step as written now passes: "If …, then we would no longer conclude that she is at all surprised when she leaves the room" with "We do conclude that Mary is surprised when she leaves the room" gives modus tollens. Its second box needs the name: a "she" there names no one in its box (r27.50).
* **A gendered pronoun and a neutral one** (the user's rule, which they chose to adopt). Their rule: "when there are two pronouns and two referents, if one pronoun is gendered and the other is neutral, and if the two possible referents are such that one is gendered and the other is not, then the gendered pronoun goes to the gendered referent, and the neutral pronoun goes to the neutral referent." It rests on three constraints English applies:
  * "it" is never a person, and a personal name names a person unless the box says otherwise;
  * a subject and an object of one clause are two things unless the pronoun is reflexive;
  * in one sentence a thing keeps one pronoun, so what the box calls "it" it does not also call "she".

  So an animal the box calls "it" -- the one thing a referential "it" or "its" there can be -- competes with no name for "he" or "she", and that "it" is not asked about. "The cat bit Mary and then it bit her again": "her" is Mary. The pronoun is still asked about where the box does not say what it calls the animal ("Mary's dog barked and she ran"), or where its "it" may be two things ("Mary's dog chased the ball and she caught it").
* **A pronoun asked about offers its referents** (the user's suggestion: "incorporate the user's chosen suggested disambiguation into the node"). The reading chooser already wrote a chosen reading into its box, but a pronoun asked about had no readings. It now offers the box with each someone the pronoun may be put in its place: the names before it, and the people and animals ("Mary's dog barked and Mary ran" or "Mary's dog barked and Mary's dog ran"; "a teacher" becomes "the teacher"). Each reading is marked where it gives the step, and "Rewrite the box this way" writes it in. A reading reads back though the box keeps a question it had anyway and does not block with ("when" may say when or why).
* **A possessive opens a subject.** "Mary's dog barked", "my dog barked" and "that dog barked" had been read as one claim each: with a plain verb, a subject could begin only with "a", "an" or "the". So "Mary's dog barked and Mary ran" is now two claims, and gives "Mary ran" by conjunction elimination. "This gives you no reason" keeps "this" as its subject.
* **A pronoun in a predicate** may go back to the claim's own subject ("The teacher loves her job"; "The cat chased its tail") or to someone in an earlier clause ("The teacher left and the student praised her": the teacher). An object cannot go back to the subject of its own clause: "The teacher praised her" is asked about, since English would say "herself".
* **Tests.** The basis test checks the following:
  * the emphatic "do" and where it stays;
  * "at all" and where it stays;
  * the user's step as written, and that "no longer" alone is no denial;
  * the pronoun rule and what it leaves asked;
  * the pronoun's offered readings and their read-back;
  * possessive subjects;
  * pronouns in predicates;
  * the pill's colour.

  A browser session opens the chooser on "Mary's dog barked and she ran", picks "…and Mary ran", and the step becomes conjunction elimination.

### September 25, evening: a pronoun tracks its referent (r27.50)

* **Inside what "we" conclude, "she" is the box's name.** The user's step against S5: "If while in the room, Mary has acquired all the physical facts …, then we would not conclude that she is surprised when she leaves the room" with "We conclude that Mary is surprised when she leaves the room" was "? not recognized". The user: "Pronouns should track referent in the node so that each unambiguous use of the pronoun is linked as interchangeable with the referent." A pronoun inside what somebody believes, concludes or says had never been read as the box's name (r27.38). That rule protects the de se reading: "Mary believes that she is late" may be about Mary as she thinks of herself (Castañeda's she*). But that reading needs the believer to be the referent. Now the pronoun is kept as written only where the believer may be its referent ("Mary believes that she …", "Bob said that she …", "Mary thinks that we believe that she …"). Where the believer is "I", "we", "you" or "they", the pronoun reads as the box's one name, like any other. So both boxes say "Mary is surprised when Mary leaves the room", and the step is modus tollens.
* **A pronoun with nothing to go back to is asked about, in a claim kept whole too.** With "she" in both boxes, the step had passed, because the two were matched as written, and the second box names nobody. Its "she" is now asked about ("a pronoun has no uniquely determined referent in this premise; name its referent explicitly"). This holds inside what "we" conclude, and in a claim the reader keeps whole ("She is surprised when she leaves the room", "In the story, she is surprised"), which had never been checked.
* **A "he" or "she" goes back only to someone in its box.** The user: "The nodes are supposed to be standalone, intelligible sentences whose pronouns only refer to referent within the node." So "After the storm, she left" is asked about, since "the storm" is no one. A "he" or "she" needs exactly one of the following before it in its clause: the box's one name (read for it); "someone", "everyone", "no one" and the like; or a noun phrase whose noun is a person or an animal ("When the teacher arrives, she opens the door"; "If a student cheats, he fails"; "We believe that every mother loves her child"; "When the dog barks, he wags his tail"). A believer who may be its referent also keeps it as written. A phrase in front that says in what story, place or view the rest holds ("in the story") is no referent.
* **Another person or animal before it makes it ambiguous.** "Mary's mother left, and she cried", "Mary saw the teacher, and she smiled" and "Mary's dog barked and she ran" are no longer read as Mary: "she" may be the other person, or the dog. The user, of the dog: "Do you think that is unambiguous?" It is not. The sentence had been an r27.33 test of the possessive ending ("Mary's" names Mary), which now uses "Mary's car stalled and she ran". Two things compete with no one:
  * a noun after "is", which says what someone is ("Mary is a teacher, and she is tired" is Mary);
  * the subject of an object pronoun's own clause, which English would refer to with "herself" ("Mary left because the teacher scolded her" and "Mary left because the dog bit her" are Mary).
* **A name in quotation marks is mentioned, not used.** The user: "In 'Hamlet', Ophelia is mad, and she drowns" -- "she" could only refer to the used name "Ophelia" (in single, double or curly quotation marks). A quoted pronoun is no pronoun either ("Byron wrote 'She Walks in Beauty'"). Unquoted, "In Hamlet, …" could suggest that Ophelia is inside Hamlet, as the user pointed out, and it gives two names: its pronoun is asked about, with a note saying to put the title in quotation marks.
* **"Apparently" is no name.** "Apparently, she is late" had read "she" as "Apparently", the capitalized first word. A hedge, a frame or an empty word in front ("Apparently", "Usually", "Moreover") is no longer counted as a name.
* **Tests.** The basis test checks the following: the user's step (as written: modus tollens; with a lone "she": asked); the believer rule; "the storm"; the rival person and its exceptions; quoted titles and pronouns; and "Apparently". The fuzzers are unchanged: English 0 unsound and 0 misread of 20,280 steps, three-premise 0 of 3,000, crash 0 errors in 4,000; the adversarial corpus passes 144 of 144.

### September 25, later: nothing to rebut, and narrow mode's vertical spacing (r27.49)

* **Narrow mode, as the user specified it.** The spacing between co-premises is minimized. The tallest co-premise sets the minimal vertical distance to the children of the group. Where first cousins must share horizontal space, more weight goes to the side with more co-premises; otherwise each parent is centered above its children. Vertical spacing is minimized, and so is the horizontal spacing between siblings that are not co-premises. The minimal parent-to-child gap is 50px; each extra routing channel adds 14px.
* **A lone line no longer pushes its row down.** In the user's Mary's Room map, the objections sat 184px below the S5/S6/S7 row instead of 50px, which broke "vertical spacing is minimized". The cause was a routing rule from the r23–r25 layout work: a line that is its box's only child bends at the exact midpoint between that box and the child. O3's line runs to S6, which is 137px shorter than the implicit S7, and its stem rises 7.5px from S7's edge. So the midpoint cleared S7 only when the whole row dropped (O1 dropped with it, as one group's children share a row). The user chose (from a mockup) that the midpoint gives way. A lone line still bends at its midpoint wherever that is clear. Where a taller co-premise of its own parent stands there, it bends as near the midpoint as the clearances allow, just below that box, and the row keeps the minimal gap below the tallest co-premise. Wide mode is unchanged on this map (its objections hang straight). The routing checks R8f, R8h and R8j now assert the new rule; every collision and clearance test passes, and switching narrow ↔ wide with Depth on or off holds the gap with no routing errors.

* **A bare denial needs no answer.** The user: "A bare 'not P' doesn't need to be rejected, as it's not a valid counterargument. There's no premise for the rebuttal to attack if all the objection is is a denial of P without argument." So a rebuttal of a bare denial or a bare challenge (one that nothing argues for below its box) is marked "✗ nothing to rebut". An objection to a restatement is marked "✗ nothing to object to". Neither carries any force, and neither argues for the contention: r27.48's indirect proof through a bare denial is withdrawn. The message says what to do instead: "If these premises argue for “P”, make them a support of it." Once the bare box is argued for below it ("✓ argued below"), it has reasons to answer, and an attack on it is an ordinary step.
* **Tests.** The indirect-warrant fixture's objection now argues below its box, and that argument is itself refuted, so the rebuttal decides. A rebuttal of a bare denial is checked to prove nothing. The basis test checks the three cases (under a bare denial, a bare challenge, a restatement), and that an attack on a box argued for below it is an ordinary step.

### September 25: a challenge is an argument, the that-clause convention, the layout as it was (r27.48)

* **A challenge needs reasons.** The user: "a weak objection needs to be an argument. You can't just say, 'We can't conclude X'. On what basis? … if you still feel like asking for support should be enough, you can always do that indirectly with modus ponens." A weak objection whose one premise only says that its box has not been established ("It has not been shown that Poe is black", "We can't conclude that Poe is black", "Probably, Poe is not black") is marked "✗ bare challenge". Like a bare denial, it carries no force in the verdicts. Its message offers premises that do argue the challenge: "If we lack reasons to think that Poe is black, then we can't conclude that Poe is black" and "We lack reasons to think that Poe is black", which give it by modus ponens. The offer is checked before it is made. A conditional box is bracketed in it ("… that (if Poe is a raven, then Poe is black), then …"), and where no wording passes, none is offered. A hedge brought by a reason still challenges: "If Poe is a crow, then Poe is probably not black" with "Poe is a crow". "Possibly, Poe is not black" is still asked about ("for all we know", or "it could be so"); read as "for all we know", it is a bare challenge, and the diagnosis says so.
* **A bare step whose box is argued for below it is an argument.** Many maps argue this way: an objection box says only "Physicalism is false", and its reasons sit under it as supports. Such a step is now marked "✓ argued below", and it stands or falls with the argument under its box. The same holds for a bare challenge or a restatement. Without that argument, none of them has any force.
* **A rebuttal of a bare denial still argues for the contention.** *(Withdrawn in r27.49: a bare denial needs no answer, so such a rebuttal has nothing to rebut.)* Take "P", objected to by "not P", rebutted by "If Q, then P" and "Q". The rebuttal is an argument for P, so the contention is warranted by it, as before. The bare denial is only passed through, never credited.
* **A bare step is one however it is spelled.** "It has not been shown that God Exists" against "God exists" is a bare challenge, capitals aside: correcting the spelling would not make it an argument. Where spelling does stand between a step and its rule, the hint names only the word that differs ("'Exists' and 'exists' differ in capital letters"). It had also named the sentence-initial "God".
* **The that-clause convention, in the readings.** The user: "Yes, adopt the that-clause convention for readings." Some readings must show how far a belief, "necessarily" or an "iff" reaches. Such a reading puts the grouped clause last, introduced by "it is the case that", which takes all that follows it: to the end of the sentence, or to the "then" of its if-clause. For example:
  * "Bob believes that it is the case that Mary sings iff Ann dances" against "Ann dances iff Bob believes that Mary sings";
  * "Necessarily, it is the case that Bob sings iff Mary dances" against "Mary dances iff it is necessary that Bob sings";
  * "Bob sings iff it is the case that Mary dances iff Ann runs" against "Bob sings iff Mary dances, and Mary dances iff Ann runs";
  * "If Bob sings, then it is the case that Mary dances iff Ann runs" against "Ann runs iff it is the case that if Bob sings, then Mary dances".

  The reader reads these words the same way, so every reading can be written into the box and reads back as itself. Brackets the user writes still group. The grouping words are read as brackets round what they group, so whatever reads the words again sees the grouping too. As a result, "If Bob believes that it is the case that Mary sings iff Ann dances, then Tom walks" is no longer asked about (its if-clause had lost the grouping), and in "S knows that it is the case that p iff S believes that p, p is true, and S is justified" the "and" is part of what S knows. Similarly, in "Bob thinks that Poe is black, and Fido is white", the reading on which "Fido is white" is a claim of its own is offered as "Fido is white, and Bob thinks that Poe is black". It had been "…, and, as a claim of its own, Fido is white", which could not be written into the box. Written as "Bob thinks that it is the case that Poe is black, and Fido is white", the "and" is part of what Bob thinks.

  **Not adopted: the convention as a rule for reading every that-clause** (that a that-clause always runs to the end). Under that rule, "S knows that p iff (1) S believes that p, (2) p is true, and (3) S is justified" would silently read as "S knows that (p iff …)", though it is the standard way of stating a definition. So a that-clause the user writes before an "iff", without "it is the case that", is still asked about.
* **The layout raise is reverted.** The user: "let's revert the layout changes. A few things seem to have broken or just don't look right when switching between wide and narrow modes." Fans start below the tallest box of their parent's group again (r27.47's fourth point is withdrawn), pending a diagnosis of what the wide/narrow switch needs.
* **Tests.** The test maps' bare challenges now argue ("If E, then it has not been shown that Q" and "E"), and a weak move against one challenges its evidence, E. The hard cases' "challenge" expectations are now "bare". New checks cover the following: the bare challenge and its checked offer; "argued below"; a bare step spelled differently; the spelling hint; the grouping words read as brackets; and the attitude reading written back. The fuzzers are clean: English 0 unsound of 20,280 steps, three-premise 0 of 3,000, derive 0 bad of 190, crash 0 errors in 4,000; the adversarial corpus passes 144 of 144.

### September 24, late: generics asked where they matter, no restatements, fans under their own box (r27.47)

* **A generic is asked about only where its answer decides the step.** The user: "only mark the inferences as generic if it plays a role in the inference. The instances of modus ponens in the Mary argument didn't seem to depend on disambiguating the generics." Every box of a step is read the same way for each generic subject -- all, some, most, in general, as the question offers them -- and the step is tried on each. Where it follows on every reading, or on none, nothing is asked: "If color sensations are non-physical events, then physicalism is false" with "Color sensations are non-physical events" gives modus ponens, whatever the generic means, so long as it means the same in both. Where it follows on some readings and not others, the step waits for the answer and names the readings that give it: "Mosquitoes carry malaria" with "Bob is a mosquito" gives "Bob carries malaria" only read as "all mosquitoes"; "If a raven sings, it is black" gives universal modus ponens read as "every raven that sings is black", not read "in general". This replaces r27.46's asking every generic outright, and its exemption of modals, which is no longer needed ("a mere series of flashing lights can produce consciousness" asks nothing in the zombie map, where it means the same in both boxes).
* **A restatement is no argument.** A support whose one premise is its box ("same claim") is not certified: "✗ restates its box" -- a claim is no reason for itself. A conditional that restates ("P → P", "(P ∧ Q) → (P ∧ Q)") is still no rule instance. A premise that says its box in other words by a rule (quantifier negation, renamed bound variables) is that rule's step, as before.
* **A challenge is not an inference.** *(Superseded in r27.48: a challenge needs reasons, and one without them is a "✗ bare challenge" with no force.)* A weak objection that says its box has not been established is tagged "⚑ challenge" (it was "✓ direct challenge") and is not counted among the certified steps. It keeps its part in the verdicts: a challenge asks for support and needs none, and the map's warrant claims ("we can't conclude C from P1 and P2") rest on challenges to premises. Whether a challenge should need reasons too is the user's to decide.
* **A fan hangs just below its own box.** *(Reverted in r27.48.)* Children used to start below the tallest box of their parent's group; now each fan under a shorter box rises, with its whole branch, to the gap its own lines need below that box -- as far as nothing is in the way: every box above a box of the branch (or above the corridors of the branch's own lines) keeps that gap, and no box enters a neighbouring fan's corridor. Where another box reaches into the band its lines cross -- a taller co-premise over part of the fan -- the fan keeps its row, since the row's gap is what routes its lines clear of that box. A group of level boxes keeps its row, and depth mode keeps all rows. In the user's Mary's Room map, O1 and O2 now sit just below S5; O3 and O4 keep their row, as O4 is under S7. The routing tests' simulated page now spreads a Wide-mode group's boxes by their margins, as the page does; it had drawn them at the plain gap.

### September 24, night: bare denials, questions a box asks, "would" in a conditional, Mary's Room (r27.46)

* **A bare denial is no argument.** An objection whose one premise only states the denial of the box it objects to -- "Poe is not black" against "Poe is black" -- was certified as "direct denial". It is not an argument: nothing is given for the denial. Its tag now says "✗ bare denial", and why: give the reasons for the denial as the objection's premises. Where the denial holds only on one reading of an ambiguous box ("We can't leave" against "We should be able to leave", on the hedge reading of "should"), the diagnosis says so. In the verdicts a bare denial counts as no valid objection. "Same claim" (a support that repeats its box) and "direct challenge" (a weak objection saying its box has not been established) are unchanged, pending a decision.
* **Slips of agreement are asked, not passed over.** "Conscious mental occurrences causes physical effects E" had been read as a clause despite the slip (r27.45). A plural subject with a singular verb, or a singular one with a plural verb, now marks the box "? grammar" and offers the fixes to choose from: the verb made to agree ("Conscious mental occurrences cause ...") or the noun ("A conscious mental occurrence causes ..."); "Physical effects E is a physical effect" offers "Physical effects E are physical effects". No step with such a box is certified until it is answered. Where the words leave it open, nothing is said: a capitalized word is a name ("Socrates is mortal", "The Beatles is a band"), unless it opens the box with a common noun's ending ("Arguments is valid"); a relative "that" does not begin a clause ("Everything that flies is a bird"); verbs joined in a relative clause are verbs ("Every bird that sings or dances is happy"); a plural noun that modifies another is no subject ("Sports cars go fast"); nouns of either number are either ("sheep", "species", "the data", "the police"); and after a plural noun only a known verb in -s counts ("occurrences causes", not "arms races").
* **Generics are asked.** "Mosquitoes carry malaria" is not true of even half of all mosquitoes. A bare plural subject without "all", or "a raven" as a subject, marks the box "? generic" with the readings all (or every), some, most, and "generally, ..." -- the last read as one claim, true with exceptions. The chooser writes the answer into the box ("All mosquitoes carry malaria"), in place inside a longer box ("If all mosquitoes carry malaria, ..."). The subject may be a noun compound ("Color sensations are non-physical events") and the predicate may split ("non-physical events": not physical, and events). Not asked: what is said of a kind ("Zombies are possible", "Dodos are extinct", "Ravens exist"), and not outright under a modal ("A mere series of flashing lights can produce consciousness"), where the question is raised only if a reading would decide a step.
* **An indefinite in an if-clause is asked too.** The user's first thought was that "If a raven sings, it is black" is simply universal; asked for a reason it might not be, there are standard ones: the generic reading, true with exceptions ("If a mosquito bites you, you get malaria"); the specific reading, about one thing in particular (Fodor and Sag's "If a friend of mine from Texas had died in the fire, I would have inherited a fortune"); and the weak reading of donkey sentences ("If I have a dime in my pocket, I'll put it in the meter" -- not every dime). The question is the conditional's, in whole sentences: "Every raven that sings is black" or "Generally, if a raven sings, it is black".
* **In a conditional with "would", the "would" belongs to the "if".** The user: "It's a kind of entailment. 'If she has this property, then it follows that she won't be surprised', 'She is surprised', so, 'She doesn't have that property.'" So "If ..., then she would not be surprised" has "she is not surprised" for its consequent, and modus tollens with "She is surprised" certifies; modus ponens gives the plain claim. The if-clause is read back from the past the "would" puts it in: "if she knew everything" is the present ("if she knew everything now"), "if she had known everything" the past ("she knew everything"), "if she were rich" "she is rich"; "would have been surprised" is "was surprised". Where the past tense may mean the past, that reading is offered ("if Mary had known everything, ..."), and named in the diagnosis where it is the one that gives the step. Said alone, "She would not be surprised" keeps its "would": it is not "She is not surprised". Counterfactuals still do not chain, transpose, export or turn material. With "should" in its place ("then she should not be surprised") the step is not certified -- "should" hedges ("presumably") or says what ought to be, and what is so refutes neither -- and the diagnosis says that with "would" it would follow.
* **"When" that says when lets a "not" through.** "Mary is not surprised when she leaves the room" is the denial of "Mary is surprised when she leaves the room": a "when" that names one time is transparent to the "not" in its clause. And "She does not learn X", said without the time, denies "When E, she learns X": what is so at no time is not so then. The note that "when" may mean "if" or "whenever" stays, and on that reading the two are no denials.
* **The Mary's Room steps pass in their own words.** "If while in the room, Mary has acquired all the physical facts ..., then she would not be surprised when she leaves the room" with "Mary is surprised when she leaves the room", against S5: modus tollens. "If Mary acquires knowledge-how when she exits the room and sees a ripe red tomato, then she does not learn a new fact about the sensation of seeing red, namely its subjective character" with its antecedent, against S6: modus ponens. S5, S6, and "If, while in the room, ..., and when Mary exits the room and sees a ripe red tomato, she learns ..., then there are non-physical facts about color sensations": modus ponens with the conditions together, without an extra "if". The fixes: a verb in -s with "a" or "the" after it continues the phrase before its "and" ("... when she exits the room and sees a ripe red tomato"), and after a "when" clause it stays in that clause; a name and a verb make a clause at any length; "When P, Q" is a clause, so "if A, and when B, C, then D" has two conditions; a front frame ("while in the room,") does not reach past a later part with a frame of its own; and a pronoun in a predicate with a noun phrase before it in its clause ("the sensation of seeing red, namely its subjective character") is matched as written. A pronoun with nothing before it ("Mary eats it"; "she" in a box that names nobody) is still asked about.
* **Parentheses in the chooser (a note on r27.45).** *(Superseded in r27.48: the readings below now use the that-clause convention, not brackets.)* Readings use "both ... and", "either ... or" and "neither ... nor" as English's own brackets. Brackets remain only where English has no grouping word: around an "iff" ("Bob sings iff (Mary dances iff Ann runs)"); for how far "necessarily" reaches ("Necessarily, (Bob sings iff Mary dances)" against "(Necessarily, Bob sings) iff Mary dances"); and for how far a belief reaches ("Bob believes that (Mary sings iff Ann dances)" against "(Bob believes that Mary sings) iff Ann dances").
* **The Evaluation Overview.** "Go to" and "Delete" are gone from its entries: clicking a note (not the text entry box) shows its box on the map, as "Go to" did; a note is deleted by the trash can beside reply and edit, which replaces the X.
* **A box moved among its co-premises takes its branch.** Children with no target of their own stand on the first box; the reorder had left them at the first slot, so the co-premise moved there inherited them. Every child now goes with its box.

### September 24, evening: grouping words, conditions together, "given that", letters as shorthand (r27.45)

* **"Both … and", "either … or" and "neither … nor" group, as brackets do.** Each opens a group that holds its own "and", "or" or "nor", and runs on to a comma with a connective after it, or to the end; a group inside another's first part ends at the other's own word. So "Bob sings, and both Mary dances and Ann runs" is Bob sings and (Mary dances and Ann runs) -- it had read "both Mary dances" as a claim; "Either Bob sings or Mary dances, and Ann runs" is (A or B) and C -- it had been A or (B and C); "Neither Bob sings nor Mary dances, and Ann runs" keeps Ann out of the "neither"; "Bob sings and either Mary dances or Ann runs" and "Tom walks if both Bob sings and Mary dances" are no longer flagged, the words having settled them; "Bob loves both Mary and Ann, and Tom sings" is two claims, not one long verb phrase.
* **The chooser's readings use them.** Where two plain claims are grouped, a reading says "both A and B" or "either A or B"; a list, or a claim set apart from the rest, is numbered -- "(1) If Bob sings, then both Mary dances and Ann runs, and (2) Tom walks" -- with (i), (ii) inside a numbered list; brackets stay only where English has no grouping word (around an "iff", or the reach of "necessarily" or of a belief). A "not" in a verb phrase is offered as whole sentences, "Poe is not either black or white" and "Poe is either not black or white" (it had been two fragments, which could not be written into the box). "It is not the case that P if Q" offers "It is not the case that if Q, then P". Every reading can still be written into the box and reads back as itself.
* **Conditions together, given one by one.** "If Q and imagination is a non-physical event, then R", with "Q" and "Imagination is a non-physical event" as premises, was not certified: the reader takes "a non-physical event" as two claims (not physical; an event), so the condition had three conjuncts and the premises two. Modus ponens with the conditions together now takes a premise that is itself a conjunction as a run of the conjuncts, in its own order, as conjunction introduction already did. In another order ("small and black" for "black and small") it is still not certified.
* **Condition lists.** "If A and B and C, then D", "If A, and if B, and if C, then D" and "If A, B, and C, then D" now all certify with A, B and C given. The first and the last failed when a clause had a slip of agreement ("Conscious mental occurrences causes physical effects E"), which did not look like a clause and was glued to the one before it; a plural subject of up to four words and a verb is now a clause, "of" in the subject still making a noun phrase ("a series of lights"). The second was read as two conditionals side by side, the first ending "…, and if B": a consequent does not begin with "and", "or" or "if". Clauses in a comma list are a clause too, so "Mary dances, Ann runs, Tom walks, and Sue swims" is four claims (with four it had been one predication of Mary).
* **"Given that" is flagged, and asks.** It may give a reason, as "because" does, or a condition, as "if" does, and the words do not always say which: the box offers "Because P, Q" (which the inference-word flag then asks to split into two boxes) and "If P, then Q"; after its claim, "Q because P" and "Q if P".
* **A letter after a plural is a shorthand.** "Physical effects E are fully caused by purely physical events" had been about one thing named "physical effects E", so with "All physical effects are fully caused by purely physical events" and "Physical effects E is a physical effect" -- a premise as empty as "x = x" -- it was certified by universal modus ponens, though the universal only restates it. A capital letter after a plural noun (at the start, or after a determiner or a known adjective) is now read as a shorthand for the phrase, and the box says so: the claim is about physical effects. From the universal alone the step is flagged -- read with "all", it is the same claim. "Bob sees E", "The mind causes E" and "the students A and B" keep their letters; "the ravens" is read as before.
* **The checker's own words agree in number.** A case it asks for or supplies of a plural term reads "The ravens are ravens", not "The ravens is a raven"; and a reading's words take the box's capitals for names only ("all physical effects", not "all Physical effects", where the box began "Physical effects ...").
* **The Logic panel folds from a button that looks like one** -- the chevron beside "Logic" in the Evaluation Overview, now outlined, with a tooltip saying "Collapse the Logic panel" or "Expand the Logic panel". The panel remembers which it was.

### September 24, later: inference words flagged, numbered lists (r27.44)

* **A box with an inference word is flagged, and no step with it is certified.** A box holds one claim: its reasons are the boxes below it, what it is a reason for is the box above. So "because", "since", "therefore", "thus", "hence", "so" and their like either put a parent and a child in one node or make a premise sound established. The step's tag names the word -- "✗ “because” in a box" -- and says what to do:
  * *A reason inside the box* -- "because", "since", "seeing that", "inasmuch as", "on the grounds that", "the reason is that", "as a result of", "∵": "put “it rains” in a box of its own under “The ground is wet”".
  * *A whole inference* -- a conclusion word between two clauses ("It rains, so the ground is wet"; "It rains; therefore the ground is wet"; "P ∴ Q"): "put “It rains” in a box of its own under “the ground is wet”".
  * *A claim that says it was inferred* -- "Therefore, the soul is immortal", "The soul is therefore immortal", "It follows that ...", "In conclusion, ...", "As a result, ...", "Hence ...": what it follows from is the boxes below it, so the word says nothing the check can use, and in a premise it makes the claim sound established: leave it out. (The reader had silently dropped a leading "therefore", "so" or "it follows that", and read "in conclusion" as a word that adds nothing.)
  * Not flagged: words inside quotation marks, which are mentioned ('the argument "P because Q" is invalid'); "since" of time ("since 1990", "ever since", "since the war"); "so long as", "so that", "so is Fido", "so far", "if so"; "thus far"; "accordingly" of manner ("Bob acted accordingly"); and phrases that do not begin a clause -- "it is not warranted to conclude that P", "there is no reason to conclude that P" are weak objections, not conclusions. "Given that" keeps its own flag, which offers the reading "if".
  * Such a box gets no "✓ AXIOM" tag, and Derive Parent derives nothing from it; it says why.
* **Lists of three or more after "then" and "iff" stay flagged, and the user chooses** -- now among three readings, each written as a numbered list: the whole list ("If P, then (1) A, (2) B, and (3) C"), a list whose first item is the conditional ("(1) If P, then A, (2) B, and (3) C"), or all but the last ("(1) If P, then (A and B) and (2) C"). "S knows that p iff S believes that p, p is true, and S is justified" asks two things at once -- whether "knows that" takes in the "iff", and which list follows it -- and one choice among four readings now settles both.
* **Numbered lists.** Numbering says where a list begins: "(1) A, (2) B, and (3) C", "(i) ...", "(a) ...", "1) ...". The list is one part of the sentence at the place its numbering begins, and each item is one claim, read on its own (so "(1) S believes that p" does not take in "(2) p is true"). A list after "if" ends at its "then": "If (1) A, (2) B, and (3) C, then D". The numbering must start at 1, i or a and go on in order, each item must be a clause, and "and" and "or" may not be mixed. The chooser writes its numbered readings into the box in the same form.
* **Numbers in brackets that make no list refer to other claims**: "(1) and (2) entail (3)", "Premise (1) is false" are read as one claim, and say so. The first had been read as the claims "1" and "(2) entail (3)", and gave the second on its own.
* **Bracketed claims joined are read as a junction first** ("(S believes that p), (p is true), and (S is justified)"): the belief reader had taken the whole as one belief. And "(A), (B)" -- bracketed claims in a list -- counts as a clause, as "A, B" does.

### September 24: "iff" with its scope, brackets that group, very long boxes (r27.43)

* **"If and only if" is read with its scope.** The reader had split a sentence at its first "iff", wherever it stood, and read the rest as it came. It now finds the connective at the top of the sentence only, and where the sentence has another connective too, the words settle which takes in which or the box is flagged with each reading:
  * *Settled by the words.* "P iff Q, and Q iff R" is two biconditionals -- it had been read "P iff ((Q and Q) iff R)", which with "not Q" and "not R" certified "P". "If P iff Q, then R" has the biconditional as its condition (it had been two nonsense claims); "If and only if P, Q" is "Q if and only if P"; "(P iff Q) iff R" groups as bracketed. "P iff Q and R" keeps "Q and R" together, as "if P, then Q and R" does.
  * *Flagged, each reading offered.* A run -- "P iff Q iff R" says that each is equivalent to the next (as in mathematics), or groups them, "P iff (Q iff R)", and the two say different things; "P iff Q and R iff S" may also be two. A condition in front with the "iff" after it ("if P, then Q iff R"). "Necessarily", "probably", "according to Bob", "it has not been shown that" or somebody's belief in front ("Bob believes that P iff Q"): the whole biconditional, or its first side. An "and" or "or" after a comma ("P iff Q, and R"), as a conditional's is. An "if" or "unless" on either side ("P iff Q if R").
  * *Not flagged:* a denial in front. "It is not the case that P iff Q" may deny the whole or its first side, but not-(P iff Q) is (not-P) iff Q, so either reading says the same; it keeps its old one.
  * So "S knows that p iff S believes that p, p is true, and S is justified" is now flagged -- whether "knows that" takes in the "iff" is a question the words leave open -- and the chooser writes it "(S knows that p) iff S believes that p, p is true, and S is justified".
* **Brackets group in English as they do in symbols.** The reader found connectives in a copy of the sentence with its brackets blanked to spaces, and its searches took those spaces as part of the gap around a connective. So "if Bob sings, then (Mary dances and Ann runs)" lost its consequent and was not read; "(P or Q) and R", "either (P and Q) or R" and the chooser's own "(if P, then Q), and R" were not read; "(Bob sings) and (Mary dances)" was a claim about "(bob". Several readers also matched their connective in the raw words, so "(P) and (God exists if and only if God exists)" was cut at the "only if" inside the bracket. The blank is now a character no search takes for a space, every connective is found at the top level, and a bracketed part is one clause. A bracketed fuzzer (random "and", "or", "not", "if", "iff" over English clauses, every compound part bracketed) reads 20,000 sentences as they say, and of 20,000 steps shaped like rules and fallacies, with brackets inside, certifies 12,122 -- every rule instance -- and nothing invalid. Quoted clauses joined by connectives ("“Bob sings” or “Mary dances”") are read as the clauses joined.
* **Conditionals side by side, however many.** "If it rains, then the ground is wet, and if it snows, then the roads are icy, and if it hails, then the crops fail" had been read with "it snows then the roads" as a subject: two conditionals were taken apart, three were not.
* **"Because" and "since" anywhere make the whole sentence one claim.** "P iff Q because R" and "if P, then Q because R" had been split first, leaving only the part with "because" unread -- under a note that said the sentence was not read. "So is Fido" is still a clause cut short, not an inference. (Since r27.44 such a box is also flagged, and no step with it is certified: see above.)
* **A scope flag stays in its clause.** "Poe is not white and each raven is white" was flagged as a "not ... each" ambiguity -- the "not" of one conjunct with the "each" of the other -- and "it is not the case that each owl is white" as one too, though a sentential denial is not ambiguous. And after a relative clause, "or everything is an owl" is a claim of its own ("there is a crow that is not black or everything is an owl"), as "or Bob sings" was; "a crow that the owl saw or the bird heard" still continues the "that".
* **The dilemmas give their literal instances.** "Either the ground is wet or the ground is wet" from "it rains or it snows" and a conditional from each to "the ground is wet" is constructive dilemma (proof by cases also gives "the ground is wet"); "either it does not rain or it does not rain" is destructive dilemma where both conditionals begin "if it rains".
* **Every reading the chooser offers can be written into the box.** Its words had kept the reader's mark on a name ("There is a raven that Poe does not love" was offered with an invisible mark before "Poe"), and "(none)" after "does not love any raven"; bracketed readings of "necessarily" and "believes that" did not read back as themselves. All the flagged constructions now round-trip.
* **Very long boxes.** A box too long or involved to take apart -- more than 400 parts, or more than 600 part-readings to try -- is read as one claim, matched as written, and says so: "this box is too long for the check to take apart ... split it into boxes to check its steps". Within one sentence each part is read once (the reader had re-read the same words by one route and then another: twelve conditionals took 15,627 readings, and a run of thirty-two "iff"s filled the page's memory). A step whose boxes run past 1,500 characters gets no near-miss hints, which re-run the check under looser matching; certification is unchanged. Realistic sentences take one to forty part-readings, and a thousand-character paragraph about fifty milliseconds.

### September 22: the basis and its extensions, one rule per step, scope flagged, misreadings fixed

* **The basis is Hilbert's** (September 22). Eight axiom schemas, every universal closure of them, and one rule:

  | | schema | | schema |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | H1 | A → (B → A) | Q1 | ∀x A → A[t/x] |
  | H2 | (A → (B → C)) → ((A → B) → (A → C)) | Q2 | ∀x (A → B) → (∀x A → ∀x B) |
  | H3 | ¬¬A → A | Q3 | A → ∀x A, x not free in A |
  | E1 | t = t | E2 | t = s → (A → A′), A atomic |

  The other connectives are abbreviations, and a map writes them as themselves, so what they abbreviate is an axiom too, both ways round: "¬A" is "A → ⊥", "A ∧ B" is "¬(A → ¬B)", "A ∨ B" is "¬A → B", "A ↔ B" is "(A → B) ∧ (B → A)", "∃x A" is "¬∀x ¬A". **The one rule is modus ponens.**

  Every rule the check offers -- conjunction introduction and elimination, the dilemma, reductio, hypothetical syllogism, exportation, universal elimination, existential introduction and elimination, the identity rules -- is a step in the **Intro and elimination** group, on by default, and each is derived from the axioms: Kleene's ten axioms, `⊥ → A`, `A → A`, both ↔ rules, `A(t) → ∃x A`, and identity's symmetry, transitivity and compound substitution, 5,625 lines in all, every line checked in the app (`logic-r27-hilbert-test.js`). A supposition is discharged by the deduction theorem, which H1 and H2 give; a map still holds none.

  **Rule instances**, on by default, keep the uncurried conditionals a map is used to: "(A ∧ ¬A) → ⊥" states Contradiction's step, "((A → B) ∧ A) → B" modus ponens'. The axioms themselves are curried, as Hilbert's are -- that is what lets modus ponens be the only rule -- and a box that matches one is tagged "✓ AXIOM".
* **Pronouns** (September 23, a reported bug; r27.34 for the rest). "When Mary exits the room and sees a ripe red tomato, she learns a new fact about the sensation of seeing red" was flagged ambiguous for "she", which held back every step it appeared in. Two things changed:
  * **"he", "she", "him", "his" and "her" take the one name their own claim gives.** "Poe wrote a poem and his raven is black" is about Poe's raven; "Mary studied color and her knowledge is complete" about Mary's knowledge. Only that claim counts -- a name in another box never supplies a referent -- and the name must come first. With two names ("Mary told Alice she was late") the pronoun is left as written and flagged, as before. Where the pronoun is the object of the name's own clause it is somebody else -- English would say "herself" there -- so "Bob praised her" is left as written and flagged; across a clause boundary the two may be one, as in "Mary left because the dog bit her". "Mary's dog barked and she ran" names Mary, not "Mary's", and "it bit her again" reads "her" as the one bitten, since an adverb is not something owned. Two genders of pronoun are two people, and a claim that gives one name cannot say which of them it names: "Mary said he loves her" is left as written, while "He left and Mary arrived and she smiled" still reads "she". An object pronoun is measured from the last reading of the name, not only from the name: "Mary left and then she hurt her" leaves "her" alone, since the one who hurt is Mary already, while "Mary is a doctor and her patients trust her" reads both, a possessive being no such reading. And a pronoun may come first where the claim opens with a clause that waits for its main one -- "Before she left, Mary called", "When she exits the room, Mary learns a new fact" -- though nowhere else: "She met Mary" is still left as written.

  **Not inside what somebody believes, says or knows** (r27.38). "Mary believes that she is late" is no longer read as "Mary believes that Mary is late": the "she" may be Mary as she thinks of herself, and she can believe the one without the other -- Castañeda's "she*", Perry's shopper with the torn sack. In a report of what was said, the pronoun may not be Mary at all. So a pronoun after "believes", "thinks", "says", "hopes", "doubts" and the like, or after "knows that", "learns that", "sees that", "told Bob that", is kept as written, and the clause is matched as written, like any clause the reader keeps whole -- which also means the box is no longer flagged for it ("Bob believes that it rains" used to be). A pronoun outside such a clause still reads: "When Mary exits the room, she learns a new fact" is about Mary.

  **Why a pronoun is read at all.** Gender is never consulted, which is what makes this safe where names do not follow it: a gender-neutral name, or a name used outside its usual gender, is read exactly as any other. What the reading rests on is the node: a pronoun must find its referent among the words of its own box, there must be exactly one name to find, and the pronouns that look for it must want the same person. Where any of that fails -- no name, two names, two genders, or a place where English would have said "herself" -- nothing is read and the box is flagged as before. And the reading is never silent: the box says which name was used, so a wrong one can be seen and the name written out instead. The reading says what it did: *"she" is read as "Mary", the one name this claim gives*, so a wrong guess is visible. So "Mary sings and she dances" gives "Mary dances" by conjunction elimination, and a premise saying "she" matches a condition saying "Mary".
  * **A pronoun inside a claim the reader leaves whole no longer flags it.** Nothing looks inside such a claim -- it is matched as written -- so the pronoun settles nothing. A pronoun the reading does depend on, the subject of a predication ("She is happy") or an object in one, is still flagged. "They" is left alone throughout: the check's own wordings use it ("given A and B, they do not establish C").
* **Pairs of negations inside a rule instance** (September 23). While double-negation replacement is on, a conditional that states a rule's step up to pairs of negations states that step: "¬¬(P ∧ Q) → P" and "(P ∧ Q) → ¬¬P" are conjunction elimination's, "((A → B) ∧ ¬¬A) → B" modus ponens'. The explanation says a pair was taken out or put in. Switched off, the conditional must state the step exactly. A restatement ("P → P") is still no instance, and order is still content: "¬(P ∨ ¬P) → (P ∧ ¬P)" is a commutation beyond De Morgan's.
* **Names with capitals inside them** (r27.42). "H2O", "DNA", "McDonald" and "Au" are names on the right of "is" as elsewhere, so "Water is H2O" is an identity: with "Water is wet" it gives "H2O is wet" -- though still not "Necessarily, H2O is wet" from "Necessarily, water is wet". The note names each side as the box writes it ("H2O", "Dr. Jekyll"). A capitalized name with "the" in front ("the Evening Star") is still a description, as the rule for descriptions says.
* **Derive Parent reaches what the check certifies** (r27.41). It now takes a case spread over several premises ("every raven that sings is black", "Fido is a raven", "Fido sings" give "Fido is black" -- before, only a case in one box), the quantifier-bound "it", one alternative of a kind, and substitution of equivalents ("Bob runs if and only if Mary runs" and "Bob runs and Ann sings" give "Mary runs and Ann sings"). It still derives nothing from an ambiguous box.
* **Names of more than one word** (r27.40). "Clark Kent", "Mark Twain", "Samuel Clemens" and "John Stuart Mill" are each one name: as the subject of a verb ("Clark Kent wears glasses" was not read at all), on either side of an identity ("Clark Kent is Superman", "Superman is Clark Kent", "Mark Twain is the same person as Samuel Clemens"), in either place of a relation ("Lois loves Clark Kent" gives "Lois loves Superman"), and for a pronoun to take ("Clark Kent is tired and he is late"). So "Clark Kent is Superman" and "Clark Kent wears glasses" give "Superman wears glasses", while "Lois believes that Clark Kent is a reporter" still gives nothing of Superman. A part of a name is not the name ("Kent wears glasses" gives nothing), a common word before a name is no part of it ("Today Mary sings"), and the last word of a name keeps its letters -- "Clemens" had been read as the plural of "clemen", and in "Samuel Clemens wrote a novel" as a verb. A capitalized plural of a kind of people is still a plural ("All Greeks are mortal"). A title or an initial with a full stop is part of the name too (r27.41): "Dr. Jekyll is Mr. Hyde" and "Dr. Jekyll is a doctor" give "Mr. Hyde is a doctor"; "J. S. Mill", "St. Augustine", "Martin Luther King Jr." are each one name, and a pronoun takes the whole of one. The full stop ends no sentence there (Dr., Mr., Mrs., Ms., St., Prof., Rev., Jr., Sr. and the like, and a single capital), and it is kept in the words the checker writes back. A step that uses part of a name an identity gives whole asks about it -- *Did you mean ‘Dr. Jekyll’ where it says ‘Jekyll’? The identity is about Dr. Jekyll; if Jekyll is someone else, it says nothing about them.* -- since "Jekyll" may be another Jekyll.
* **A universal short of its case says what is missing** (r27.39). "Every raven is black" and nothing else, with "Fido is black" to show: the step explains *"Every raven is black" would give this once it is shown that ‘Fido is a raven’*. So with "Every raven is black if it sings" and "Fido is a raven" (‘Fido sings’ is missing), and with a kind whose alternatives a premise does not supply (‘Tweety is black or white’).
* **"Only Bob runs" and "even if" are flagged, with their readings** (r27.39, asked for). Each has two readings in a live dispute, and the box is now marked ambiguous and says what they are:
  * "Only Bob runs": *everything that runs is Bob*, or *Bob runs, and everything that runs is Bob* -- whether "only" asserts, presupposes or merely implicates its prejacent is Horn's question;
  * "Even if Mary runs, Bob runs" (or "Bob runs even if Mary runs"): *Bob runs, and if Mary runs, then Bob runs* -- the concessive reading most accounts give -- or *if Mary runs, then Bob runs*.

  A step that needs one of the readings says so: *Read as "Bob runs, and everything that runs is Bob", the step follows by conjunction elimination.* Until a reading is chosen the box derives nothing.

  **Choosing a reading** (r27.39, asked for). Click the step's **? AMBIGUOUS** tag: a small panel names the box and what is ambiguous in it, lists its readings written out as sentences -- the one the step needs says *the step follows by ...* -- and **Rewrite the box this way** replaces the box's words with the reading you pick, so the map says what it means; undo puts the old words back. It works for every ambiguity the checker raises, not only these two ("All ravens are not white" offers *No ravens are white* and *Not all ravens are white*). A reading the checker would not read back as itself cannot be written in; the panel says to put it in your own words. In review mode, or on a map shared read-only, the readings are shown but nothing is written. **Every step...** at the bottom opens the list of steps, which is what the tag used to do.

  To make the concessive reading writable, "Bob runs, and if Mary runs, then Bob runs" is now read as a claim and a conditional joined -- before, the conditional was taken as the end of the first claim's verb phrase.
* **"Anyone" in the object's place, and "everyone who ..."** (r27.38). "Bob loves anyone" and "Mary is a person" give "Bob loves Mary"; so do "Bob loves everyone who sings", "Mary is a person" and "Mary sings", and "Bob loves everything that sings" with "Fido sings". Before, only "every raven" and "everyone" were read there -- in the subject's place all of these already worked. "Anyone" is "everyone" only in a plain clause: after a denial ("does not love anyone") it is "someone", read as before, and in a condition ("if Bob loves anyone") it is not taken as universal. No existential import: "Bob loves anyone" does not give "Bob loves someone". And a quantifier word is no longer treated as a name when a sentence is put into symbols ("Bob loves anyone" had been read as a relation between Bob and something called "anyone"; nothing unsound followed from it, but the reading was wrong).
* **"Just in case" says so** (r27.38). "P just in case Q" is read as "P if and only if Q", its sense in philosophy, and the box now says so -- in everyday English it is a precaution ("bring an umbrella just in case it rains"), which says no such thing.
* **A description says so** (r27.37, asked for). "Ada is the winner", "Hesperus is the evening star", "Water is the liquid that fills the oceans": the box now says *"the winner" is a description, not a second name: this says what Ada is, so nothing said of Ada is carried over to it, or back*. Nothing about the checking changed -- a description never was a term here, and the identity rules never reached it -- but the silence was easy to mistake for an oversight. "Ada is the F" is covered too, where the reader keeps only the noun.

  Why it stays that way: a description is not a singular term. On Russell's reading "a = ιxF(x)" says that exactly one thing is F and a is it, so swapping one for the other is not identity's business but a definitional expansion -- and the expansion has a scope, which the words of one box do not settle. Nor is this only a matter of opaque contexts: since a description may fail to denote, even a denial divides the readings ("the king of France is not bald" is true if the quantifier goes inside the denial and false if it goes outside). A map that wants the Russellian conclusion can write the expansion out, and the basis takes it from there with ordinary identity substitution -- in a few boxes rather than one, which puts the scope decision where a reader can object to it. An ι shorthand, if it is ever wanted, belongs with the modal packages, as an expansion with an explicit scope marker rather than as a substitution rule.

  Identity locutions are untouched: "Ada is the same person as Bea" is still an identity, and "Poe is Edgar" still reads as one.
* **A pronoun the quantifier binds** (r27.36, asked for). "Every raven is black if it sings" and "If a raven sings, it is black" both say what "every raven that sings is black" says, and the checker now reads them that way: with "Fido is a raven" and "Fido sings" they give "Fido is black" by universal modus ponens. So do "everything is black if it is a raven", "no raven is black if it sings" and "every bird is happy if it sings or dances". Before, the pronoun had no referent the checker would accept and the box took part in nothing. The reading is shown: *"it" is read as what the quantifier speaks of: this says what "every raven that sings is black" says*.

  Three things it is not. The "it" of "it rains", "it is cold", "it seems that ..." stands for nothing, and those are left as conditionals -- the same list that already guarded "if something is a raven, then it is black". "Some raven is black if it sings" says something weaker than a universal, so it is not read this way; the determiner must be one that speaks of each such thing ("every", "each", "all", "any", "no", "everything", and in a condition "a", "an", "any", "something"). And a whole claim in the condition binds nothing: "if every raven sings, it is black" is left alone.
* **A restrictor that offers alternatives** (r27.36, asked for). "Every bird that is black or white is happy", with "Tweety is a bird" and "Tweety is black or white", gives "Tweety is happy" -- the relative clause's "or" is now read of an adjective as it always was of a verb ("that sings or dances"). One alternative on its own is enough (r27.39, asked for): "Every bird that is black or white is happy", "Tweety is a bird" and "Tweety is black" give "Tweety is happy" in one step. That is the same allowance the rule has always made for a list -- "every bird that sings and dances" is shown of Tweety by "Tweety sings" and "Tweety dances" in separate boxes -- made for alternatives: a part of the kind that offers alternatives is shown by any one of them. No switch is needed, since nothing about it depends on order or on anything contested. An alternative the kind does not offer shows nothing ("Tweety is red"). Both words at once leaves the grouping open ("that is black and old or white") and is not read.
* **Substitution of equivalents** (r27.36, asked for; a familiar step, on by default). A biconditional lets either side stand for the other inside another claim: "P if and only if Q" and "P and R" give "Q and R", and "R only if P" gives "R only if Q". It reaches only what the words lay open, which is what keeps Lois out of trouble: "Lois thinks that Clark is not Superman" is a claim the checker keeps whole, so nothing is put into it, and she is not made to think that Superman is not Superman. Nor is a counterfactual's supposition rewritten, where a claim with the same truth value is not the same supposition.

  For the same reason a name no longer goes into a modal predicate: "Ada might be tall", "must be tall", "could have been tall", "would be tall" keep the name they were given, as "is necessarily tall" already did. Names are rigid, so Kripke would allow the substitution; the checker refuses it because English "might" is also read of how things are said ("the teacher of Alexander might not have been the teacher of Alexander"), and because it cannot tell which reading a box intends. Say the word if you would rather have the Kripkean rule as a switch.
* **Instances up to the replacements** (r27.35, asked for). A step applies one rule, so a conditional that needs a rule *and then* a replacement states neither: "¬(P ∨ ¬P) → (P ∧ ¬P)" is De Morgan's and a commutation, and by default it needs support. Switch on **instances up to the replacements** (under *Extra rule instances*) and the replacements that are on may be taken on the way: that conditional is then De Morgan's, so is "¬(P ∧ Q) → (¬Q ∨ ¬P)", and "((P ∧ Q) ∧ R) → (R ∧ (Q ∧ P))" is commutation. The explanation says the order or grouping was changed. Only what the replacements allow: with commutation switched off the order is content again, and nothing invalid follows -- "(P ∨ Q) → (Q ∧ P)" and "¬(P ∨ Q) → (¬P ∨ ¬Q)" stay out, as does a restatement.
* **"Poe is Edgar" is an identity** (September 23). "Is" and a name, rather than "is" and a property: so identity symmetry, transitivity and substitution reach it, as they always did "a = b". The reader still builds it as a predication, so "every raven other than Fido" is still matched by "Bob is not Fido".

  Capitalized and still no name: what somebody is of a nation, a faith, a party, a school or a period. "Bob is Greek", "Bob is Bavarian", "Bob is Republican", "Bob is Martian" and "Bob is Portuguese" say what Bob is like, so none of them gives "Republican is tall" from "Bob is tall"; the endings *-ist* and *-ese* belong to no given name and are taken as well. "Poe is a raven" keeps its article and stays a predication. No list of words can be complete, so the reading says what it did: *"Edgar" is read as a name: this says Poe and Edgar are one thing, not what Poe is like*.
